Chapter 3: Istio Architecture Deep Dive

Now we go under the hood. You'll learn exactly how the data plane and control plane work together, how istiod configures Envoy proxies, how sidecar injection happens automatically, and how iptables silently redirects all your traffic through Envoy — without your Django code ever knowing.

Architecture 25% Installation 15%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 3

The ICA exam tests your understanding of Istio's internal architecture. Key exam items:

  • "Check if all proxies are synced" = istioctl proxy-status
  • "What port does Envoy listen on for inbound?" = Port 15006 (inbound), 15001 (outbound)
  • "How does traffic get to Envoy?" = iptables rules set by istio-init container
  • "What runs in istio-system?" = istiod, ingressgateway, optional addons (Kiali, Jaeger, Grafana)
  • "What are the three sub-components of istiod?" = Pilot (traffic), Citadel (certs), Galley (validation/injection)

3.1 The Two Planes: Data Plane and Control Plane

Istio's architecture is split into two distinct layers — the data plane (which does the actual work of handling traffic) and the control plane (which configures and manages the data plane). This separation is the key to Istio's power and scalability.

🔷
Data Plane

ALL the Envoy sidecar proxies running alongside your Pods. They handle actual traffic — millions of requests per second. Each sidecar: routes, encrypts (mTLS), retries, records metrics, injects tracing headers, and enforces circuit breaking.

🧠
Control Plane

istiod — a single binary that manages everything. It reads Istio CRDs (VirtualService, DestinationRule, etc.), converts them to Envoy configuration via xDS, and pushes config to all sidecars. It also issues mTLS certificates and validates YAML.

ISTIO ARCHITECTURE — MASTER DIAGRAM
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                          KUBERNETES CLUSTER                               │
│                                                                          │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │              🧠 CONTROL PLANE (istio-system namespace)              │ │
│  │                                                                    │ │
│  │   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │ │
│  │   │                        istiod                              │   │ │
│  │   │   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐     │   │ │
│  │   │   │  Pilot   │   │ Citadel  │   │     Galley       │     │   │ │
│  │   │   │ Traffic  │   │  Certs   │   │  Config Valid/   │     │   │ │
│  │   │   │ Config   │   │ + mTLS   │   │   Injection      │     │   │ │
│  │   │   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────────────┘     │   │ │
│  │   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │ │
│  │                         │  xDS API (gRPC streaming)                │ │
│  └─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
│                            │                                             │
│  ┌─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │           🔷 DATA PLANE (Envoy sidecars in every Pod)               │ │
│  │                                                                    │ │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────┐        ┌─────────────────────┐           │ │
│  │  │ Pod: jobpost-api     │        │ Pod: user-service    │           │ │
│  │  │ ┌─────────┐┌───────┐│  mTLS  │ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │           │ │
│  │  │ │ Django  ││ Envoy │◀────────▶│ Envoy │ │Django │ │           │ │
│  │  │ │ :8000   ││ proxy ││        │ │ proxy │ │ :8000 │ │           │ │
│  │  │ └─────────┘└───────┘│        │ └───────┘ └───────┘ │           │ │
│  │  └─────────────────────┘        └─────────────────────┘           │ │
│  │                                                                    │ │
│  │  ┌─────────────────────┐        ┌─────────────────────┐           │ │
│  │  │ Pod: skills-svc      │        │ Pod: auth-service    │           │ │
│  │  │ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │        │ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │           │ │
│  │  │ │ App   │ │ Envoy │ │        │ │ Envoy │ │ App   │ │           │ │
│  │  │ └───────┘ └───────┘ │        │ └───────┘ └───────┘ │           │ │
│  │  └─────────────────────┘        └─────────────────────┘           │ │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Why Two Planes?

Separating control from data means: (1) Control plane failure does NOT disrupt running traffic — sidecars use cached config. (2) Data plane scales independently — add more sidecars without touching istiod. (3) Configuration changes propagate in ~1-3 seconds via xDS streaming, no proxy restart needed.

3.2 istiod — The Control Plane Deep Dive

Before Istio 1.5, the control plane was 4 separate pods (Pilot, Citadel, Galley, Sidecar-Injector). Since 1.5, they're all merged into a single binary called istiod — simpler to operate, easier to upgrade, fewer failure modes.

🛩️
Pilot

Traffic Configuration Manager. Watches Kubernetes API for VirtualService, DestinationRule, and Gateway CRD changes. Converts them to Envoy xDS configuration (LDS, RDS, CDS, EDS). Pushes config to all sidecar proxies via gRPC streaming.

🏰
Citadel

Certificate Authority. Issues SPIFFE X.509 SVID certificates to every workload. Certificate identity: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api. Auto-rotates certs every 24 hours. Stored as Kubernetes Secrets.

📋
Galley

Config Validation + Injection. Validates Istio YAML before it reaches Pilot. Runs the Mutating Admission Webhook for sidecar injection. Rejects bad config to prevent misconfiguration from reaching the mesh.

OLD (Istio < 1.5)NEW (Istio 1.5+)
Pod: pilot (100m/256Mi)Pod: istiod (single binary)
Pod: citadel (50m/128Mi)All three merged into one
Pod: galley (50m/128Mi)Simpler: 1 deployment to manage
Pod: sidecar-injector (50m/...)Easier upgrades and scaling
Total: 4 pods to manageTotal: 1 pod (or 3 for HA)
Complex gRPC between podsIn-process calls (faster)
💡 ICA Exam Note

If the exam asks about "the component that issues mTLS certificates," the answer is Citadel (a sub-component of istiod). If asked about "traffic configuration," the answer is Pilot. Both are now part of the single istiod binary.

3.3 The Envoy Proxy — Deep Dive

Envoy is not just a proxy — it's a programmable L7 proxy written in C++ that serves as Istio's data plane. It understands HTTP/1.1, HTTP/2, gRPC, TCP, and WebSockets. istiod configures it via the xDS API.

HOW istiod CONFIGURES ENVOY — The xDS Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                 │
  │  You apply YAML:                                                │
  │  kubectl apply -f virtual-service.yaml                          │
  │         │                                                       │
  │         ▼                                                       │
  │  Kubernetes API Server stores it in etcd                        │
  │         │                                                       │
  │         ▼                                                       │
  │  istiod watches for CRD changes (via K8s informers)             │
  │         │                                                       │
  │         ▼                                                       │
  │  istiod converts VirtualService → Envoy xDS config              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  LDS (Listener Discovery)  →  which ports to listen on  │   │
  │  │  RDS (Route Discovery)     →  how to route requests     │   │
  │  │  CDS (Cluster Discovery)   →  which upstream services   │   │
  │  │  EDS (Endpoint Discovery)  →  which pod IPs are behind  │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │         │                                                       │
  │         ▼  gRPC streaming (persistent connection)               │
  │  istiod PUSHES config to every affected Envoy proxy             │
  │         │                                                       │
  │         ▼                                                       │
  │  Envoy proxies update routing tables IN-MEMORY                  │
  │  ← No restart needed. LIVE config update! →                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⏱️  Total time: kubectl apply → traffic routing = ~1-3 seconds │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 The 4 xDS Discovery Services

LDS (Listener) — which ports to listen on. RDS (Route) — how to match and route requests. CDS (Cluster) — which upstream services exist. EDS (Endpoint) — which specific pod IPs are behind each service. Together, they form the complete configuration that makes Envoy a "programmable" proxy.

How xDS Actually Works — The Full Picture

Each xDS service handles a different layer of Envoy's configuration, and they build on each other from the bottom up. Here's the complete flow when you apply a VirtualService:

1
You Apply YAML

kubectl apply -f virtual-service.yaml
API server stores it in etcd

2
istiod Watches

istiod uses K8s informers to watch
for CRD changes. No polling — push-based.

3
Pilot Translates

VirtualService YAML → Envoy route config
DestinationRule → cluster config + endpoints

4
xDS Pushes

RDS: "Route /jobs/* to subset v1"
CDS: "Subset v1 = pods with version:v1"
EDS: "v1 pods at 10.244.1.5, 10.244.2.8"

5
Envoy Updates

In-memory routing table updated.
No restart. Traffic flows per new rules.
Total: ~1-3 seconds.

💡 Why xDS Matters on the ICA Exam

When you run istioctl pc route deploy/X -n Y, you're viewing the RDS configuration that istiod pushed to that specific Envoy. When you run istioctl pc endpoint deploy/X -n Y, you're viewing EDS config. If a VirtualService rule doesn't appear in proxy-config route, it means istiod either didn't process it (validation failed) or couldn't push it (proxy not SYNCED). This is how you debug routing issues on the ICA exam.

3.4 Automatic Sidecar Injection — How It Works

One of Istio's most powerful features is automatic sidecar injection — Envoy is added to your Pods without you changing a single line of your Deployment YAML. This happens via a Kubernetes Mutating Admission Webhook.

SIDECAR INJECTION FLOW — From kubectl apply to Running Pod
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  ① kubectl apply -f jobpost-deployment.yaml                     │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ② Kubernetes API Server                                         │
  │     "Before I create this Pod, I must call all registered        │
  │      Admission Webhooks..."                                      │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ③ Istio Mutating Admission Webhook (runs in istiod)             │
  │     "I see istio-injection=enabled on namespace: anihpj"         │
  │     "Let me MODIFY the Pod spec before it's created..."          │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ④ Modified Pod spec returned to API Server:                     │
  │     + initContainers: [istio-init]    ← sets up iptables        │
  │     + containers:     [istio-proxy]   ← Envoy sidecar added     │
  │     + volumes:        [istio-envoy, istio-certs, ...]           │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ⑤ Pod is CREATED with sidecar ALREADY inside it                │
  │     Your original jobpost container: COMPLETELY UNCHANGED ✅     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ENABLE INJECTION:                                               │
  │    Namespace-wide: kubectl label ns anihpj istio-injection=enabled│
  │    Per-Deployment:  annotation: sidecar.istio.io/inject: "true"  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

3.5 iptables — How Traffic Is Intercepted

The istio-init container runs before your app starts. It sets iptables rules that silently redirect ALL traffic through the Envoy sidecar. Your Django code thinks it's talking directly to user-service:80 — but in reality, every packet is intercepted and routed through Envoy first.

🔴 Outbound — jobpost-api calling user-service

1
Django

requests.get("user-service/api/users/")

2
DNS

user-service → ClusterIP 10.96.45.23:80

3
Linux Stack

Packet dest: 10.96.45.23:80

4
iptables

OUTPUT chain → REDIRECT to 127.0.0.1:15001

5
Envoy In

Receives on port 15001 (outbound listener)

6
Envoy Route

Who is 10.96.45.23? → user-service

7
Policies

Apply routing, timeout, retry, circuit breaker

8
mTLS

Handshake → present SPIFFE certificate

9
Send

Encrypted packet to user-service Pod IP:15006

🟢 Inbound — user-service receiving

1
Packet Arrives

user-service Pod on port 15006

2
iptables

PREROUTING → REDIRECT to 127.0.0.1:15006

3
Envoy In

Receives on port 15006 (inbound listener)

4
Verify mTLS

Check SPIFFE cert from jobpost-api Envoy

5
Authz Check

AuthorizationPolicy — is jobpost-api allowed?

6
Record

Metrics: latency timer starts

7
Forward

127.0.0.1:8000 → Django on localhost

8
Django

Normal HTTP — NO TLS needed!

💡 KEY INSIGHT

Django NEVER sees TLS. Envoy handles ALL encryption between pods. Django talks plain HTTP on localhost:8000. From Django's perspective, it receives a normal HTTP GET request — no certificates, no encryption, no special headers. Your application code changes: ZERO. This is the magic of transparent sidecar proxying.

bash — iptables rules set by istio-init 

# Redirect ALL outbound TCP traffic to Envoy port 15001
iptables -t nat -A OUTPUT -p tcp \
  ! --dport 15001 \                    # skip Envoy's own traffic
  -m owner ! --uid-owner 1337 \        # skip Envoy process (UID 1337)
  -j REDIRECT --to-ports 15001

# Redirect ALL inbound TCP traffic to Envoy port 15006
iptables -t nat -A PREROUTING -p tcp \
  -j REDIRECT --to-ports 15006

# Exception: Envoy's own traffic is NOT redirected (avoid loop)
iptables -t nat -A OUTPUT -m owner \
  --uid-owner 1337 -j RETURN
📖 Why UID 1337?

The istio-proxy container runs as UID 1337. The iptables rules check --uid-owner 1337 and skip redirection for traffic from this UID. Without this exception, Envoy's own outbound traffic would be redirected back to itself, creating an infinite redirection loop. UID 1337 is the safety valve.

3.6 Sidecar Debugging Checklist — When Things Go Wrong

When your pod shows 1/1 instead of 2/2, or traffic isn't flowing as expected, use this systematic debug checklist:

🔍 SIDECAR DEBUGGING DECISION TREE
Pod shows 1/1 instead of 2/2?
Namespace labeled?
→ kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels
→ Fix: kubectl label ns anihpj istio-injection=enabled
Pod existed before label?
→ Injection only at CREATION time
→ Fix: kubectl delete pod <name> -n anihpj
Webhook working?
→ kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
→ Fix: Check istiod logs

3.7 Inside Envoy — Proxy Configuration Hierarchy

Envoy's configuration follows a strict hierarchy. Understanding this helps you debug routing issues quickly:

🏗️ ENVOY CONFIGURATION HIERARCHY — Bottom to Top
EDS
Endpoints

Pod IPs behind each service.
istioctl pc endpoint → IP list

CDS
Clusters

Upstream services (logical).
istioctl pc cluster → service list

RDS
Routes

How to route (path, headers, weights).
istioctl pc route → VS rules

LDS
Listeners

Which ports Envoy listens on.
istioctl pc listener → 15001, 15006

📖 Debugging Tip: Check Bottom-Up

When traffic isn't routing: (1) Check EDS first — are there healthy endpoints? (2) Check CDS — does the cluster exist? (3) Check RDS — are the routing rules applied? (4) Check LDS — is Envoy listening on the right ports? This bottom-up approach finds the root cause fastest.

📝 Chapter 3 Summary

  • Two planes: Data plane (Envoy sidecars handle traffic) + Control plane (istiod configures everything).
  • istiod = 3-in-1: Pilot (traffic), Citadel (certs), Galley (validation). Single binary since Istio 1.5.
  • xDS API: gRPC streaming — istiod pushes to Envoy in ~1-3s, no restart needed.
  • Sidecar injection: Automatic via Mutating Admission Webhook. istio-injection=enabled label triggers it.
  • iptables: istio-init redirects ALL traffic through Envoy. Inbound → 15006, Outbound → 15001, UID 1337 exempt.

📝 Chapter 3 — ICA Practice Questions (Hands-On Lab Scenarios)

Q1
You apply a new VirtualService but traffic still follows old routing rules. You suspect config didn't propagate. Task: Write the command to check proxy sync status for ALL proxies in the anihpj namespace. Explain what SYNCED, STALE, and NOT SENT mean. Then run the 4 proxy-config commands to verify the VirtualService rules are actually in Envoy's tables. Explain which xDS service each command maps to.
Show Answer
Answer
# Check ALL proxy sync status
istioctl proxy-status -n anihpj
# SYNCED  = proxy has latest config from istiod ✅
# STALE   = proxy hasn't acknowledged in a while ⚠️
# NOT SENT = istiod couldn't reach the proxy ❌

# Verify config is actually in Envoy (bottom-up debug):
istioctl pc endpoint deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj  # EDS: pod IPs
istioctl pc cluster deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj   # CDS: upstream services
istioctl pc route deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj     # RDS: VirtualService rules
istioctl pc listener deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj  # LDS: ports (15001, 15006)

Mapping: EDS=endpoints, CDS=DestinationRule, RDS=VirtualService, LDS=listeners. If your VS rule doesn't appear in pc route, istiod didn't push it.

Explanation

These 4 commands are your window into Envoy's brain. They map directly to the xDS API: EDS (Endpoint Discovery) shows pod IPs, CDS (Cluster Discovery) shows upstream services + subsets, RDS (Route Discovery) shows VirtualService rules, LDS (Listener Discovery) shows ports. On the ICA exam, ALWAYS debug bottom-up: check endpoints exist first, then clusters, then routes, then listeners. If endpoints are empty → no healthy pods. If route is missing → VS not applied. If listener is missing → sidecar not injected properly.

Q2
You SSH into a pod and inspect it. You see 3 containers: jobpost-api, istio-proxy, and an init container istio-init that already exited. Task: Identify which istiod sub-component (Pilot, Citadel, or Galley) is responsible for each of the following: (a) injecting the sidecar, (b) issuing the SPIFFE certificate, (c) pushing VirtualService config via xDS. Then explain why these three were merged into one istiod binary in Istio 1.5 and list what the OLD architecture looked like (4 pods vs 1 pod).
Show Answer
Answer
TaskComponentWhat It Does
(a) Sidecar injectionGalleyRuns the MutatingWebhook; validates CRDs before Pilot processes them
(b) SPIFFE certsCitadelCertificate Authority; issues X.509 SVIDs; auto-rotates every 24h
(c) xDS config pushPilotWatches CRDs → converts to Envoy config → pushes via gRPC xDS

OLD architecture (Istio < 1.5): 4 separate pods — pilot (100m/256Mi), citadel (50m/128Mi), galley (50m/128Mi), sidecar-injector (50m/128Mi). Complex gRPC between them. NEW (1.5+): 1 istiod pod (3-in-1). Fewer failure modes, in-process calls (faster), simpler upgrades.

Explanation

The ICA exam may reference the OLD names (Pilot, Citadel, Galley). Know that they still exist as sub-components inside istiod. Pilot = traffic configuration brain (CRDs → xDS). Citadel = security brain (SPIFFE certs, mTLS keys). Galley = quality gate (validates YAML, runs injection webhook). The merge to istiod eliminated 3 failure domains and 3 gRPC connections between components. On the exam, if asked "which component does X," the answer is the sub-component name, even though they all run in one binary now.

Q3
You're debugging why jobpost-api can't connect to user-service. You exec into the pod and run curl http://user-service:80/users/ — it connects fine. But the Django app (running as UID 1000) can't reach user-service. Task: Explain how iptables redirect works. Why did your curl work but Django fails? Write the command to view the iptables rules inside the Envoy container. Explain what would happen if the UID 1337 exemption didn't exist.
Show Answer
Answer

Why curl works but Django fails: When you exec into the pod, you typically run as root (UID 0). iptables rules redirect traffic based on UID — --uid-owner 1337 is EXEMPTED (Envoy's own traffic). Your root curl may bypass the redirect depending on how iptables is configured. Django runs as non-root (UID 1000) — its traffic IS caught by iptables and redirected to Envoy on port 15001.

# View iptables rules inside istio-proxy container:
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- \
  iptables -t nat -L -n -v

# Key rules:
# OUTPUT chain: redirect ALL TCP (except UID 1337) → port 15001
# PREROUTING chain: redirect ALL inbound TCP → port 15006

# Check UID 1337 exemption (critical!):
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- id
# uid=1337(istio-proxy) gid=1337(istio-proxy)

Without UID 1337 exemption: Envoy's own outbound traffic (to other Envoy proxies) would be caught by iptables and redirected back to ITSELF on port 15001 → infinite loop → Envoy crashes. The exemption is NOT optional.

Explanation

iptables operates at kernel level — transparent to applications. When Django calls requests.get("http://user-service"), the kernel resolves user-service to a ClusterIP, creates an outbound TCP packet, and the OUTPUT chain catches it: "Not from UID 1337? → REDIRECT to 127.0.0.1:15001." Envoy receives it, applies routing/mTLS/retry policies, and sends it to the real user-service pod. The entire redirect is invisible to Django — it thinks it connected directly to user-service on port 80. On the ICA exam, the istio-init container sets up these rules. UID 1337 is the Envoy process — always exempt.

Q4
Your production cluster runs 500 pods with Istio sidecars. istiod crashes at 3 AM. Task: Explain exactly what happens to: (a) the 500 existing pods and their traffic, (b) a new Deployment you try to roll out, (c) a VirtualService change you try to apply. Write the command to scale istiod to 3 replicas with a PodDisruptionBudget of minAvailable=2. Explain why separating control plane from data plane is the key architectural decision behind this resilience.
Show Answer
Answer
ScenarioResult
(a) 500 existing pods✅ Traffic flows NORMALLY. Envoy uses cached config in memory.
(b) New deployment❌ Pods stuck in Init. Can't get SPIFFE cert or config from istiod.
(c) VS change❌ Not propagated. No istiod to process the CRD and push via xDS.
 
# Scale to 3 replicas
kubectl scale deployment istiod -n istio-system --replicas=3

# PodDisruptionBudget: ensure at least 2 istiod pods are always available
kubectl create pdb istiod-pdb -n istio-system \
  --selector=app=istiod --min-available=2
Explanation

This is the architectural genius of Istio: control plane (brain) and data plane (muscles) are SEPARATED. If the brain (istiod) dies, the muscles (Envoy sidecars) keep working with their last-known-good configuration. This is like an airplane's autopilot — if air traffic control goes down, planes already in the air continue flying. With 3 replicas + PDB minAvailable=2, even if one node fails and istiod is rolling-updated, at least 2 copies are always available. On the ICA exam: "Does istiod failure cause traffic interruption?" → NO (for existing traffic). YES (for new pods and config changes).

Q5
You labeled the anihpj namespace with istio-injection=enabled and applied a new Deployment. Pods show 2/2 — sidecar injected. But one pod needs to NOT have a sidecar (it's a database migration job that must connect directly). Task: Write the per-pod annotation to opt OUT of sidecar injection while keeping namespace-level injection enabled for other pods. Then write the annotation to opt IN for a specific pod in an unlabeled namespace. Explain when to use namespace-level vs per-pod injection.
Show Answer
Answer
# OPT OUT: Pod in labeled namespace → no sidecar
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: db-migration-job
  namespace: anihpj
  annotations:
    sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false"    # ← Opt OUT
spec:
  # ... job container ...

# OPT IN: Pod in unlabeled namespace → WITH sidecar
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: special-pod
  namespace: default
  annotations:
    sidecar.istio.io/inject: "true"     # ← Opt IN
spec:
  # ... app container ...

# Verify injection status:
kubectl get pods -n anihpj db-migration-job
# Expected: READY 1/1 (no sidecar!)
kubectl get pods -n default special-pod
# Expected: READY 2/2 (sidecar injected!)

When to use namespace-level: All pods in the namespace should be meshed (production default). When to use per-pod: Gradual migration (inject one by one), exceptions (jobs, init containers), or testing.

Explanation

The annotation sidecar.istio.io/inject OVERRIDES the namespace label. Priority: per-pod annotation > namespace label. "false" in a labeled namespace = no injection. "true" in an unlabeled namespace = injection for only that pod. On the ICA exam, you may need to deploy a pod that must NOT have a sidecar (database migration, network test, init-only job). The annotation is the correct approach — don't remove the namespace label. Also: injection is a ONE-TIME event at pod CREATION. Adding the annotation to a running pod does nothing — you must delete and recreate it.

Q6
You run istioctl proxy-status and see one proxy showing STALE. The pod is Running (2/2) and serving traffic, but the config is from 10 minutes ago. Task: Explain what STALE means vs SYNCED vs NOT SENT. Write the commands to: (a) check if the proxy has the latest config version, (b) verify istiod can reach the proxy on port 15012, (c) force the proxy to reconnect. Explain what could cause STALE status.
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Check config versions
istioctl proxy-status deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj
# Shows CDS/LDS/EDS/RDS version numbers
# Compare with istiod's current version

# (b) Verify istiod→proxy connectivity
kubectl exec deploy/istiod -n istio-system -- \
  nc -zv <proxy-pod-ip> 15012
# Port 15012 = xDS gRPC (istiod→Envoy control channel)

# (c) Force proxy reconnect by restarting the sidecar
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- \
  curl -X POST localhost:15000/quitquitquit
# Envoy restarts and reconnects to istiod automatically

# Alternative: Delete the pod
kubectl delete pod <pod-name> -n anihpj

STALE causes: Network policy blocking port 15012, istiod overloaded (too many proxies), Envoy process hung, or temporary network blip that prevented the periodic heartbeat acknowledgment.

Explanation

SYNCED = proxy acknowledged latest config from istiod. STALE = proxy hasn't acknowledged in a while (config may still be current, but istiod can't confirm). NOT SENT = istiod couldn't even establish the connection. STALE is the most common — the proxy is still working with its last config but isn't getting updates. On the ICA exam, always check proxy-status after applying config changes. If a proxy is STALE, new VirtualService/DestinationRule changes won't reach it. Fix: restart the sidecar or delete the pod.

Q7
You're writing an AuthorizationPolicy that restricts access based on service identity. Task: Write the complete SPIFFE identity for a pod in the anihpj namespace using the jobpost-api service account. Break down each component: trust domain, namespace, service account. Then write the command to verify a specific pod's SPIFFE certificate is valid and has the correct identity. Explain why SPIFFE identities are more secure than IP-based access control.
Show Answer
Answer

SPIFFE Identity: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api

ComponentValueMeaning
Trust Domaincluster.localThe root of trust for this mesh
Namespacens/anihpjKubernetes namespace
Service Accountsa/jobpost-apiKubernetes ServiceAccount
 
# Verify SPIFFE certificate for a pod:
istioctl proxy-config secret deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj

# Check mTLS is active between two services:
istioctl authn tls-check deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj
# Shows: certificate chain, SPIFFE URI, validity period, mTLS status

SPIFFE vs IP: IP can be spoofed (ARP poisoning, IP hijacking). SPIFFE certificate is cryptographic — tied to the ServiceAccount, signed by istiod CA, impossible to forge without the private key.

Explanation

SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone) is the identity standard that underpins Istio's zero-trust model. Every workload gets a cryptographically verifiable identity tied to its Kubernetes ServiceAccount — not its IP address. An attacker can spoof an IP; they CANNOT spoof a SPIFFE certificate. The trust domain (cluster.local) is configurable — multi-cluster meshes share the same trust domain for cross-cluster mTLS. On the ICA exam, AuthorizationPolicy can match principals using the full SPIFFE identity or patterns like cluster.local/ns/anihpj/*.

Q8
You deploy a new pod in the anihpj namespace. It shows 1/1. The namespace IS labeled istio-injection=enabled. Other pods in the same namespace show 2/2. Task: List ALL possible causes (minimum 4) and the diagnostic command for each. Write a systematic debugging checklist in the CORRECT order. Include the webhook check, namespace label verification, and the command to test injection by simulating a pod creation.
Show Answer
Answer
# DEBUG CHECKLIST (in order):
# 1. Verify namespace label (most common miss)
kubectl get namespace anihpj --show-labels | grep istio-injection
# Expected: istio-injection=enabled

# 2. Verify webhook exists
kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
# Expected: NAME=istio-sidecar-injector, WEBHOOKS=1

# 3. Check per-pod annotation (overrides namespace label)
kubectl get pod <name> -n anihpj -o yaml | grep sidecar.istio.io/inject
# If shows "false" → pod opted OUT

# 4. Check if Deployment has injection disabled at template level
kubectl get deployment jobpost-api -n anihpj -o yaml | grep sidecar.istio.io/inject

# 5. Test injection by simulating pod creation:
kubectl apply -f - --dry-run=server -o yaml <
Explanation

This is THE most-asked ICA troubleshooting scenario. The order matters: start with the simplest check (namespace label), then webhook availability, then per-pod overrides, then Deployment template overrides. The --dry-run=server test is the definitive check — it runs the ACTUAL webhook against your spec and shows the modified output. If the dry-run output doesn't include istio-proxy, injection is broken at the webhook level. If it does include it, the issue is specific to that pod's annotations or labels. On the ICA exam, always follow this order — don't jump to complex diagnoses first.

Q9
You need to verify that Envoy is receiving configuration from istiod for a specific service. Task: Write the 4 istioctl proxy-config commands (listener, cluster, route, endpoint) for the jobpost-api deployment. For each command, explain what SUCCESS looks like. Then show how to use these commands to diagnose why traffic to user-service is getting 503s — what would you check and in what order?
Show Answer
Answer
# 1. LISTENER: Which ports is Envoy listening on?
istioctl pc listener deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj
# ✅ SUCCESS: Shows 0.0.0.0:15001 (outbound) and 0.0.0.0:15006 (inbound)
# ❌ MISSING: Sidecar not injected or iptables not configured

# 2. CLUSTER: Which upstream services does Envoy know about?
istioctl pc cluster deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj | grep user-service
# ✅ SUCCESS: Shows "outbound|8000||user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"
# ❌ MISSING: Service not in mesh or ServiceEntry not created

# 3. ROUTE: How does Envoy route to user-service?
istioctl pc route deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj | grep -A10 user-service
# ✅ SUCCESS: Shows VirtualService rules (weights, timeouts, retries)
# ❌ MISSING: VirtualService not applied or host mismatch

# 4. ENDPOINT: Which pod IPs are behind user-service?
istioctl pc endpoint deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj | grep user-service
# ✅ SUCCESS: Shows pod IPs with HEALTHY status
# ❌ EMPTY/UNHEALTHY: Circuit breaker open or all pods down

# 503 DEBUG ORDER (bottom-up):
# ENDPOINT → CLUSTER → ROUTE → LISTENER

503 diagnosis: Always start at ENDPOINT. If healthy endpoints exist but you still get 503s, the circuit breaker may be OPEN. Check outlierDetection in DestinationRule.

Explanation

The 4 proxy-config commands form Envoy's configuration hierarchy: ENDPOINT (pod IPs, bottom) → CLUSTER (logical service groups) → ROUTE (how requests are matched and forwarded) → LISTENER (ports, top). Debug BOTTOM-UP: if endpoints are empty, nothing else matters — the service has no healthy pods. If endpoints exist but cluster is missing, the DestinationRule isn't applied. If cluster exists but route is missing, the VirtualService isn't applied. On the ICA exam, this bottom-up approach is the FASTEST way to pinpoint routing issues.

Q10
Your security team asks you to explain how Istio prevents a compromised pod from injecting itself as a fake sidecar or intercepting traffic. Task: Explain the full Kubernetes admission chain that a Pod CREATE request goes through (kubectl → API → auth → webhooks → etcd). Explain exactly where Istio's MutatingWebhook sits in this chain. Then explain what happens if someone DELETES the istio-sidecar-injector webhook — how do you detect this, and how do you recover?
Show Answer
Answer

Kubernetes Admission Chain:

1
kubectl

Sends Pod spec to API server

2
Authentication

Who are you? (cert, token, OIDC)

3
Authorization

Are you allowed to create Pods? (RBAC)

4
Mutating Webhooks

⬅️ ISTIO INJECTS SIDECAR HERE
Pod spec MODIFIED before persistence

5
Validating Webhooks

Is the final spec valid?

6
etcd

Final modified spec persisted

# Detect: Webhook deleted
kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
# Error: not found → WEBHOOK IS GONE!

# Detect: New pods show 1/1 despite labeled namespace
kubectl get pods -n anihpj
# All new pods: READY 1/1 (no sidecar being injected)

# Recover: Reinstall Istio components
istioctl install --set profile=demo -y
# This recreates the webhook as part of istiod's configuration

# Verify:
kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
kubectl delete pod <test-pod> -n anihpj  # Force recreation
kubectl get pods -n anihpj  # Should show 2/2
Explanation

The MutatingAdmissionWebhook is Istio's injection mechanism. It runs INSIDE istiod (as part of Galley). When ANY pod is created in a labeled namespace, the Kubernetes API server calls this webhook with the Pod spec. The webhook returns a MODIFIED spec (adding istio-proxy container + istio-init container). Kubernetes persists the MODIFIED spec to etcd. Your original YAML is never changed on disk — the modification is in-memory only. If the webhook is deleted, pods are created WITHOUT modification → 1/1. On the ICA exam, always check the webhook if injection suddenly stops working cluster-wide.

💼 Chapter 3 — What This Means For Your Daily Work

  • Check proxy sync daily: istioctl proxy-status — all should show SYNCED
  • Know the ports: 15001 (outbound), 15006 (inbound), 15090 (metrics)
  • Debug injection issues: Check namespace label first, then redeploy pods
  • Understand the architecture: Data plane handles traffic, control plane configures. They're independent.
  • For ICA: "Check sync" = proxy-status. "How traffic gets to Envoy" = iptables. "Who issues certs" = Citadel.

Chapter 4: Installing and Configuring Istio

Time to get hands-on. You'll install Istio on a Kubernetes cluster, configure the anihpj namespace for automatic sidecar injection, deploy the application, and verify everything is working using istioctl commands and observability dashboards.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🛠️ Hands-On 🎯 Installation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Install istioctl CLI on Linux, macOS, or Windows
  • ✅ Choose the right installation profile (demo, default, minimal, ambient)
  • ✅ Install Istio with a single command and verify all pods are running
  • ✅ Enable sidecar injection and deploy anihpj with correct labels
  • ✅ Verify injection, check proxy sync, validate config
  • ✅ Open Kiali, Grafana, and Jaeger dashboards
🎓 ICA Relevance
Installation 15%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 4
  • "Install Istio with ALL observability tools" = istioctl install --set profile=demo -y
  • "Enable injection" = kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled
  • "Verify injection" = pods show 2/2 under READY
  • "Check config for errors" = istioctl analyze -n <namespace>
  • Port naming is CRITICAL: must start with "http", "grpc", "tcp", or "tls"
🔴🟢 YOUR CLUSTER — Before vs After Istio Installation
❌
BEFORE — Plain Kubernetes
  • 🔓 HTTP traffic between pods (no encryption)
  • 😶 No visibility — who called whom?
  • 🪣 Simple kube-proxy load balancing
  • 🛑 No retries, no circuit breakers
  • 📉 No distributed tracing
  • 🔑 No service-to-service auth
  • 🐌 Manual canary deployments
✅
AFTER — Istio Mesh
  • 🔒 mTLS encryption between ALL pods
  • 🗺️ Kiali service graph — full visibility
  • ⚖️ Weighted routing (90/10 canary)
  • 🔄 Automatic retries + circuit breaking
  • 🔍 Jaeger distributed tracing
  • 🛡️ AuthorizationPolicy — allow/deny
  • 📊 Grafana RED dashboards

4.1 Prerequisites and Cluster Requirements

☸️
Kubernetes

Version 1.28+ recommended. kubectl installed and pointing to your cluster.

💾
Resources

istiod: ~500m CPU, 2Gi RAM. Each sidecar: ~100m CPU, 128Mi RAM. anihpj (7 svcs): ~3 CPUs, 8Gi RAM total.

🖥️
Platforms

minikube, k3s, kind, Docker Desktop, EKS, GKE, AKS, OpenShift, on-prem.

✅
Quick Check

kubectl cluster-info · kubectl get nodes (all Ready) · kubectl top nodes (resources)

4.2 Installing istioctl (The Istio CLI)

istioctl is Istio's command-line tool — like kubectl but for Istio operations. You'll use it to install, analyze, debug, and open dashboards.

bash — Installing istioctl 

# Linux / macOS
curl -L https://istio.io/downloadIstio | sh -
cd istio-1.22.*
export PATH=$PWD/bin:$PATH

# Windows (PowerShell)
Invoke-WebRequest https://istio.io/downloadIstio -OutFile istio.zip
Expand-Archive istio.zip
# Add .\istio-1.22.*\bin to your PATH

# Verify installation
istioctl version
# → client version: 1.22.0
📥
istioctl install

Install Istio on cluster. Use --set profile=demo for learning.

🔍
istioctl analyze

Validate config for errors. Always run before applying changes.

📋
istioctl proxy-status

Check if all proxies are SYNCED with istiod. STALE = problem.

🔧
istioctl proxy-config

Inspect Envoy config: cluster, listener, route, endpoint.

🗺️
istioctl dashboard

Open Kiali, Grafana, Jaeger, Prometheus in browser.

🔄
istioctl upgrade

Upgrade Istio in-place. Always check release notes first.

📝
istioctl manifest

Generate/apply IstioOperator manifest for GitOps workflow.

🧪
istioctl experimental

Pre-release features. NEVER use in exam or production.

📖 Profile Deep Dive

remote: For multi-cluster — connects to an external control plane. No local istiod. empty: Nothing pre-configured — you add every component manually via IstioOperator. ambient: Sidecar-free mesh using ztunnel (per-node) + waypoint proxies. preview: Experimental features for testing upcoming releases — NEVER use in production.

⚠️ Important

demo profile is for LEARNING only — NOT production! It includes everything for convenience but is not tuned for performance or security. Use default for production and add observability tools separately via Helm.

bash — Complete Installation 

# Step 1: Verify cluster is ready
kubectl cluster-info
kubectl get nodes

# Step 2: Install Istio with demo profile
istioctl install --set profile=demo -y

# Step 3: Verify installation (should show 7 pods running)
kubectl get pods -n istio-system
kubectl get svc -n istio-system      # ← Check services too!
# NAME                                    READY   STATUS    RESTARTS
# istiod-7d7f9c7b8c-x4k2z               1/1     Running   0
# istio-ingressgateway-6d99ff9d4-9xklq   1/1     Running   0
# istio-egressgateway-7f9ddb899-z2p8k    1/1     Running   0
# grafana-6b7857866c-bj84p               1/1     Running   0
# jaeger-7f78b6fb65-hlfgp                1/1     Running   0
# kiali-7c9d5c754f-9cwq5                 1/1     Running   0
# prometheus-64fd8ccd65-nv98w            2/2     Running   0

# Step 4: Create and label anihpj namespace
kubectl create namespace anihpj
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled
kubectl get namespace anihpj --show-labels  # ← Verify label applied
# NAME     STATUS   AGE   LABELS
# anihpj   Active   10s   istio-injection=enabled,...

# Step 5: Deploy anihpj (see Appendix B for full YAML)
kubectl apply -f anihpj/k8s/ -n anihpj

# Step 6: Verify sidecars injected (should show 2/2)
kubectl get pods -n anihpj
# NAME                            READY   STATUS    RESTARTS
# jobpost-api-7d4f8b9c-x2k4z    2/2     Running   0
# user-service-6c9d8f7b-m3j5y    2/2     Running   0

# Step 7: Open dashboards
istioctl dashboard kiali
istioctl dashboard grafana
istioctl dashboard jaeger
🧬 HOW SIDECAR INJECTION WORKS — The Mutating Webhook
1
You Deploy

kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml

2
API Server

Receives Pod creation request

3
Check Label

Namespace has istio-injection=enabled?

4
Webhook

MutatingWebhookConfiguration intercepts

5
Inject

Adds istio-proxy + istio-init containers

6
Schedule

Scheduler places modified Pod on a Node

7
Run

istio-init runs first → sets iptables → app starts

💡 Key Insight: Injection is NOT retroactive. If you label a namespace AFTER pods exist, existing pods WON'T get sidecars. You must delete and recreate them. The webhook only fires on Pod creation events.

📖 Manual vs Automatic Injection

Automatic (recommended): Label the namespace → all future pods get sidecars automatically. This is what kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled does.

Manual: Use istioctl kube-inject -f deployment.yaml | kubectl apply -f -. This reads the YAML, adds the sidecar container definitions, and pipes the modified YAML to kubectl. Useful when you don't want to label a namespace or need per-pod control.

4.5 Verifying Istio is Working

✅
Proxy Sync Status

istioctl proxy-status
All pods must show SYNCED next to istiod. If any show STALE, the proxy isn't receiving config.

🔍
Inspect Proxy Config

istioctl proxy-config cluster deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj
Shows what upstream services Envoy knows about.

🩺
Validate Config

istioctl analyze -n anihpj
Catches 80% of configuration errors — missing subsets, port mismatches, VS without DR.

🧪
Traffic Test

kubectl exec -it deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -- curl user-service/users/
Should return HTTP 200. Check Kiali to see the traffic flow.

✅ POST-INSTALL VERIFICATION CHECKLIST — Is Everything Working?
1️⃣
Pods Running

kubectl get pods -n istio-system
→ All 7 pods Running (demo profile)

2️⃣
Services Created

kubectl get svc -n istio-system
→ istiod, ingressgateway, kiali, jaeger, prometheus, grafana

3️⃣
Sidecars Injected

kubectl get pods -n anihpj
→ Every pod shows 2/2 READY

4️⃣
Proxies Synced

→ All proxies show SYNCED

5️⃣
Config Valid

istioctl analyze -n anihpj
→ No validation issues found

6️⃣
Traffic Flows

kubectl exec -it deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -- curl -s user-service/users/
→ Returns HTTP 200 with data

💡 ICA Exam Tip

If the exam asks "verify the installation is working", run ALL of the above — in order. Start from the bottom (pods) and work up (traffic). Each step validates the layer below it. A pod showing 2/2 but proxy-status showing STALE means istiod can't reach the proxy (check port 15012).

4.6 Understanding the anihpj Deployment with Istio

Your Deployment YAML needs specific labels and port naming conventions for Istio to work correctly. Here's the annotated version:

yaml — jobpost-api Deployment (Istio-ready) 

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: jobpost-api      # ← Istio uses "app" label
      version: v1           # ← REQUIRED for canary subsets!
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: jobpost-api
        version: v1          # ← DestinationRule subsets match this
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: jobpost
        image: anihpj/jobpost:v1
        ports:
        - name: http          # ← MUST be "http" (Istio protocol detection)
          containerPort: 8000 # ← Port naming matters more than number
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: jobpost-api          # ← Matches Deployment label
  ports:
  - name: http                # ← MUST be "http"
    port: 80
    targetPort: 8000
⚠️ Critical Rules for ICA Exam

(1) Port names MUST start with protocol prefix: "http", "grpc", "tcp", or "tls". (2) Pods MUST have app and version labels. (3) Service selectors MUST match Deployment pod labels. Wrong port names = Istio ignores the port entirely.

📝 Chapter 4 — ICA Practice Questions (Hands-On Lab Scenarios)

Q1
Your ICA exam terminal just opened. The cluster is bare — no Istio installed. Task: Write the complete 7-step installation sequence to install Istio with ALL observability tools, enable sidecar injection for the anihpj namespace, deploy a sample app, and verify everything works. Include the exact command to open each dashboard. Then explain WHY you chose the demo profile and what you'd do differently for production.
Show Answer
Answer
# 1. Install Istio (demo = all observability in one command)
istioctl install --set profile=demo -y

# 2. Verify control plane pods (expect 7+ pods)
kubectl get pods -n istio-system

# 3. Create and label the namespace for injection
kubectl create namespace anihpj
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled

# 4. Deploy the application
kubectl apply -f anihpj-deployment.yaml -n anihpj

# 5. Verify sidecars injected (2/2 = success)
kubectl get pods -n anihpj

# 6. Verify proxies synced
istioctl proxy-status

# 7. Open dashboards for verification
istioctl dashboard kiali      # Service graph
istioctl dashboard grafana    # RED metrics
istioctl dashboard jaeger     # Distributed traces
istioctl dashboard prometheus # Raw metrics queries

Production difference: Use --set profile=default (no observability bundled). Install Kiali, Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana separately via Helm with proper persistence, resource limits, and HA. Add istio-egressgateway if needed. Scale istiod to 3 replicas with PDB.

Explanation

On the ICA exam, you start with a bare cluster. Your first task is ALWAYS to install Istio. The demo profile gives you everything in one shot — istiod, ingressgateway, egressgateway, Kiali, Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana. istioctl install creates the istio-system namespace and all CRDs automatically. After installation: verify pods (step 2), enable injection (step 3), deploy app (step 4), verify 2/2 (step 5), verify SYNCED (step 6), verify dashboards (step 7). If ANY step fails, stop and debug before proceeding.

Q2
You run istioctl install --set profile=default -y and then try to open Kiali with istioctl dashboard kiali. It fails: "no Kiali pods found." Task: Explain which profiles include Kiali and which don't. Write the command to check which profile was installed. Then write the command to upgrade your installation to include ALL observability tools WITHOUT uninstalling first. Explain what happens to existing sidecars during the upgrade.
Show Answer
Answer
# Check current profile and components:
istioctl profile list
kubectl get pods -n istio-system
# Shows only istiod + ingressgateway → you used "default" profile

# Upgrade to demo profile (adds Kiali, Jaeger, Grafana, Prometheus):
istioctl install --set profile=demo -y
# istioctl handles the upgrade — adds missing components in-place

# Verify now:
kubectl get pods -n istio-system  # Should show 7+ pods
istioctl dashboard kiali          # Should open Kiali UI

Profile comparison: demo = istiod + ingress + egress + Kiali + Jaeger + Prometheus + Grafana. default = istiod + ingressgateway only. minimal = istiod only. Existing sidecars: No interruption during upgrade — istiod handles the transition. Sidecars stay connected to the old istiod until the new one is ready.

Explanation

On the ICA exam, knowing which profile includes what is essential. If you can't open Kiali or Jaeger, you likely used the wrong profile. istioctl install is idempotent — running it again with a different profile UPGRADES your existing installation, adding missing components. Existing sidecars are not disrupted — istiod handles the transition gracefully. This is much faster than uninstalling and reinstalling. The demo profile is the default choice for ALL ICA exam tasks unless "production" is explicitly stated.

Q3
You deployed a Service with port name api-port (port 8000). Your VirtualService with URI-based routing (match: uri: prefix: /api/v1/) doesn't work — ALL traffic goes to all pods indiscriminately. Kiali shows TCP traffic instead of HTTP. Task: Diagnose why L7 routing failed. Write the CORRECT port name. List ALL Istio features that silently break with a wrong port name. Write the command to check the port name in the running Service.
Show Answer
Answer

Diagnosis: Port name api-port doesn't start with a protocol prefix. Istio uses port NAMES (not numbers) for protocol detection. Since "api-port" doesn't start with http, grpc, tcp, or tls, Istio treats ALL traffic on port 8000 as raw TCP.

# Fix: Change port name to start with "http"
kubectl patch service jobpost-api -n anihpj --type='json' \
  -p='[{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/ports/0/name","value":"http-api"}]'

# Verify the fix:
kubectl get service jobpost-api -n anihpj -o yaml | grep -A2 ports
# Expected: name: http-api  (starts with "http")

Features that silently break with wrong port naming: URI path-based routing, header-based routing, weighted canary splitting, retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, fault injection, distributed tracing propagation, L7 metrics (per-path, per-method). ALL of these require HTTP protocol detection.

Explanation

This is the #1 mistake on the ICA exam. Istio does NOT inspect packet contents to determine protocol — it relies ENTIRELY on port naming. Valid prefixes: http (HTTP/1.1 or HTTP/2), grpc (gRPC), tcp (raw TCP), tls (TLS-encrypted, no termination). Everything after the prefix is your choice: http-web, http-api, grpc-internal. On the ICA exam, if ANY L7 feature doesn't work, check port names FIRST. The fix is instant — patch the Service port name, and Envoy detects the protocol change within seconds via EDS updates.

Q4
You applied several Istio CRDs (Gateway, VirtualService, DestinationRule, PeerAuthentication). Before testing, you want to validate them. Task: Write the command to analyze ALL Istio configurations in the anihpj namespace. Explain what types of errors istioctl analyze catches. Then simulate a config error: write a VirtualService referencing a non-existent subset. Run analyze and show the expected error output. Fix the error.
Show Answer
Answer
# Analyze all configs in namespace:
istioctl analyze -n anihpj

# Cluster-wide analysis:
istioctl analyze --all-namespaces

# Simulate error: VS references non-existent subset
cat <

Errors caught: Missing subsets, port name mismatches, VS without DR, Gateway hosts not matching VS hosts, mTLS misconfigurations, conflicting rules, invalid YAML syntax, and Service selector mismatches.

Explanation

istioctl analyze is your PRE-APPLY validation step. It catches ~80% of configuration errors BEFORE they hit production. On the ICA exam, ALWAYS run istioctl analyze -n <ns> after applying CRDs and BEFORE testing. It's like terraform validate — if it shows errors, your config is broken and traffic will NOT route correctly. The analysis checks Istio's internal consistency — it knows which subsets exist in DestinationRules and will flag VirtualService references to non-existent ones.

Q5
Your pods in the anihpj namespace show 1/1 instead of 2/2. The namespace IS labeled istio-injection=enabled. Task: Write a systematic debugging checklist (minimum 5 checks in ORDER). For each check, write the exact command and what SUCCESS looks like. Include: namespace label verification, webhook existence check, per-pod annotation check, Deployment template check, and a dry-run test to confirm injection works.
Show Answer
Answer
# CHECK 1: Namespace label
kubectl get namespace anihpj --show-labels | grep istio-injection
# ✅ SUCCESS: istio-injection=enabled
# ❌ FAIL: Label missing or typo → kubectl label ns anihpj istio-injection=enabled

# CHECK 2: Webhook exists
kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
# ✅ SUCCESS: NAME=istio-sidecar-injector, WEBHOOKS=1
# ❌ FAIL: not found → istioctl install --set profile=demo -y

# CHECK 3: Pod annotation (overrides namespace)
kubectl get pod JOBPOST-POD-NAME -n anihpj -o yaml | grep sidecar.istio.io/inject
# ✅ SUCCESS: No "false" annotation
# ❌ FAIL: Shows "false" → pod opted OUT; remove annotation and redeploy

# CHECK 4: Deployment template annotation
kubectl get deployment jobpost-api -n anihpj -o yaml | grep -A2 annotations
# ❌ FAIL: sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" in template → remove it

# CHECK 5: Dry-run test to confirm injection works
kubectl apply -f - --dry-run=server -o yaml <
Explanation

This 5-step checklist is your ICA exam injection debugging workflow. ORDER MATTERS: start simple (namespace label), then infrastructure (webhook), then pod-level overrides (annotations), then template-level, then the definitive dry-run test. The dry-run test is the GOLD STANDARD — it actually calls the webhook with your spec and shows the modified output. If the dry-run doesn't include istio-proxy, injection is fundamentally broken. If it DOES include it, the issue is specific to your pod's configuration.

Q6
You deployed anihpj BEFORE labeling the namespace. All pods show 1/1. Task: Explain why existing pods didn't get sidecars. Write the CORRECT sequence to fix this (hint: labeling then doing what?). Explain what happens if you just label the namespace and wait. Then write a one-liner to restart all pods in the namespace to trigger injection.
Show Answer
Answer

Why existing pods stay at 1/1: The MutatingWebhook fires on Pod CREATE events only. Pods that already exist are NOT retroactively modified. Labeling the namespace tells the webhook "inject into FUTURE pods."

# CORRECT sequence:
# 1. Label namespace FIRST
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled

# 2. THEN deploy pods (or redeploy existing ones)
# For existing pods — trigger a rolling restart:
kubectl rollout restart deployment -n anihpj

# Verify:
kubectl get pods -n anihpj
# All pods should now show 2/2 (sidecar injected!)

# Alternative: Delete all pods (Deployments recreate them)
kubectl delete pods --all -n anihpj
# Each new pod gets the sidecar injected at creation time

If you just label and wait: Nothing happens. Existing pods stay at 1/1 forever. The webhook only fires on CREATION events, not on label changes.

Explanation

This is the single most common Istio newcomer mistake. Injection is a ONE-TIME event at Pod CREATION. The sequence MUST be: label namespace FIRST → then deploy pods. If you deployed first, you must redeploy (rollout restart or delete pods). The webhook cannot modify running pods — it intercepts the API call that CREATES them. On the ICA exam, always check the ORDER of operations: was the namespace labeled BEFORE or AFTER the pods were created? If after → redeploy. kubectl rollout restart deployment is the cleanest approach — it performs a rolling update, creating new pods (with sidecars) before terminating old ones.

Q7
Your ICA exam scenario says: "Configure Istio for a production environment. The cluster has limited resources — 4 CPU, 8GB RAM total." Task: Choose the right profile and justify your choice. Explain what you'd install and what you'd SKIP. Calculate the resource overhead: istiod (500m/2Gi), each sidecar (~100m/128Mi), and the total for 7 anihpj services. Determine if 4 CPU/8GB is sufficient.
Show Answer
Answer

Recommended profile: default — includes istiod + ingressgateway only. Skip Kiali, Jaeger, Prometheus, Grafana (add later if resources permit, or use external monitoring).

istioctl install --set profile=default -y
ComponentCPURAM
istiod (1 replica)500m2Gi
ingressgateway100m128Mi
7 sidecars (100m/128Mi each)700m896Mi
anihpj app containers~1,400m~3.5Gi
TOTAL~2,700m (2.7 CPU)~6.5Gi

Verdict: ✅ 4 CPU / 8GB is SUFFICIENT with the default profile. The demo profile would add ~1.5Gi and ~600m more for observability tools — pushing RAM to ~8Gi (tight but possible).

Explanation

On the ICA exam, resource constraints questions test your ability to choose the right profile. Default = production minimum (istiod + ingress). Minimal = only istiod (no ingress — use NodePort or external load balancer). Demo = everything (wasteful for production with limited resources). Each sidecar costs ~128Mi RAM and ~100m CPU. For 7 services: ~900Mi overhead. Plan your cluster resources accordingly. If resources are tight, consider ambient mesh (6× less memory — no per-pod sidecars).

Q8
You run kubectl get pods -n istio-system after installing with profile=demo. You see only 5 pods instead of the expected 7. Task: Identify which two pods are likely missing and why. Write the commands to: (a) check pod status and resource requests, (b) describe a Pending pod to see why it won't schedule, (c) switch to a smaller profile if resources are insufficient. Explain what each expected pod does.
Show Answer
Answer

Most likely missing: Kiali and Jaeger (or Prometheus/Grafana). Reason: insufficient cluster resources — these pods have higher resource requests and are stuck in Pending.

# (a) Check pod status and resource requests
kubectl get pods -n istio-system
kubectl describe nodes | grep -A5 "Allocated resources"

# (b) Describe Pending pod for scheduling failure reason
kubectl describe pod -n istio-system -l app=kiali
# Look for: "Events: 0/3 nodes are available: insufficient cpu"

# (c) Switch to smaller profile if resources are insufficient:
istioctl install --set profile=default -y
# default = istiod + ingressgateway only (~600Mi vs demo's ~2Gi)

Demo profile pods (7): istiod (control plane), istio-ingressgateway (external traffic entry), istio-egressgateway (external traffic exit), kiali (service graph), jaeger (distributed tracing), prometheus (metrics collection), grafana (metrics dashboards).

Explanation

On the ICA exam, always verify your installation with kubectl get pods -n istio-system. Count the pods — the number tells you which profile was installed. Demo = 7+, default = 2, minimal = 1. If pods are Pending, kubectl describe pod shows the reason (usually "insufficient cpu" or "insufficient memory"). The ICA exam cluster should have enough resources for the demo profile, but if not, fall back to default and add only what you need. Knowing what each pod does helps you decide which ones are optional.

Q9
You successfully installed Istio and deployed anihpj. Pods show 2/2. Now you need to verify that Istio is ACTUALLY routing traffic through the sidecars — not just that the containers are running. Task: Write the 4-command verification sequence: (a) proxy sync status, (b) Envoy cluster check, (c) a live traffic test using curl from inside a pod, (d) Kiali verification showing the service graph. For each, explain what SUCCESS looks like.
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Proxy sync — all must show SYNCED
istioctl proxy-status
# ✅ SUCCESS: All proxies show SYNCED
# ❌ Any STALE or NOT SENT → istiod can't reach that proxy

# (b) Envoy knows about upstream services
istioctl proxy-config cluster deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj | grep user-service
# ✅ SUCCESS: Shows "outbound|8000||user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"
# ❌ Empty → Service not in mesh or ServiceEntry missing

# (c) Live traffic test through the mesh
kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- \
  curl -s http://user-service:8000/users/ | head -c 100
# ✅ SUCCESS: Returns HTTP 200 with JSON data
# ❌ 503 → Circuit breaker open or no healthy endpoints

# (d) Kiali verification
istioctl dashboard kiali
# ✅ SUCCESS: Service graph shows jobpost-api → user-service with green edges
# ❌ No edges → traffic not flowing through mesh
Explanation

Pods showing 2/2 only proves the sidecar CONTAINER is running — not that it's routing traffic. These 4 verification steps confirm Istio is FUNCTIONALLY working: (a) proxy sync confirms istiod pushed config, (b) cluster check confirms Envoy knows about upstream services, (c) live curl confirms traffic actually flows through the mesh (the response goes through Envoy's inbound and outbound listeners), (d) Kiali visually confirms the traffic topology. On the ICA exam, always run ALL 4 checks after installation — never assume it works because pods show 2/2.

Q10
Your team wants to deploy anihpj to 3 environments using GitOps: dev, staging, prod. Each needs different Istio configurations. Task: Design the profile strategy for each environment. Specify which profile, which components, and which observability settings. For production, write the complete configuration: profile, istiod replicas, PDB, resource limits, tracing sampling rate, and mTLS mode. Explain why you can't use the same profile everywhere.
Show Answer
Answer
EnvironmentProfileComponentsSettings
devdemoAll-in-one: istiod + ingress + egress + Kiali + Jaeger + Prometheus + GrafanaTracing: 100% sampling, mTLS: PERMISSIVE
stagingdefault + addonsistiod + ingress + Kiali + JaegerTracing: 50% sampling, mTLS: PERMISSIVE→STRICT, istiod: 2 replicas
proddefault + addonsistiod + ingress + Kiali + Prometheus + GrafanaTracing: 1% sampling, mTLS: STRICT, istiod: 3 replicas + PDB, resource limits set
 
# Production configuration:
istioctl install --set profile=default -y \
  --set values.pilot.resources.requests.cpu=500m \
  --set values.pilot.resources.requests.memory=2Gi \
  --set meshConfig.defaultConfig.tracing.sampling=1.0

# Scale for HA:
kubectl scale deployment istiod -n istio-system --replicas=3
kubectl create pdb istiod-pdb -n istio-system \
  --selector=app=istiod --min-available=2

Why not same profile everywhere: demo has EVERYTHING with no resource limits — fine for dev but wastes 1.5Gi RAM in production. demo's PERMISSIVE mTLS is not secure enough for prod. demo's 1% tracing sampling is useless for debugging in dev where you need 100%.

Explanation

Environment-specific configuration is a production best practice. Dev needs full observability (100% tracing, all dashboards) for debugging. Staging mirrors production config to catch issues before deployment. Production prioritizes security (STRICT mTLS), performance (1% tracing), and reliability (3 istiod + PDB). The demo profile is ONLY for dev/learning — the ICA exam will ask you to identify the correct profile for a given scenario. Key differentiators: resource constraints → default/minimal, security requirements → default + STRICT mTLS, full observability → demo.

🔧 Troubleshooting — Common Installation Issues

⚠️
Pods stuck in Pending

Symptom: kubectl get pods -n istio-system shows pods stuck in Pending.

Fix: kubectl describe pod <name> -n istio-system — likely insufficient CPU/RAM. Either add nodes or use minimal profile.

⚠️
Sidecar NOT injected (1/1)

Symptom: Pod shows 1/1 instead of 2/2 after deploying.

Fix: (1) Verify namespace label: kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels. (2) Delete pod — injection happens at CREATION time. (3) Check webhook: kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration.

🚫
istioctl: command not found

Symptom: Terminal says "istioctl: command not found".

Fix: Add istio's bin/ directory to PATH: export PATH=$PWD/bin:$PATH (Linux/Mac) or update System Environment Variables (Windows).

🚫
istioctl analyze shows errors

Symptom: istioctl analyze returns validation errors.

Fix: Common causes: port names not prefixed with protocol, VirtualService without DestinationRule, missing subsets, or Service selector mismatch with Deployment labels.

🔄
Proxy shows STALE

Symptom: istioctl proxy-status shows STALE for some proxies.

Fix: istiod can't reach the proxy. Check: (1) Network policy blocking port 15012. (2) istiod pod is Running. (3) Restart the stale proxy's pod.

🔌
Services unreachable after install

Symptom: curl between services returns 503 or timeout.

Fix: (1) Check mTLS mode — STRICT requires both sides. (2) Verify DestinationRule exists. (3) Run istioctl proxy-config cluster deploy/<name> -n anihpj to see if Envoy knows about upstream.

💼 Chapter 4 — Daily Work Takeaways

  • One command to install: istioctl install --set profile=demo -y
  • One label to enable: kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled
  • Three checks after install: pods (2/2), proxy-status (SYNCED), analyze (no errors)
  • Port naming is non-negotiable: "http", "grpc", "tcp", "tls" — anything else and Istio ignores the port

🚦 Part 5: Traffic Management — Controlling How Services Communicate Chapters 5-8

Chapter 5: Gateway — The Front Door to Your Mesh

The Gateway is the entry point for ALL external traffic into your Istio service mesh. Think of it as the doorman at an exclusive club — it checks WHO you are (hostname), WHICH door you're using (port), and whether you have the right credentials (TLS). Without a Gateway, no external traffic can reach your services.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🌐 Networking 🎯 ICA 30%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand what a Gateway is and how it differs from Kubernetes Ingress
  • ✅ Write Gateway YAML to accept HTTP and HTTPS traffic for anihpj
  • ✅ Connect Gateway to VirtualService (the mandatory two-resource pattern)
  • ✅ Configure TLS termination at the edge
🎓 ICA Relevance
Traffic Management 30%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 5
  • "Accept external traffic" = Gateway CRD with selector: istio: ingressgateway
  • "Gateway + VS connection" = VS must list Gateway in gateways field: <ns>/<gw-name>
  • "TLS at the edge" = mode: SIMPLE + credentialName referencing a TLS secret in same namespace
  • "Multiple hosts" = hosts: ["api.anihpj.com", "*.anihpj.com"] — wildcards supported
  • "Gateway without VS" = Traffic DROPPED — Gateway opens port but has no routing rules
  • Exam hands-on: Write Gateway YAML from scratch — hosts, port, protocol, selector, TLS
🌐 COMPLETE REQUEST JOURNEY — External User → anihpj Pod
1
Internet

GET https://api.anihpj.com/jobs/

2
Cloud LB

AWS ALB / GCP LB → NodePort

3
Gateway

Host match + TLS termination

4
VirtualService

URI /jobs/* → 90% v1, 10% v2

5
DestinationRule

LEAST_CONN + mTLS + circuit breaker

6
K8s Service

Endpoints: 10.244.1.5:8000, ...

7
Pod

Envoy → Django :8000 → JSON response

📋 RESOURCE RESPONSIBILITY — Who Does What?

ResourceQuestion It AnswersScope
GatewayWHO can enter? (host, port, TLS)Edge ingress
VirtualServiceWHERE to route? (path, weight, headers)Per-service routing
DestinationRuleHOW to send? (LB, mTLS, circuit breaker)Per-service policies
K8s ServiceWHAT pods exist? (endpoint discovery)Pod discovery
PeerAuthenticationENCRYPT? (mTLS mode per namespace)Transport security
AuthorizationPolicyALLOWED? (who can call this)Access control

5.1 What is an Istio Gateway?

A Gateway is the entry point for traffic FROM OUTSIDE the mesh. It replaces (or works alongside) a standard Kubernetes Ingress but is far more powerful. The Gateway is deployed as its own Pod — istio-ingressgateway — which is an Envoy proxy instance dedicated to edge traffic.

📦
Kubernetes Ingress
  • ✅ Simple host/path routing
  • ❌ No header manipulation
  • ❌ No mTLS termination
  • ❌ No weighted routing
  • ❌ Limited TLS options
  • ❌ No egress control
🌐
Istio Gateway
  • ✅ Same host/path routing + MORE
  • ✅ Full header control
  • ✅ mTLS at the edge
  • ✅ Weight-based traffic split
  • ✅ Advanced TLS/SNI
  • ✅ Egress gateway for outbound
📖 Two Types of Gateways

IngressGateway: External traffic INTO the mesh (what we focus on). Deployed as istio-ingressgateway Pod.

EgressGateway: Controlled traffic OUT of the mesh to external APIs. Deployed as istio-egressgateway Pod. Covered in Chapter 8.

5.2 Gateway YAML — Accepting Traffic for anihpj

Here's the complete annotated Gateway resource for anihpj. It accepts HTTP on port 80 and HTTPS on port 443 for anihpj.example.com.

yaml — anihpj-gateway.yaml 

apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: anihpj-gateway          # ← name this gateway
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway       # ← which Envoy pod handles this
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 80               # ← accept HTTP on port 80
      name: http
      protocol: HTTP
    hosts:
    - "anihpj.example.com"     # ← only accept this hostname
  - port:
      number: 443              # ← accept HTTPS on port 443
      name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
    tls:
      mode: SIMPLE            # ← TLS termination at gateway
      credentialName: anihpj-tls-cert  # ← K8s secret with cert
    hosts:
    - "anihpj.example.com"
💡 Key Gateway Fields

selector.istio: ingressgateway — This targets the istio-ingressgateway Pod that Istio creates during installation. That Pod has the label istio: ingressgateway.

TLS mode SIMPLE — Standard one-way TLS. The gateway holds the certificate, client verifies it. For mutual TLS at the edge, use MUTUAL.

credentialName — References a Kubernetes Secret in the same namespace containing tls.crt and tls.key.

5.3 How Gateway Connects to VirtualService

The Gateway and VirtualService work as a mandatory pair. The Gateway says "I accept this traffic" and the VirtualService says "now route it like this." They are linked by the gateways field in VirtualService.

🔗 GATEWAY + VIRTUALSERVICE — The Mandatory Two-Resource Pattern
1️⃣
STEP 1: Gateway

"I accept this traffic"

  • Listen on port 80/443
  • For host: anihpj.example.com
  • On: istio-ingressgateway Pod
2️⃣
STEP 2: VirtualService

"Now route it like this"

  • gateways: [anihpj-gateway]
  • hosts: [anihpj.example.com]
  • match: uri /jobs/*
  • route: to jobpost-api:8000
⚠️ CRITICAL RULE

Gateway WITHOUT VirtualService = traffic DROPPED. The Gateway accepts the connection but has no routing rules, so the request goes nowhere.

VirtualService without Gateway = mesh-internal routing only. The VS only applies to service-to-service calls within the mesh, not external traffic.

🌐 Gateway — Visual Recap

🚪
Gateway
External entry point
🏷️
Hosts
Which domains accepted
🔢
Ports
80 (HTTP) / 443 (HTTPS)
🔒
TLS
SIMPLE / MUTUAL modes
🎯
Selector
istio: ingressgateway
🔗
+ VS
Mandatory pair pattern

5.X Common Gateway Patterns — Production-Ready Configurations

🌐
Pattern 1: HTTP Only

Port 80, no TLS. Simple dev setup. Not for production.

port: {number:80, name:http, protocol:HTTP}
hosts: ["api.anihpj.com"]
🔒
Pattern 2: HTTPS + TLS

Port 443 with SIMPLE TLS. Production standard.

port: {number:443, name:https, protocol:HTTPS}
tls: {mode:SIMPLE, credentialName:cert}
hosts: ["api.anihpj.com"]
🔄
Pattern 3: HTTP→HTTPS Redirect

Two servers: port 80 redirects to 443.

servers:
- port:80 → redirect to 443
- port:443 → HTTPS + TLS
💡 ICA Exam Pattern

The exam frequently asks you to configure a Gateway that accepts HTTPS on port 443 for a specific hostname. Remember: selector: istio: ingressgateway is MANDATORY — without it, the Gateway config has no Envoy to bind to. The TLS secret must be in the SAME namespace as the Gateway (typically istio-system).

📝 Chapter 5 — ICA Practice Questions (Hands-On Lab Scenarios)

Q1
Your ICA exam terminal has Istio installed. Task: Write a complete Gateway YAML from scratch that exposes the jobpost-api service on HTTP port 80 for the hostname api.anihpj.com. Then write the matching VirtualService that routes all requests to the v1 subset. Apply both and verify using istioctl proxy-config listener that the Gateway listener was pushed to the ingressgateway Envoy. Explain what happens if you test with curl -H "Host: wrong.com".
Show Answer
Answer
# Gateway YAML
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: anihpj-gateway
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 80
      name: http
      protocol: HTTP
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"

---
# VirtualService YAML
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-vs
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  hosts:
  - "api.anihpj.com"
  gateways:
  - anihpj-gateway
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v1

# Apply
kubectl apply -f gateway.yaml
kubectl apply -f virtual-service.yaml

# Verify listener pushed
istioctl proxy-config listener deploy/istio-ingressgateway -n istio-system | grep 80
# ✅ Shows 0.0.0.0:80 bound to api.anihpj.com

curl -H "Host: wrong.com": Returns 404 — the Gateway's hosts list only matches api.anihpj.com. Requests with mismatched Host headers are rejected at the Gateway level before reaching any VirtualService.

Explanation

The Gateway + VirtualService is Istio's two-resource ingress pattern. Gateway = L4 listener (opens the port, checks Host header). VirtualService = L7 routing (decides which service+subset). Both must be in the same namespace (typically istio-system). The gateways field in VirtualService MUST reference the Gateway by name — without it, the VS only applies to mesh-internal traffic. The three-part chain is: request Host → Gateway hosts → VirtualService hosts → destination. Break any link = 404.

Q2
You created a Gateway but external traffic gets 503 "no healthy upstream." The Gateway and VirtualService both exist. Task: Debug this systematically using THREE commands. Check: (a) Gateway listener is bound, (b) VirtualService route is populated, (c) endpoint is reachable. Write the fix if the VirtualService's gateways field is missing the Gateway reference.
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Check Gateway listener is bound
istioctl proxy-config listener deploy/istio-ingressgateway -n istio-system
# ✅ Must show 0.0.0.0:80 with route to VirtualService
# ❌ Empty → Gateway not pushed

# (b) Check VS route is populated in Envoy
istioctl proxy-config route deploy/istio-ingressgateway -n istio-system
# ✅ Must show jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:80
# ❌ Empty → VS not connected to Gateway

# (c) Check endpoint exists
istioctl proxy-config endpoint deploy/istio-ingressgateway -n istio-system | grep jobpost-api
# ✅ Shows pod IPs for jobpost-api service
# ❌ Empty → No pods or Service selector mismatch

# FIX: If gateways field is missing from VS
kubectl patch virtualservice jobpost-api-vs -n istio-system --type='json' \
  -p='[{"op":"add","path":"/spec/gateways","value":["anihpj-gateway"]}]'
Explanation

503 troubleshooting is a 3-step chain: Gateway listener → VS route → Service endpoint. On the ICA exam, work in this ORDER. The most common cause: forgetting gateways field in VirtualService — the VS routes mesh-internal traffic but NOT traffic coming through the Gateway. Without this field, the Gateway opens the port but has no routing instructions, resulting in 503. istioctl proxy-config commands show you exactly what Envoy knows — use them instead of guessing.

Q3
You need to configure a production Gateway with TLS for api.anihpj.com. Task: (a) Generate a self-signed TLS certificate and create the Kubernetes secret. (b) Write the Gateway YAML with SIMPLE TLS mode on port 443 referencing credentialName. (c) Verify the TLS configuration was pushed to Envoy using istioctl proxy-config listener and check for the TLS context. (d) What would you change for MUTUAL mode with client certificate verification?
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Generate self-signed cert and create secret
openssl req -x509 -newkey rsa:4096 -keyout server.key -out server.crt \
  -days 365 -nodes -subj "/CN=api.anihpj.com"
kubectl create secret tls anihpj-tls-cert \
  --cert=server.crt --key=server.key \
  -n istio-system

# (b) Gateway YAML with SIMPLE TLS
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: anihpj-gateway
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 443
      name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
    tls:
      mode: SIMPLE
      credentialName: anihpj-tls-cert
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"

# (c) Verify TLS config in Envoy
istioctl proxy-config listener deploy/istio-ingressgateway -n istio-system -o json | grep -A5 tls
# ✅ Shows TLS context with certificate chain
# (d) MUTUAL mode — add caCertificates
tls:
  mode: MUTUAL
  credentialName: anihpj-tls-cert
  # Secret must also contain ca.crt for client verification
Explanation

On the ICA exam, expect to configure TLS on a Gateway. SIMPLE mode = one-way (gateway presents cert, client verifies). MUTUAL mode = two-way (both present certs). The secret MUST be in the same namespace as the Gateway and must be type kubernetes.io/tls. For MUTUAL, the secret needs ca.crt additionally. The credentialName field references the Kubernetes secret — Istiod mounts it into the ingressgateway Envoy as a Secret Discovery Service (SDS) resource.

Q4
Your Gateway uses the selector app: my-custom-gateway but the Gateway configuration never appears in any Envoy. Task: Write the command to verify the selector matches a running pod. If no pod has that label, deploy a custom ingressgateway Deployment with the correct label. Then verify the Gateway config is pushed to the new Envoy. Explain why the default istio: ingressgateway selector wouldn't work.
Show Answer
Answer
# Verify selector matches — empty = no pod has label
kubectl get pods -n istio-system -l app=my-custom-gateway
# ❌ No resources found → selector doesn't match any pod

# Deploy custom ingressgateway with the label
kubectl apply -f - <<EOF
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: my-custom-gateway
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: my-custom-gateway
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: my-custom-gateway
        istio: my-custom-gateway
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: istio-proxy
        image: auto  # Injected by Istio
EOF

# Verify config pushed
istioctl proxy-config listener deploy/my-custom-gateway -n istio-system

Why default selector won't work: The Gateway selector is a LABEL MATCH. If you use app: my-custom-gateway, Istiod only pushes this Gateway config to pods with that label. The default istio-ingressgateway pod has label istio: ingressgateway — not app: my-custom-gateway. Label mismatch = Gateway config floats in etcd with nowhere to go.

Explanation

The Gateway selector is a LABEL SELECTOR — it binds Gateway CRD configuration to Envoy pods by matching labels. If no pod's labels match the selector, the Gateway config is never pushed to ANY Envoy. This is a silent failure: kubectl get gateway shows the resource exists, but it's useless. On the ICA exam, always verify: kubectl get pods -n istio-system -l <selector-key>=<selector-value>. If empty = your Gateway is dead. Use istio: ingressgateway (default) or deploy a custom gateway with matching labels.

Q5
Your Gateway YAML has hosts: ["*"]. The VirtualService has hosts: ["jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"]. Task: Will external traffic reach the VirtualService?
