Explain the three-part matching chain in detail. Then rewrite the VirtualService to correctly match the Gateway. Test with two different Host headers (api.anihpj.com and admin.anihpj.com) — which one reaches the service?
Show Answer
Answer

Will it work? ❌ NO. The Gateway accepts ALL hosts (*), but the VirtualService's hosts doesn't match any external hostname. The VS only routes requests whose Host header matches jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local — an internal K8s service name, not an external hostname.

# The three-part chain:
# 1. Request arrives with Host: api.anihpj.com
# 2. Gateway matches: hosts: ["*"] ✅ — accepts the connection
# 3. VirtualService matches: hosts: ["jobpost-api.anihpj.svc..."] ❌ — NO MATCH

# FIX: VirtualService must use external hostname
spec:
  hosts:
  - "api.anihpj.com"       # Match external hostname
  gateways:
  - anihpj-gateway
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local  # Internal destination
        subset: v1

Test results: curl -H "Host: api.anihpj.com" → ✅ reaches service. curl -H "Host: admin.anihpj.com" → 404 (Gateway matches * but VirtualService doesn't match admin...).

Explanation

This is the most confusing part of Gateway/VS matching on the ICA exam. The hosts field in Gateway and VirtualService serve DIFFERENT purposes: Gateway hosts = which external Host headers to accept (L4), VirtualService hosts = which requests to route (L7). They must ALIGN. If Gateway uses *, the VS can use any external hostname. If Gateway uses api.anihpj.com, the VS must use the SAME hostname. The DESTINATION host inside the VS's route block can be an internal K8s service FQDN — that's separate from the VS's hosts field.

Q6
You need to expose 3 services through one Gateway: api.anihpj.com (HTTP), admin.anihpj.com (HTTPS with TLS), and all other subdomains (HTTP). Task: Write the complete Gateway YAML with all server entries. Show BOTH the port-80 and port-443 server blocks. Then write THREE separate VirtualServices (one per hostname). Explain why you can't use a single VirtualService for all three.
Show Answer
Answer
# Gateway: Two servers (HTTP + HTTPS), three host categories
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: anihpj-gateway
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 80
      name: http
      protocol: HTTP
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"
    - "*.anihpj.com"       # Wildcard covers all other subdomains
  - port:
      number: 443
      name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
    tls:
      mode: SIMPLE
      credentialName: admin-tls-cert
    hosts:
    - "admin.anihpj.com"

Three separate VSes: You CAN technically use one VS with multiple hosts, but SEPARATE VSes are best practice — each service gets its own routing rules, fault injection, retries, and timeouts. One VS per hostname keeps configurations independent.

# VS 1: API
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: api-vs
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  hosts: ["api.anihpj.com"]
  gateways: [anihpj-gateway]
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        port: {number: 8000}
Explanation

A single Gateway can have multiple servers entries — each with different ports, protocols, and host lists. This is the standard production pattern. On the ICA exam, you may be asked to configure mixed HTTP/HTTPS on one Gateway. Each server entry is independent — port 80 servers don't know about port 443 servers. Wildcards (*.anihpj.com) match single-level subdomains. For multiple explicit hosts on the same port, list them all in the same server block's hosts array.

Q7
Your Gateway references credentialName: anihpj-tls-cert but clients get "SSL_ERROR_NO_CYPHER_OVERLAP" or connection refused on port 443. Task: Debug this systematically: (a) verify the secret exists and has correct type, (b) verify data keys are tls.crt and tls.key (not cert.pem), (c) verify the Gateway and secret are in the SAME namespace, (d) check istiod logs for SDS errors. Write the fix for each failure.
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Verify secret exists with correct type
kubectl get secret anihpj-tls-cert -n istio-system
# ✅ TYPE: kubernetes.io/tls
# ❌ Not found → create it; wrong type → recreate

# (b) Verify data keys (MUST be tls.crt and tls.key)
kubectl get secret anihpj-tls-cert -n istio-system -o jsonpath='{.data}' | jq 'keys'
# ✅ ["tls.crt", "tls.key"]
# ❌ ["cert.pem", "key.pem"] → WRONG keys

# Fix: Create with correct field names
kubectl create secret tls anihpj-tls-cert \
  --cert=server.crt --key=server.key -n istio-system

# (c) Verify Gateway and secret are in SAME namespace
kubectl get gateway anihpj-gateway -n istio-system -o yaml | grep credentialName
# Gateway in istio-system → secret MUST be in istio-system

# (d) Check istiod for SDS errors
kubectl logs deploy/istiod -n istio-system | grep -i "sds\|tls\|secret"
# Look for: "failed to generate secret" or "secret not found"
Explanation

TLS secret debugging on the ICA exam follows this checklist: 1) Secret EXISTS? 2) Secret TYPE is kubernetes.io/tls? 3) Data keys are tls.crt and tls.key (not arbitrary names)? 4) Secret in SAME namespace as Gateway? 5) credentialName matches secret name EXACTLY? Any mismatch = TLS silently breaks. The kubectl create secret tls command auto-sets the correct type and keys. Avoid creating TLS secrets with kubectl create secret generic — it uses the wrong type.

Q8
You need a Gateway that redirects ALL HTTP (port 80) traffic to HTTPS (port 443) for api.anihpj.com. Task: Write the Gateway YAML with TWO server entries — one for HTTP that redirects, and one for HTTPS with SIMPLE TLS. Then write the VirtualService that handles the HTTPS traffic. Explain where the redirect rule lives (Gateway or VirtualService?) and what HTTP status code is returned to the client.
Show Answer
Answer
# Gateway: HTTP redirect + HTTPS serve
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: anihpj-gateway
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 80
      name: http
      protocol: HTTP
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"
    tls:
      httpsRedirect: true        # ← Redirect happens HERE (Gateway level)
  - port:
      number: 443
      name: https
      protocol: HTTPS
    tls:
      mode: SIMPLE
      credentialName: anihpj-tls-cert
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"

Where does the redirect live? In the GATEWAY's server configuration — tls: httpsRedirect: true. It's a Gateway-level setting, not a VirtualService rule. The Gateway returns HTTP 301 Moved Permanently to the client, redirecting to HTTPS.

Explanation

HTTPS redirect is a Gateway-level feature — set tls: httpsRedirect: true on the HTTP server block. The Gateway itself sends the 301 redirect; the request never reaches a VirtualService. On the ICA exam, this is a common scenario: "ensure all HTTP traffic is redirected to HTTPS." The answer is in the Gateway, not the VirtualService. Remember: you need TWO server entries (port 80 for redirect, port 443 for actual TLS serving).

Q9
You applied a Gateway and VirtualService but the ingressgateway pod shows "STALE" in istioctl proxy-status. Task: Explain what STALE means vs SYNCED vs NOT SENT. Write the command to check. Then debug: (a) check if istiod can reach the gateway pod, (b) check if there's a network policy blocking port 15012, (c) force a config push. Explain how the XDS protocol keeps proxies in sync and what happens when it breaks.
Show Answer
Answer
# Check proxy sync status
istioctl proxy-status
# ✅ SYNCED   — Config is pushed and acknowledged
# ⚠️ STALE    — Config was pushed but proxy hasn't ACKed recently
# ❌ NOT SENT — istiod hasn't even pushed config yet

# (a) Check connectivity istiod → gateway
kubectl exec deploy/istiod -n istio-system -- \
  curl -s http://istio-ingressgateway:15020/healthz
# ✅ Returns OK

# (b) Check for NetworkPolicy blocking port 15012 (XDS)
kubectl get networkpolicy -n istio-system
# Any DENY policy on port 15012 blocks XDS sync

# (c) Force config push by touching a resource
kubectl annotate gateway anihpj-gateway -n istio-system \
  force-sync="$(date +%s)" --overwrite

XDS protocol: istiod streams config updates to Envoy over gRPC on port 15012. Envoy ACKs each update. STALE means the stream is broken — Envoy stopped ACKing. NOT SENT means the stream was never established.

Explanation

Proxy sync status is the first thing to check on the ICA exam when routing doesn't work. istioctl proxy-status shows the sync state of EVERY proxy in the mesh. SYNCED = healthy. STALE = Envoy disconnected (network issue, pod restart, resource exhaustion). NOT SENT = istiod can't reach the proxy. The XDS stream on port 15012 MUST be open — check NetworkPolicies, firewalls, and pod-to-pod connectivity. A quick force-sync trick: annotate any resource to trigger istiod to re-push all configs.

Q10
You need to expose anihpj's jobpost-api to external B2B partners who must present a client certificate. Task: Write the complete end-to-end configuration: (a) generate CA, server cert, and client cert using OpenSSL, (b) create the Kubernetes secret with ALL required fields for MUTUAL TLS, (c) write the Gateway with mode: MUTUAL and the required caCertificates field, (d) verify the client cert is being validated. Explain what happens if a client connects WITHOUT a certificate or with an UNTRUSTED certificate.
Show Answer
Answer
# (a) Generate certs
# CA
openssl genrsa -out ca.key 4096
openssl req -x509 -new -nodes -key ca.key -sha256 -days 365 -out ca.crt \
  -subj "/CN=anihpj-ca"

# Server cert
openssl genrsa -out server.key 2048
openssl req -new -key server.key -out server.csr \
  -subj "/CN=api.anihpj.com"
openssl x509 -req -in server.csr -CA ca.crt -CAkey ca.key \
  -CAcreateserial -out server.crt -days 365 -sha256

# Client cert
openssl genrsa -out client.key 2048
openssl req -new -key client.key -out client.csr \
  -subj "/CN=partner-client"
openssl x509 -req -in client.csr -CA ca.crt -CAkey ca.key \
  -CAcreateserial -out client.crt -days 365 -sha256
# (b) Secret MUST contain tls.crt, tls.key, AND ca.crt
kubectl create secret generic anihpj-mtls-cert \
  --from-file=tls.crt=server.crt \
  --from-file=tls.key=server.key \
  --from-file=ca.crt=ca.crt \
  -n istio-system

# (c) Gateway with MUTUAL mode
spec:
  servers:
  - port:
      number: 443
      name: https-mutual
      protocol: HTTPS
    tls:
      mode: MUTUAL
      credentialName: anihpj-mtls-cert
    hosts:
    - "api.anihpj.com"

Without cert: TLS handshake fails immediately — "SSL_ERROR_HANDSHAKE_FAILURE_ALERT" (server requires client certificate). Untrusted cert: Server validates client cert against ca.crt — fails → connection refused (certificate verify error).

Explanation

MUTUAL TLS is the strictest Gateway TLS mode — BOTH sides must present valid certificates. The secret needs three files: tls.crt (server), tls.key (server private key), and ca.crt (CA to verify client certs). On the ICA exam, the key difference from SIMPLE is the ca.crt data key — SIMPLE doesn't need it, MUTUAL requires it. If you forget ca.crt, the Gateway cannot verify ANY client certificate, and all connections fail. This is commonly tested for B2B/partner API scenarios.

Chapter 6: VirtualService — Smart Traffic Routing

The VirtualService is Istio's traffic routing brain. It overrides Kubernetes' default round-robin and lets you control EXACTLY where each request goes — based on URI path, headers, weights, or any combination. Think of it as the GPS for your traffic: "turn left here, go 90% this way, 10% that way."

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~3 hours 🚦 Routing 🎯 ICA 30%
🎓 ICA Relevance
Traffic Management 30%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 6
  • "Route traffic by URI" = match: uri: prefix: /jobs/ in VirtualService
  • "Canary traffic split" = weight: 90 + weight: 10 on route destinations (need DestinationRule subsets)
  • "Add retries" = retries: {attempts: 3, perTryTimeout: 2s} in VirtualService http section
  • "Add timeout" = timeout: 5s — total request budget including all retries
  • "Test resilience" = fault: {delay: {percentage: {value: 50}, fixedDelay: 5s}} — inject delays
  • "Multiple match conditions" = AND within one match block, OR across separate match blocks
  • Exam hands-on: Write VirtualService with weights, retries, timeouts, and fault injection from scratch

6.1 What is a VirtualService?

A VirtualService defines WHERE traffic goes, HOW MUCH traffic, and under WHAT conditions. It works for both external traffic (via Gateway) and internal service-to-service calls within the mesh. It attaches to a Kubernetes Service by name and overrides K8s' default simple round-robin.

📖 VirtualService vs DestinationRule — The Golden Rule

VirtualService = WHERE (the GPS route — which road to take). DestinationRule = HOW (the driving rules — speed limit, lane choice). VirtualService decides the destination first, then DestinationRule applies policies to that destination.

6.2 Basic Routing — Send All Traffic to One Version

jobpost-api has v1 and v2 running simultaneously. By default, Kubernetes alternates between both. A VirtualService can pin ALL traffic to v1:

yaml — Basic route: 100% to v1 

apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-vs
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - jobpost-api              # ← the K8s Service name
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v1           # ← 100% to v1 (subset defined in DestinationRule)

6.3 Weight-Based Traffic Splitting (Canary Deployments)

Deploy jobpost-api:v2 and gradually shift traffic: 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%. Roll back instantly by changing one number — zero downtime.

yaml — Canary: 90% v1, 10% v2 

spec:
  hosts:
  - jobpost-api
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v1           # ← stable version
      weight: 90             # ← 90% to v1
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v2           # ← canary version
      weight: 10             # ← 10% to v2
⚖️ CANARY DEPLOYMENT — Gradual Traffic Shift
Phase 1
v1: 100% · v2: 0%
Deploy v2, no traffic
Phase 2
v1: 95% · v2: 5%
Smoke test v2
Phase 3
v1: 75% · v2: 25%
Monitor metrics
Phase 4
v1: 50% · v2: 50%
Halfway confidence
Phase 5
v1: 0% · v2: 100%
Full migration ✅

6.4 Header-Based Routing (A/B Testing)

Route users to different versions based on HTTP headers. Premium users see the new UI, everyone else stays on stable.

Incoming request to jobpost-api — Which version?
Header: x-user-type = premium?
✅ jobpost-api:v2 (new UI)
No matching header?
✅ jobpost-api:v1 (stable)

🧪 A/B Testing Use Cases for anihpj

Header / ConditionRoute To
x-user-type: premiumjobpost-api:v2 (premium UI)
x-canary: "true"jobpost-api:v2 (canary)
x-internal-user: "1"skills-svc:v2 (beta features)
user-agent: contains "Mobile"jobpost-api mobile-optimized
cookie: session=stagingall services:v2 staging
yaml — Header-based A/B testing for jobpost-api 

spec:
  http:
  - match:                          # ← check this FIRST
    - headers:
        x-user-type:
          exact: premium            # ← if header matches "premium"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v2                  # ← premium users → v2 (new UI)
  - route:                          # ← default (no header match)
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v1                  # ← everyone else → v1 (stable)
💡 Match Order Matters!

VirtualService evaluates rules top-to-bottom, first match wins. Always put specific matches (header-based) BEFORE the default catch-all route. If you reverse the order, the default route matches first and your A/B test never triggers.

6.5 URI-Based and Path-Based Routing

Route different URL paths to different microservices — all through ONE VirtualService:

yaml — Path-based routing for all anihpj services 

spec:
  http:
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: /jobs/        # → jobpost-api:8000
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: /users/       # → user-service:8080
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: /skills/      # → skills-service:8090
    route:
    - destination:
        host: skills-service
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: /locations/   # → location-service:8070
    route:
    - destination:
        host: location-service
  - route:                    # ← default (no match)
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api     # fallback

6.6 Retries — Automatic Failure Recovery

Without Istio: if user-service returns a 503, Django fails immediately. With Istio retries: Envoy automatically retries up to 3 times before giving up. No code changes needed.

🔄 RETRY TIMELINE — How Envoy Absorbs Transient Failures
❌ WITHOUT Retries

T=0ms: jobpost-api → user-service → 503
T=1ms: Envoy returns 503 to Django
T=2ms: Django returns 500 to browser
💥 User sees error (service recovers 200ms later)

✅ WITH Retries (attempts: 3)

T=0ms: Attempt 1 → 503 ❌
T=100ms: Attempt 2 → 503 ❌
T=200ms: Attempt 3 → 200 ✅
T=201ms: Django gets success
✅ User never sees an error!

yaml — Retry configuration 

spec:
  http:
  - retries:
      attempts: 3              # ← try up to 3 times
      perTryTimeout: 2s        # ← each attempt has 2s timeout
      retryOn: 5xx,gateway-error,reset  # ← what errors trigger retry
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service
⚠️ Retries Are Only Safe for Idempotent Requests

GET requests are idempotent — retrying is safe. POST/PUT/DELETE are NOT — retrying could double-charge a payment or create duplicate records. Always set retryOn to only retry on safe status codes.

6.7 Timeouts — Don't Wait Forever

Without timeouts, a slow service can block Django threads for 30-60 seconds, causing thread pool exhaustion. Istio timeouts return a fast 504, protecting your application.

⏱️ TIMEOUT — Fast Failure vs Slow Hang
❌ Without Timeout

T=0s: Django calls slow skills-service
T=10s: Still waiting...
T=30s: Still waiting... thread BLOCKED
T=60s: Django default timeout → error
💥 Thread pool exhaustion!

✅ With Timeout (3s)

T=0s: Django calls skills-service
T=3s: Envoy returns 504 Gateway Timeout
T=3.01s: Django gets fast 504 → graceful degradation
✅ Thread pool protected!

💡 Combined Retry + Timeout Pattern

timeout: 10s (total budget) + retries: attempts: 3, perTryTimeout: 3s = 3 attempts × 3s = 9s max, under the 10s budget. ✅ Always ensure retries × perTryTimeout < timeout.

6.8 Fault Injection — Testing Resilience

Intentionally inject failures to verify your timeouts, retries, and circuit breakers actually work. Two types: delay (slow responses) and abort (error responses).

🐌
Delay Injection

"Delay 30% of requests to user-service by 7 seconds"

Tests: Do timeouts fire correctly? Does the app degrade gracefully? Are retries configured properly?

💥
Abort Injection

"Return HTTP 500 for 10% of requests to auth-service"

Tests: Does the circuit breaker open? Does the fallback mechanism work? Are error messages user-friendly?

yaml — Fault injection examples 

# Delay: 30% of requests delayed by 7 seconds
spec:
  http:
  - fault:
      delay:
        fixedDelay: 7s
        percentage:
          value: 30
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service
---
# Abort: 10% of requests return HTTP 500
spec:
  http:
  - fault:
      abort:
        httpStatus: 500
        percentage:
          value: 10
    route:
    - destination:
        host: auth-service

🚦 VirtualService — Visual Recap

🎯
Match
URI / headers / query
⚖️
Weight
Canary 90/10 splits
🔄
Retries
Auto-recover from 5xx
⏱️
Timeout
Fast failure, no hangs
🐌
Delay
Test timeout handling
💥
Abort
Test circuit breakers

6.X Route Matching Precedence — How Envoy Chooses Routes

When multiple VirtualService rules could match a request, Envoy uses specific precedence. Understanding this prevents "my route isn't working" confusion.

🏆 ROUTE MATCHING PRECEDENCE — First Match Wins (Top→Bottom)
1
Exact Match

uri: exact: /health
Matches ONLY /health

2
Prefix Match

uri: prefix: /api/v1/
Matches /api/v1/jobs, /api/v1/users...

3
Regex Match

uri: regex: ^/api/v[12]/.*
Matches v1 or v2 API paths

4
Default Route

No match block — catch-all.
Must be LAST in the list.

⚠️ Order Matters!

VirtualService http routes are evaluated top to bottom. Always put most specific rules FIRST. If the catch-all (no match) route comes before a specific route, the specific route will NEVER be reached. This is the #1 VirtualService misconfiguration.

📋 COMMON VIRTUALSERVICE PATTERNS — Copy-Paste Templates

PatternUse CaseKey Fields
Weight-based canary90% v1, 10% v2weight: 90 + weight: 10
Header-based A/BPremium users → v2headers: {x-user-type: exact: premium}
Path-based routing/api/* → service-Auri: prefix: /api/
Retry + Timeout3 retries, 5s totalretries: {attempts:3, perTryTimeout:1s} + timeout: 5s
Fault injectionTest resiliencefault: {delay: {percentage: {value:50}, fixedDelay: 5s}}
Traffic mirrorTest v2 silentlymirror: {host: svc, subset: v2}

6.X+1 Sidecar CRD — Controlling Proxy Scope and Performance

The Sidecar CRD lets you fine-tune what each Envoy proxy can reach. By default, every Envoy knows about EVERY service in the mesh — which means huge memory usage in large deployments (500+ services). The Sidecar resource lets you limit this scope for performance and security.

⚠️
Without Sidecar Resource

Every Envoy proxy knows about ALL services in the mesh. 500 services = 500 clusters in EACH Envoy's config. Memory per sidecar: 300MB+. Config push time: seconds per change. Not scalable.

✅
With Sidecar Resource

Limit each Envoy to only the services it actually calls. jobpost-api only needs: user-service, skills-service, auth-service. Config size: 90% smaller. Memory: 50MB. Production-ready.

yaml — Sidecar: Limit jobpost-api's scope to only 3 services 
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: Sidecar
metadata:
  name: default
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  egress:
  - hosts:
    - "./user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"   # Only these
    - "./skills-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local" # services are
    - "./auth-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"   # visible to Envoy
    - "istio-system/*"                            # Always allow istiod
💡 When to Use Sidecar on the ICA Exam

If a question mentions "reduce memory usage per sidecar" or "limit which services a pod can reach," the answer is Sidecar CRD. In production with 100+ services, Sidecar resources are mandatory for performance. Without them, each Envoy's config grows unboundedly, causing high memory usage and slow config propagation.

6.X+2 Application vs Mesh-Level Failure Recovery — Avoid Conflicts

A critical concept from the official docs: your application code and Istio handle failures independently. They don't know about each other — and this can cause conflicts.

⚠️ CONFLICT EXAMPLE — App Timeout vs Istio Timeout
Your Django code:
  requests.get('http://user-service/users/', timeout=2)  # ← 2s timeout

Istio VirtualService:
  timeout: 3s        # ← 3s timeout (applied by Envoy)
  retries:
    attempts: 1
    perTryTimeout: 2s

What ACTUALLY happens:
  T=0s    Django sends request
  T=2s    Django's OWN timeout fires → raises Timeout exception
          Envoy NEVER gets to apply its retry!
          The 3s VirtualService timeout is useless.

🔑 Golden Rule: App timeout MUST be >= (Istio retries × perTryTimeout) + buffer
   Fix: Set Django timeout to 5s (or remove it and let Istio handle it)
⚠️ Important: Fault Injection + Retries/Timeout

The official Istio docs state: fault injection configuration cannot be combined with retry or timeout configuration on the same VirtualService. If you need fault injection AND retries, create separate VirtualServices or test them separately. This is a known Istio limitation — not a bug.

📝 Chapter 6 — ICA Practice Questions (Hands-On Lab Scenarios)

Q1
You have two Deployments of jobpost-api labeled version: v1 and version: v2. A DestinationRule already defines subsets v1 and v2. Task: Write a VirtualService that routes 90% of traffic to v1 and 10% to v2. Verify the split works by running a 100-request test and counting which version served each response. Write the bash one-liner that counts version distribution. Then explain what happens if you change the DestinationRule's subset labels AFTER traffic is flowing.
Show Answer
Answer
# VirtualService with weighted routing
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-vs
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - jobpost-api
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v1
      weight: 90
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v2
      weight: 10

# Apply
kubectl apply -f jobpost-api-vs.yaml
# Verify: 100 requests, count v1 vs v2 responses
for i in $(seq 1 100); do
  kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- \
    curl -s http://jobpost-api/version
done | sort | uniq -c
# Expected output: ~90 v1, ~10 v2

Changing DestinationRule subsets live: The VirtualService destinations update immediately when the DestinationRule changes — Envoy re-evaluates the subset labels and redistributes endpoints. Pods that no longer match the labels lose traffic immediately. Pods newly matching get traffic within seconds via EDS updates.

Explanation

Weighted routing is the #1 ICA exam scenario. The VirtualService defines the split; the DestinationRule defines the subsets. They MUST exist together. Weights are relative — 90:10 is the same as 9:1 or 900:100. The sum doesn't need to equal 100. On the ICA exam, always create the DestinationRule FIRST (or in the same kubectl apply). If the VirtualService references a non-existent subset → 503 for ALL traffic to that destination. Verify with a loop test — don't trust that config looks right, prove it works with real requests.

Q2
Your jobpost-api calls user-service but sometimes gets 504 Gateway Timeout. Task: Diagnose the issue — write a VirtualService with a 3-second timeout and 2 retries for calls to user-service. Verify using istioctl proxy-config route that the timeout is reflected in Envoy's route config. Then test with a curl that simulates a slow endpoint (add a 4-second delay to user-service). Explain the retry budget formula: (retries × perTryTimeout) must be less than the total timeout.
Show Answer
Answer
# VirtualService with timeout + retries
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: user-service-vs
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - user-service
  http:
  - timeout: 3s
    retries:
      attempts: 2
      perTryTimeout: 1s
      retryOn: 5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local

# Verify in Envoy
istioctl proxy-config route deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj | grep -A5 timeout
# ✅ Shows "timeout: 3.000s" and retry policy with per_try_timeout: 1.000s
# Retry budget formula:
# (attempts × perTryTimeout) + initial_attempt_time < timeout
# (2 × 1s) + 1s = 3s ≤ 3s ✅  — All retries fit within budget
# (3 × 1s) + 1s = 4s > 3s ❌  — Third retry exceeds timeout
Explanation

Timeout + retries is a VirtualService concern. On the ICA exam, calculate the budget carefully: timeout ≥ perTryTimeout × (attempts + 1). The "+1" is for the initial attempt (which isn't a "retry"). If the timeout fires before all retries complete, Envoy returns 504. The retryOn field specifies which error conditions trigger a retry — always include 5xx, connect-failure, and refused-stream for production readiness. Use istioctl proxy-config route to verify the config was pushed to Envoy.

Q3
You need to test how the anihpj frontend handles backend failures BEFORE a real outage happens. Task: Write a VirtualService that injects HTTP 503 errors on 50% of requests to user-service. Verify the 503s are occurring using a curl loop. Then modify the VirtualService to inject a 3-second delay on 25% of requests instead. Explain why fault injection cannot be combined with retry/timeout configuration on the SAME VirtualService (documented Istio limitation).
Show Answer
Answer
# Fault injection: Abort 503 on 50% of requests
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: user-service-vs-fault
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - user-service
  http:
  - fault:
      abort:
        httpStatus: 503
        percentage:
          value: 50
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local

# Verify: Half should fail with 503
for i in $(seq 1 20); do
  kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -- \
    curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" http://user-service/health
done
# Modified: 3s delay on 25% of requests
  - fault:
      delay:
        fixedDelay: 3s
        percentage:
          value: 25
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local

Why fault + retry/timeout can't coexist on one VS: Istiod rejects the combination. Fault injection bypasses Envoy's upstream cluster logic — retries and timeouts operate at a different layer. If you need both, use SEPARATE VirtualServices (one for fault testing, one for production routing with retries). Toggle between them by applying/removing the fault VS.

Explanation

Fault injection is a TESTING tool — use it to validate that your retries, timeouts, and circuit breakers work. Abort injection tests retry logic (does the caller retry on 503?). Delay injection tests timeout logic (does the 3s timeout fire before the 5s delay?). On the ICA exam, start with low percentages (10%) and increase gradually. Always remove fault injection VS before re-enabling production VS with retries. The Istio limitation is enforced at apply time — istioctl analyze will flag the conflict.

Q4
You need to route traffic based on URI path: /jobs/* → jobpost-api, /users/* → user-service, /admin/* → admin-service. Task: Write a single VirtualService that does URI-based routing for ALL three paths. Use prefix matching. Include a catch-all default route that sends unmatched requests to a homepage service. Then verify routing with curl calls to each path. Explain why prefix (not exact) is needed for paths with wildcards.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: anihpj-path-routing
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - api.anihpj.com
  gateways:
  - anihpj-gateway
  http:
  # URI matches are evaluated TOP-TO-BOTTOM, first match wins
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: "/admin/"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: admin-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: "/jobs/"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: "/users/"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
  # Default: catch-all (no match block = everything else)
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: homepage.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Verify routing
kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- curl -s http://api.anihpj.com/jobs/123
# → jobpost-api
kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- curl -s http://api.anihpj.com/users/456
# → user-service

Why prefix, not exact: prefix: "/jobs/" matches /jobs/, /jobs/123, /jobs/123/details. exact: "/jobs/" matches ONLY /jobs/ — nothing with subpaths. For REST APIs with resource IDs in the path, always use prefix.

Explanation

URI-based routing is the core of API gateway functionality. Rules are evaluated TOP-TO-BOTTOM, first match wins. Put the most specific prefixes first, catch-all last. On the ICA exam: use prefix for path-based microservice routing, exact for specific endpoints like /health or /metrics, regex for complex patterns. The default route (no match block) is essential — it catches everything that doesn't match specific rules.

Q5
Your QA team needs to test v2 of jobpost-api without affecting regular users. Task: Configure header-based routing: if the request has header x-qa-testing: true, route to subset v2. All other traffic goes to v1. Verify using curl with and without the header. Then explain how AND logic works within a single match block vs OR logic across multiple match blocks.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-qa-routing
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - jobpost-api
  http:
  # Specific match (QA testing) — evaluated FIRST
  - match:
    - headers:
        x-qa-testing:
          exact: "true"      # AND: could add header conditions here
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v2           # QA goes to v2
  # Default route — catches everything else
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v1           # Everyone else stays on v1
# Verify QA header → v2
kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- \
  curl -s -H "x-qa-testing: true" http://jobpost-api/version
# ✅ Returns "v2"

# Verify without header → v1
kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- \
  curl -s http://jobpost-api/version
# ✅ Returns "v1"

AND vs OR: Conditions within ONE match block are ANDed. Separate - match: blocks are ORed. Top-to-bottom, first match wins.

Explanation

Header-based routing enables targeted testing without affecting users. On the ICA exam, this is a common A/B testing pattern. Order matters: put specific match blocks FIRST (QA header), catch-all LAST. Multiple conditions in one match block are ANDed — BOTH header AND URI must match. Multiple match blocks are ORed — ANY can trigger the route. This allows complex rules like: "if (x-qa-testing: true AND uri prefix: /beta) → v2, else → v1".

Q6
You configured a VirtualService with timeouts and retries for the user-service. Task: Write the complete YAML combining all resilience features: (a) 3 retries with 2s perTryTimeout, (b) overall 8s timeout, (c) fault injection of 500ms delay on 10% of requests to test timeout behavior. Explain that you CANNOT put fault + retry/timeout in the SAME VirtualService — show the workaround of splitting into two VirtualServices. Demonstrate how to test with the fault VS applied, then remove it and apply the retry VS for production.
Show Answer
Answer
# VirtualService 1: Production with retries + timeout
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: user-service-prod
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - user-service
  http:
  - timeout: 8s
    retries:
      attempts: 3
      perTryTimeout: 2s
      retryOn: 5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream,reset
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local

# Budget check: (3 retries × 2s) + 2s first attempt = 8s ≤ 8s ✅
# VirtualService 2: Fault injection for testing (apply separately)
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: user-service-test
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - user-service
  http:
  - fault:
      delay:
        fixedDelay: 500ms
        percentage:
          value: 10
    route:
    - destination:
        host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Workflow:
# 1. Delete prod VS: kubectl delete vs user-service-prod -n anihpj
# 2. Apply test VS: kubectl apply -f user-service-test.yaml
# 3. Run tests, verify fault injection in Kiali
# 4. Delete test VS: kubectl delete vs user-service-test -n anihpj
# 5. Apply prod VS: kubectl apply -f user-service-prod.yaml
Explanation

This is the Istio limitation: fault injection and retry/timeout CANNOT share a VirtualService. The workaround is two VSes — one for testing (fault only), one for production (retry/timeout only). On the ICA exam, you'll need to demonstrate this pattern. The workflow is: remove prod VS → apply test VS → test → remove test VS → apply prod VS. Never have both active simultaneously. The retryOn conditions: 5xx (server errors), connect-failure (TCP connection), refused-stream (connection refused), reset (connection reset).

Q7
You need to route API traffic based on version prefix in the URI: /api/v1/* → v1 pods, /api/v2/* → v2 pods, /api/v3/* → v3 pods. Task: Write the VirtualService using THREE match blocks with prefix. Verify routing with 3 curl calls. Then rewrite using a SINGLE match block with regex — capture the version from the URI and explain why it's more concise. Explain when regex is overkill and prefix is preferred.
Show Answer
Answer
# Approach 1: Three prefix match blocks (explicit)
http:
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/api/v3/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
      subset: v3
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/api/v2/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
      subset: v2
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/api/v1/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
      subset: v1
# Approach 2: Single regex match (concise)
http:
- match:
  - uri:
      regex: "^/api/v([123])/.*"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
      subset: v1  # Note: regex can't dynamically pick subset

When regex is overkill: For 2-3 services, use prefix — it's clearer and faster to debug. For complex pattern matching (e.g., matching any version number /api/v\d+/.*), use regex. Prefix is also faster at Envoy's L7 filter level. On the ICA exam, prefer prefix for clarity unless the task specifically requires regex.

Explanation

This question tests both approaches. Prefix is the workhorse — clear, fast, and easy to debug in istioctl proxy-config route. Regex is powerful but overkill for simple cases. Key limitation: Envoy's regex matching doesn't support dynamic subset selection based on captured groups — you still need one match block per destination. On the ICA exam, use prefix unless you need to match variable patterns like /api/v[0-9]+/.* for all numeric versions.

Q8
You have a VirtualService with URI-based routing. Requests to /admin/debug should be rejected with HTTP 403 for security. Task: Add a match block for /admin/debug that returns a direct 403 response using fault: abort — no routing to any service. Place it BEFORE other match blocks. Verify with curl that /admin/debug returns 403 while /admin/stats returns normally. Explain the security implications of using VirtualService for access control vs AuthorizationPolicy.
Show Answer
Answer
http:
# Block debug endpoint FIRST (before routing rules)
- match:
  - uri:
      exact: "/admin/debug"
  fault:
    abort:
      httpStatus: 403
      percentage:
        value: 100
  # No route block — request is aborted before reaching any service
# Normal admin routing
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/admin/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: admin-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Verify
kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- \
  curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" http://api.anihpj.com/admin/debug
# → 403

kubectl exec deploy/source -n anihpj -- \
  curl -s http://api.anihpj.com/admin/stats
# → Normal response from admin-service

VS vs AuthorizationPolicy: VirtualService abort is a blunt tool — it blocks at L7 routing but doesn't enforce RBAC. AuthorizationPolicy with JWT or mTLS is the proper security mechanism. Use VS abort for blocking misconfigured/malicious paths, use AuthorizationPolicy for access control.

Explanation

Aborting with no route is a clean pattern for blocking unwanted paths. The request never reaches any service — Envoy returns the status code directly. On the ICA exam, this is a quick way to block specific endpoints. However, for real access control, use AuthorizationPolicy (which integrates with JWT, OAuth, and mTLS). VS abort = L7 routing decision, AuthorizationPolicy = actual security enforcement. They complement but don't replace each other.

Q9
You need to rewrite the request path: external clients call /api/v2/users/123 but your service expects /users/123 (no version prefix). Task: Write a VirtualService that uses rewrite to strip /api/v2 from the URI before forwarding. Verify with curl that the service receives the rewritten path. Also add a rewrite for /api/legacy → /v1. Explain WHERE the rewrite happens (at the Envoy sidecar level — the service never sees the original path).
Show Answer
Answer
http:
# Rewrite: strip /api/v2 prefix
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/api/v2/"
  rewrite:
    uri: "/"                    # Replaces matched prefix with "/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Request: /api/v2/users/123 → forwarded as: /users/123

# Rewrite: /api/legacy → /v1
- match:
  - uri:
      prefix: "/api/legacy/"
  rewrite:
    uri: "/v1/"                 # Replaces matched prefix with "/v1/"
  route:
  - destination:
      host: user-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
# Request: /api/legacy/users/123 → forwarded as: /v1/users/123

Where rewrite happens: In the SIDECAR Envoy's HTTP connection manager, AFTER route matching but BEFORE forwarding to the upstream. The service never sees /api/v2 — it receives /users/123.

Explanation

URI rewriting is essential for API versioning. External clients use versioned paths (/api/v2/...), but services are version-agnostic. The rewrite rule transforms the path BEFORE it reaches the service. This is a VirtualService-only feature — it doesn't exist in DestinationRule. On the ICA exam, remember: rewrite replaces the MATCHED PREFIX with the uri value. If you match prefix: "/api/v2/" and rewrite to "/", the result is /users/123 (the prefix is replaced with "/").

Q10
Your VirtualService has URI matching, header matching, fault injection, retries, timeouts, and URL rewrite — all for the same host. The order of rules matters critically. Task: Write a complete VirtualService for jobpost-api that demonstrates correct rule ordering: (1) security block (abort 403 for /admin/debug), (2) specific rewrite rule, (3) header-based A/B test routing, (4) weighted canary split, (5) catch-all default with retries and timeout. Place the rules in the CORRECT order and explain what happens if you reverse the order.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-complete
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - jobpost-api
  http:
  # 1️⃣ SECURITY: Block debug endpoint FIRST
  - match:
    - uri:
        exact: "/admin/debug"
    fault:
      abort:
        httpStatus: 403
        percentage:
          value: 100

  # 2️⃣ REWRITE: Transform legacy paths
  - match:
    - uri:
        prefix: "/api/legacy/jobs"
    rewrite:
      uri: "/jobs"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v1

  # 3️⃣ HEADER-BASED: A/B test for internal testers
  - match:
    - headers:
        x-canary:
          exact: "enabled"
    route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v2

  # 4️⃣ WEIGHTED SPLIT: Canary deployment (catch-all traffic)
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v1
      weight: 90
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
        subset: v2
      weight: 10

Wrong order consequences: If the weighted split (#4) is placed BEFORE header-based routing (#3), ALL traffic (including x-canary headers) would be split 90/10 — the header rule would NEVER be reached. If security block (#1) is last, /admin/debug requests would match earlier routing rules and reach the service. TOP-TO-BOTTOM, FIRST MATCH WINS.

Explanation

Rule ordering is the most critical VirtualService concept on the ICA exam. The evaluation is: top-to-bottom, first match wins, STOP evaluating. The correct order is: most specific FIRST (security blocks, exact matches), then header/routing rules, then weighted splits LAST (as catch-all). If the weighted split is first, it swallows ALL traffic and no other rule ever fires. This is a common exam trap: "Why is my header-based routing not working?" → Because the weighted split is above it in the VirtualService.

Chapter 7: DestinationRule — Traffic Policies and Load Balancing

The DestinationRule defines HOW traffic is sent AFTER VirtualService decides WHERE it goes. It configures load balancing algorithms, circuit breakers, connection pools, TLS mode, and named subsets for canary routing.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2.5 hours ⚙️ Policies 🎯 ICA 30%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the VirtualService + DestinationRule relationship
  • ✅ Define subsets for canary routing
  • ✅ Choose the right load balancing algorithm per service
  • ✅ Configure circuit breakers to prevent cascading failures
  • ✅ Set connection pool limits to protect slow services
🎓 ICA Relevance
Traffic Management 30%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 7
  • "Define subsets for canary" = subsets: [{name: v1, labels: {version: v1}}, {name: v2, labels: {version: v2}}]
  • "Circuit breaker" = outlierDetection: {consecutive5xxErrors: 5, interval: 30s, baseEjectionTime: 60s}
  • "Load balancing" = loadBalancer: {simple: LEAST_CONN} — ROUND_ROBIN, LEAST_CONN, RANDOM, CONSISTENT_HASH
  • "mTLS for internal" = trafficPolicy.tls.mode: ISTIO_MUTUAL — uses auto SPIFFE certs
  • "Connection pool" = connectionPool: {tcp: {maxConnections: 100}, http: {http1MaxPendingRequests: 50}}
  • "Subset without matching labels" = 503 No Healthy Upstream — most common canary failure
  • Exam hands-on: Write DestinationRule with subsets, circuit breaker, and load balancer from scratch

7.1 What is a DestinationRule?

DestinationRule applies post-routing policies. VirtualService picks the destination (which service, which subset), then DestinationRule applies: load balancing, TLS mode, circuit breaker settings, and connection pool limits.

🔀 VIRTUALSERVICE + DESTINATIONRULE — How They Work Together
1
Request

GET /jobs/ arrives

2
VirtualService

WHERE? → match /jobs/* → jobpost-api, subset v1 (90%)

3
DestinationRule

HOW? → subset v1 = pods with version:v1 label

4
Load Balancer

LEAST_CONN → pick pod with fewest active connections

5
Circuit Breaker

Check: has pod failed 5 times? → CLOSED ✅

6
mTLS

ISTIO_MUTUAL → encrypt connection to pod

7
Pod

Request delivered to jobpost-api-v1-abc

📖 The Golden Rule

VirtualService = WHERE (the GPS route). DestinationRule = HOW (the driving rules — speed limit, lane choice, seatbelt). VS decides the destination; DR applies policies to that destination.

7.2 Defining Subsets (Required for Canary)

Subsets link Pod labels to route targets. The VirtualService says "send to subset v1" and DestinationRule resolves "subset v1 = pods with label version: v1".

🏷️ SUBSET RESOLUTION — Labels → Subsets → Routing
Pod: jobpost-v1-abc

labels:
app: jobpost-api
version: v1

✅ Matches subset v1

Pod: jobpost-v2-xyz

labels:
app: jobpost-api
version: v2

✅ Matches subset v2

⚠️ COMMON MISTAKE

If pods are missing the version label, the subset resolves to ZERO endpoints → 503 errors! Always add labels: version: v1 to ALL your Deployments.

7.3 Load Balancing Algorithms

AlgorithmBehaviorBest Foranihpj Use
ROUND_ROBINEach pod in turnUniform workloadsskills-service
LEAST_CONNFewest active connectionsVariable-duration requestsjobpost-api
RANDOMRandom selectionVery fast, statelessauth-service
CONSISTENT_HASHSame user → same podSession affinity / cachinguser-service
PASSTHROUGHBypass Envoy LBCustom load balancing—

🎯 Decision Guide — Which Algorithm for Each anihpj Service?

ServiceAlgorithmWhy
jobpost-apiLEAST_CONNMix of fast/slow Django views
user-serviceCONSISTENT_HASHPer-user cache warmth (hash on x-user-id header)
skills-serviceROUND_ROBINUniform stateless queries
auth-serviceRANDOMVery fast, stateless, uniform latency
search-serviceLEAST_CONNVariable search complexity per query

7.4 Circuit Breaking (Outlier Detection)

A circuit breaker prevents cascading failures. When a service starts failing, the breaker opens and stops ALL requests to it — giving it time to recover.

🔌 CIRCUIT BREAKER STATE MACHINE
CLOSED
Normal

Requests flow ✅
Counting errors...

5 errs
→ OPEN

All requests fail immediately ❌
Service protected

30s
→ HALF-OPEN

One test request allowed
If OK → CLOSED

yaml — Circuit breaker configuration 

trafficPolicy:
  outlierDetection:
    consecutiveGatewayErrors: 5   # after 5 errors, eject host
    interval: 30s                 # check every 30 seconds
    baseEjectionTime: 30s         # keep ejected for 30 seconds
    maxEjectionPercent: 50        # never eject more than 50% of hosts

7.5 Connection Pool Settings

Control how many concurrent connections Envoy allows per service. This prevents a slow service from being overwhelmed by queued requests consuming Envoy memory.

🏊 CONNECTION POOL — Request Queuing Visualization
Incoming requests ──▶ [=====QUEUE=====|100 max|] ──▶ skills-service

Scenario A (normal):  50 requests → queue at 50% — ✅ OK
Scenario B (busy):   100 requests → queue FULL → 503 returned
Scenario C (spike):  200 requests → 100 queued, 100 fast-rejected

WHY: Without limit, 1000 requests queue up consuming memory
until the service falls over. With limit: fast 503 → caller can retry.

7.6 Complete DestinationRule for anihpj

yaml — Complete DestinationRule for jobpost-api 

apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: DestinationRule
metadata:
  name: jobpost-api-dr
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  host: jobpost-api
  trafficPolicy:
    loadBalancer:
      simple: LEAST_CONN          # smart load balancing
    connectionPool:
      tcp:
        maxConnections: 100
      http:
        http1MaxPendingRequests: 50
        http2MaxRequests: 100
    outlierDetection:             # circuit breaker
      consecutiveGatewayErrors: 5
      interval: 30s
      baseEjectionTime: 30s
      maxEjectionPercent: 50
    tls:
      mode: ISTIO_MUTUAL          # enforce mTLS
  subsets:
  - name: v1
    labels:
      version: v1                 # stable version
  - name: v2
    labels:
      version: v2                 # canary version

7.X Load Balancing Algorithm Decision Guide — Choose the Right One

🧠 LB ALGORITHM SELECTION — Decision Tree
What type of workload?
Variable-duration requests?
→ LEAST_CONN
Pick pod with fewest active connections
Best for: jobpost-api, skills-service
Need sticky sessions?
→ CONSISTENT_HASH
Same user → same pod (cache warmth)
Best for: user-service (per-user cache)
Uniform latency, stateless?
→ ROUND_ROBIN
Simple, predictable distribution
Best for: auth-service, health checks

⚖️ LOAD BALANCER COMPARISON — All 5 Algorithms

AlgorithmHow It WorksBest Whenanihpj Service
ROUND_ROBINEach pod in turn, evenlyUniform workloads, equal pod sizeauth-service
LEAST_CONNPod with fewest active connectionsVariable request durationjobpost-api, skills-service
RANDOMRandom pod selectionVery fast, stateless, uniformhealth endpoints
CONSISTENT_HASHHash key → same pod alwaysSticky sessions, warm cachesuser-service
PASSTHROUGHBypass Envoy LB entirelyCustom client-side LB—

📝 Chapter 7 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
What DestinationRule field enables circuit breaking, and what are the required sub-fields?
Show Answer
Answer

outlierDetection with: consecutiveGatewayErrors (error threshold), interval (evaluation window), and baseEjectionTime (how long the host stays ejected). When a host exceeds the error threshold within the interval, it's ejected from the load balancing pool (circuit OPENS).

Explanation

Circuit breaking in Istio follows the standard three-state pattern: CLOSED (normal), OPEN (blocked), HALF-OPEN (probing). On the ICA exam, outlierDetection always lives in DestinationRule — never in VirtualService. Key fields: consecutive5xxErrors (for HTTP), consecutiveGatewayErrors (for all errors including connection failures), interval (default 10s), baseEjectionTime (default 30s).

Q2
How are subsets defined in DestinationRule and how does VirtualService resolve them to actual pods?
Show Answer
Answer

Subsets are defined in DestinationRule with name and labels that match pod labels. VirtualService references them as subset: v1 in route destinations. Envoy resolves a subset by finding all pods whose labels match the subset's labels AND whose Service selector matches app: jobpost-api. Without matching pod labels → zero endpoints → 503.

Explanation

The full resolution chain: VirtualService says "route to subset v1" → DestinationRule says "subset v1 = labels {version: v1}" → Envoy queries Kubernetes API for pods matching {app: jobpost-api, version: v1} → returns pod IPs. If the version label is missing from pods, the subset resolves to zero endpoints, causing 503 for ALL traffic. This is the #1 canary deployment debugging issue.

Q3
What is the difference between LEAST_CONN and CONSISTENT_HASH load balancing, and which anihpj services should use each?
Show Answer
Answer

LEAST_CONN: Picks the pod with fewest active connections. Best for variable-duration requests (jobpost-api, skills-service). Prevents one slow pod from getting overloaded while others sit idle.

CONSISTENT_HASH: Routes the same hash key to the same pod every time. Best for sticky sessions with warm caches (user-service). Same user always hits the same pod, maximizing cache hit rate.

Explanation

LEAST_CONN is the default for good reason — it handles heterogenous workloads best. CONSISTENT_HASH is specialized for session affinity. On the ICA exam, the question usually hints at the use case: "variable-duration requests" = LEAST_CONN, "sticky sessions" or "cache warmth" = CONSISTENT_HASH, "stateless uniform" = ROUND_ROBIN, "fast stateless" = RANDOM.

Q4
Describe what happens at each state of the circuit breaker: CLOSED → OPEN → HALF-OPEN → CLOSED.
Show Answer
Answer

CLOSED: Normal state — all requests forwarded to the upstream host. Error counter increments on failures.

OPEN: Error threshold exceeded — ALL requests fail IMMEDIATELY with 503. Envoy doesn't even try to connect. Lasts for baseEjectionTime (e.g., 30s).

HALF-OPEN: Ejection time expired — ONE test request is allowed through. If it succeeds → CLOSED. If it fails → OPEN again (timer resets).

Explanation

This three-state model is fundamental to understanding Istio resilience. The key insight: during OPEN state, Envoy returns immediate 503 WITHOUT making a network call — this is what saves thread pools and prevents cascading failures. Retries (VirtualService) are different — they DO make network calls. On the ICA exam, know which state each scenario describes.

Q5
What is maxEjectionPercent in circuit breaker configuration and why is it critical for production?
Show Answer
Answer

It limits the percentage of upstream hosts that can be ejected simultaneously. maxEjectionPercent: 50 means "never eject more than 50% of the hosts." Without this safety valve, ALL hosts could be ejected by circuit breakers simultaneously = complete outage. Default is 100% (no limit) — always set it explicitly in production.

Explanation

This is a critical safety configuration. Imagine 3 replicas of user-service — all 3 experience a brief network blip simultaneously. Without maxEjectionPercent, all 3 get ejected and user-service is completely down even though all pods are healthy. With maxEjectionPercent: 33, only 1 gets ejected and 2 remain serving. On the ICA exam, this is the "blast radius" control for circuit breakers.

Q6
When should you use ISTIO_MUTUAL vs SIMPLE vs DISABLE TLS mode in DestinationRule?
Show Answer
Answer
ModeUse Case
ISTIO_MUTUALMesh-internal services — auto SPIFFE certs, recommended for production
SIMPLEExternal services — standard one-way TLS (e.g., api.stripe.com)
DISABLEService already handles TLS, or testing without encryption
Explanation

The DestinationRule TLS mode controls the CLIENT side (outbound Envoy). The PeerAuthentication controls the SERVER side (inbound Envoy). Both must agree for mTLS to work. On the ICA exam: for mesh-internal, use ISTIO_MUTUAL + PeerAuthentication STRICT. For external APIs, use SIMPLE + a ServiceEntry. If you see 503 errors between services, check that TLS modes on both sides are compatible.

Q7
What is the relationship between connection pool settings and circuit breakers in DestinationRule?
Show Answer
Answer

Connection pool: Proactive throttling — limits concurrent connections/requests to prevent overwhelming a service BEFORE it fails.

Circuit breaker: Reactive protection — ejects hosts AFTER they start failing to prevent cascading failures.

They complement each other: connection pool protects the target service (capacity guard), circuit breaker protects the caller (failure guard).

Explanation

Think of it as: connection pool = "don't send more than X requests at once" (rate limiting at the connection level), circuit breaker = "stop sending requests to hosts that are returning errors" (health-based ejection). Both live in trafficPolicy in DestinationRule. On the ICA exam, when asked about "preventing overload" = connection pool. When asked about "preventing cascading failures" = circuit breaker.

Q8
What happens when ALL endpoints for a DestinationRule host are unhealthy or ejected? How do you prevent this?
Show Answer
Answer

Envoy returns 503 No Healthy Upstream for ALL requests. Prevention: Set maxEjectionPercent to less than 100% (e.g., 50%) to ensure some hosts always remain in the pool even if others are ejected. Also use health checks to distinguish truly dead hosts from ones having transient errors.

Explanation

"No Healthy Upstream" is a common error in Istio. It means Envoy's endpoint list for the destination is empty — all hosts are either ejected by circuit breaker, failed health checks, or never existed (missing pod labels). On the ICA exam, when debugging 503s between services: (1) check istioctl proxy-config endpoint, (2) verify pod labels match subset labels, (3) check circuit breaker isn't ejecting all hosts.

Q9
What is the difference between connection pool limits (proactive) and circuit breaker (reactive) in DestinationRule?
Show Answer
Answer

Connection pool: Proactive — limits max concurrent connections/requests BEFORE problems occur. Protects service capacity. Fields: tcp.maxConnections, http.http1MaxPendingRequests, http.http2MaxRequests.

Circuit breaker: Reactive — ejects hosts AFTER errors are detected. Protects against failing services. Fields: outlierDetection.consecutive5xxErrors, baseEjectionTime.

Explanation

The mental model: connection pool = "I'll only send 100 requests at a time" (capacity guard). Circuit breaker = "If 5 consecutive requests fail, I'll stop sending for 30 seconds" (health guard). Both are in trafficPolicy. The ICA exam may ask you to write the YAML for either — know which fields belong to which concept.

Q10
How does DestinationRule TLS mode interact with PeerAuthentication, and what happens if they conflict?
Show Answer
Answer

DestinationRule TLS mode tells the CLIENT Envoy how to initiate the connection (ISTIO_MUTUAL, SIMPLE, DISABLE). PeerAuthentication tells the SERVER Envoy what to accept (PERMISSIVE, STRICT, DISABLE). If DR says SIMPLE but PA says STRICT → 503 errors (client sends plain HTTP, server requires mTLS). Both sides must be compatible.

Explanation

This is the TLS handshake contract: the client (DR) decides what to send, the server (PA) decides what to accept. Production best practice: DR = ISTIO_MUTUAL + PA = STRICT = full mTLS. Migration best practice: start with PERMISSIVE (accepts both), then switch to STRICT after all services have sidecars. On the ICA exam, mismatched TLS configs are a common debugging scenario causing 503s.

Chapter 8: ServiceEntry — Connecting to External Services

By default, Istio (in REGISTRY_ONLY mode) blocks ALL external traffic. Your anihpj app needs to call Stripe for payments, SendGrid for emails, and S3 for file uploads. ServiceEntry registers these external services so Istio knows about them — and can apply policies to them.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🌍 External 🎯 ICA 30%
🎓 ICA Relevance
Traffic Management 30%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapter 8
  • "Register external API" = ServiceEntry with hosts:, location: MESH_EXTERNAL, resolution: DNS
  • "No ServiceEntry in REGISTRY_ONLY" = 502 Bad Gateway — external calls BLOCKED
  • "Apply policies to external" = Once registered, create VirtualService/DestinationRule targeting the ServiceEntry host
  • "Switch to REGISTRY_ONLY" = Create ServiceEntries FIRST, then switch mode — never switch first
  • "Egress Gateway" = Central exit point — single source IP, central audit, PCI compliance
  • "Wildcards" = hosts: ["*.amazonaws.com"] — register entire domains
  • Exam hands-on: Write ServiceEntry YAML from scratch + attach VirtualService timeout

8.1 The Problem: Calling External APIs from Within the Mesh

🌍 ISTIO OUTBOUND TRAFFIC MODES
ALLOW_ANY

All external traffic passes uncontrolled. No policies.

REGISTRY_ONLY

Only registered ServiceEntry hosts allowed. Recommended for production.

Egress Gateway

All external traffic through one Pod. Central audit.

8.2 ServiceEntry YAML — Registering External Services

yaml — ServiceEntry for anihpj external services 

# Stripe payments API
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: ServiceEntry
metadata:
  name: stripe-payments
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - api.stripe.com
  ports:
  - number: 443
    name: https
    protocol: HTTPS
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL       # tells Istio this is external
  resolution: DNS
---
# SendGrid email API
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: ServiceEntry
metadata:
  name: sendgrid-email
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - api.sendgrid.com
  ports:
  - number: 443
    name: https
    protocol: HTTPS
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL
  resolution: DNS
---
# AWS S3 (wildcard for all regions)
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: ServiceEntry
metadata:
  name: aws-s3
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - "*.amazonaws.com"           # wildcard
  ports:
  - number: 443
    name: https
    protocol: HTTPS
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL

8.3 Egress Gateway — Controlled Exit from the Mesh

🔀 EGRESS GATEWAY — Centralized Outbound Traffic
❌ WITHOUT

jobpost-api → direct → api.stripe.com
user-service → direct → api.stripe.com
3 different source IPs, no audit

✅ WITH Egress Gateway

All Pods → egressgateway → external
✅ One fixed source IP
✅ Central audit log
✅ Block all by stopping gateway

8.X External Service Security Patterns — Production Best Practices

🛡️
Pattern 1: Whitelist-Only

Switch to REGISTRY_ONLY mode. Create ServiceEntries ONLY for APIs you need. All other external traffic is blocked by default. This is zero-trust for egress.

istioctl install --set meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode=REGISTRY_ONLY -y
🚪
Pattern 2: Egress Gateway

Route ALL external traffic through a single egress gateway pod. Single source IP for firewall rules. Central audit. Block all egress by stopping one pod.

Gateway(type:egress) → VirtualService → ServiceEntry
🔄 EXTERNAL TRAFFIC POLICY DECISION — Which Mode for Production?
Production security requirements?
Basic: allow everything
→ ALLOW_ANY (default, NOT recommended)
Standard: whitelist-only
→ REGISTRY_ONLY + ServiceEntries
✅ Recommended for most production
Strict: full audit + single IP
→ REGISTRY_ONLY + Egress Gateway
✅ Required for PCI/HIPAA compliance

📝 Chapter 8 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How do you register an external API (api.stripe.com) in Istio so services can call it?
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: ServiceEntry
metadata:
  name: stripe-payments
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - api.stripe.com
  ports:
  - number: 443
    name: https
    protocol: HTTPS
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL
  resolution: DNS
Explanation

ServiceEntry adds external services to Istio's internal service registry. Once registered, you can apply VirtualService (retries, timeouts) and DestinationRule (TLS mode, circuit breaker) policies to external APIs just like internal services. The location: MESH_EXTERNAL field tells Istio this host is outside the mesh. On the ICA exam, you'll need to write ServiceEntry YAML from scratch for external services.

Q2
What happens when a service tries to call an external API without a ServiceEntry in REGISTRY_ONLY mode?
Show Answer
Answer

External calls are BLOCKED with HTTP 502. Istio doesn't know about the host → no route exists → request fails. The Envoy sidecar has no endpoint for the external host in its cluster table.

Explanation

This is the fundamental security model: in production, you should use REGISTRY_ONLY mode so that ONLY explicitly registered external services can be called. In the demo profile, the default is ALLOW_ANY (all external traffic allowed). On the ICA exam, when asked to secure external access, switch to REGISTRY_ONLY and create ServiceEntries for required external APIs.

Q3
What is the purpose of an Egress Gateway, and how does it differ from a direct ServiceEntry approach?
Show Answer
Answer

An Egress Gateway forces ALL external traffic through a single Envoy pod (istio-egressgateway). Benefits: one fixed source IP (simpler firewall rules), central audit logging, ability to block all egress by stopping one pod, consistent policy enforcement. Direct ServiceEntry allows each sidecar to connect directly to external APIs.

Explanation

Think of it as: ServiceEntry = permission slip (allows external calls), Egress Gateway = controlled exit door (routes all external calls through one point). Egress Gateway is required for PCI compliance and strict security environments. On the ICA exam, when the question mentions "audit trail" or "single IP for firewall rules," the answer is Egress Gateway.

Q4
What is the difference between location: MESH_EXTERNAL and location: MESH_INTERNAL in ServiceEntry?
Show Answer
Answer

MESH_EXTERNAL: Service is outside the mesh (e.g., api.stripe.com, s3.amazonaws.com). Used for cloud APIs and external services.

MESH_INTERNAL: Service is inside the mesh but outside Kubernetes (e.g., a VM running a database registered with WorkloadEntry). Used for services in the mesh's trust domain but not on K8s.

Explanation

For cloud APIs like Stripe, SendGrid, AWS S3 — always use MESH_EXTERNAL. MESH_INTERNAL is for services you want to include in the mesh's mTLS and policy framework but that run on VMs or bare metal. On the ICA exam, if the service is a cloud API URL, it's MESH_EXTERNAL.

Q5
What are the three resolution modes in ServiceEntry, and when should you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

DNS: Standard DNS lookup — most common for external APIs (Stripe, SendGrid). Envoy resolves the hostname via DNS.

STATIC: Hardcoded IP addresses — use when you know the exact IPs and want to avoid DNS latency.

NONE: Assumes the outbound connection already routes correctly — use when the destination is already reachable without Istio intervention.

Explanation

For 90% of external API cases, use DNS resolution. STATIC is for internal IPs that don't change. NONE is for special cases where the network already handles routing. On the ICA exam, you'll almost always use resolution: DNS for external services.

Q6
After creating a ServiceEntry for api.stripe.com, can you apply VirtualService retries, timeouts, and circuit breakers to Stripe calls?
Show Answer
Answer

YES! Once a ServiceEntry is created, the external service appears in Istio's internal registry. You can create VirtualService (retries, timeouts, fault injection) and DestinationRule (TLS mode, circuit breaker, connection pool) resources targeting the ServiceEntry host — exactly like an internal service.

Explanation

This is the power of ServiceEntry — it makes external services first-class citizens in the mesh. You can add a 5s timeout to Stripe calls, set up circuit breaking for SendGrid, and configure consistent TLS for all external APIs. On the ICA exam, this is a key concept: ServiceEntry enables full Istio policy control over external traffic.

Q7
How do you switch Istio from ALLOW_ANY to REGISTRY_ONLY outbound traffic mode, and what precaution must you take?
Show Answer
Answer
istioctl install --set meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode=REGISTRY_ONLY -y

WARNING: Always create ServiceEntries for ALL required external services FIRST, then switch modes. Switching first will immediately block all unregistered external traffic — potentially breaking your application.

Explanation

This is a critical operational sequence. The migration path: (1) audit all external API calls your services make, (2) create ServiceEntries for each one, (3) test with ALLOW_ANY still enabled, (4) switch to REGISTRY_ONLY, (5) verify nothing breaks. On the ICA exam, the correct answer always involves creating ServiceEntries BEFORE switching modes.

Q8
What is the difference between MESH_EXTERNAL and MESH_INTERNAL location in ServiceEntry, and which do you use for cloud APIs?
Show Answer
Answer

MESH_EXTERNAL: Service is completely outside the mesh (cloud APIs like Stripe). Istio cannot enforce mTLS or AuthorizationPolicy on these services — they're outside the trust domain.

MESH_INTERNAL: Service is inside the mesh's trust domain but not on Kubernetes (e.g., VMs with WorkloadEntry). Istio CAN enforce mTLS and policies on these services.

Explanation

The key distinction: MESH_INTERNAL services are part of your mesh and receive SPIFFE identities; MESH_EXTERNAL services do not. For public cloud APIs (Stripe, SendGrid, Twilio, AWS), always use MESH_EXTERNAL. For your own services running on VMs that you want to include in the mesh, use MESH_INTERNAL with WorkloadEntry.

Q9
After creating a ServiceEntry for api.stripe.com, write the VirtualService YAML that adds a 5-second timeout to all Stripe API calls.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: stripe-timeout
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  hosts:
  - api.stripe.com
  http:
  - timeout: 5s
    route:
    - destination:
        host: api.stripe.com
Explanation

This demonstrates that external services become full mesh citizens after ServiceEntry registration. You use the EXACT same VirtualService/DestinationRule patterns for both internal and external services. The only prerequisite is that the host exists in Istio's registry — which ServiceEntry provides. On the ICA exam, this is a common hands-on task.

Q10
What is the Egress Gateway, when is it required, and what three resources do you need to configure it?
Show Answer
Answer

The Egress Gateway forces all external traffic through a single Envoy pod. Required for: PCI compliance, strict security environments, centralized audit logging, single source IP for firewall rules.

Three required resources: (1) Gateway (type: egress, selector: istio: egressgateway), (2) VirtualService (routes external traffic to the egress gateway), (3) ServiceEntry (registers the external host).

Explanation

The Egress Gateway is the outbound counterpart of the Ingress Gateway. Just as all external traffic ENTERS through one point, all external traffic EXITS through one point. This gives you a single choke point for monitoring, auditing, and blocking outbound traffic. On the ICA exam, configuring an Egress Gateway is an advanced task involving all three resource types.

🚦 TRAFFIC MANAGEMENT QUICK REFERENCE — Which Resource Do I Need?
What are you trying to do?
Accept external traffic into the mesh?
🌐 Gateway
Route by URI path, header, or weight?
🚦 VirtualService
Set load balancer, circuit breaker, or TLS?
📋 DestinationRule
Call an external API (Stripe, S3)?
🌍 ServiceEntry
Add retries or timeouts?
🚦 VirtualService (http section)
Define canary subsets (v1/v2)?
📋 DestinationRule (subsets)

📝 Part 5 Summary — Traffic Management (30% of ICA)

  • Gateway: External entry — hosts, ports, TLS. MUST pair with VirtualService.
  • VirtualService: WHERE to route — URI, headers, weights, retries, timeouts, faults.
  • DestinationRule: HOW to send — subsets, LB, circuit breaker, TLS mode, connection pool.
  • ServiceEntry: Register external APIs — Stripe, SendGrid, S3.
  • Canary Pattern: VS weights + DR subsets = gradual shift with instant rollback.

🚦 Traffic Management — Master Recap

🌐
Gateway
Host/Port/TLS
🚦
VirtualService
URI/Weight routing
📋
DestRule
LB/CB/mTLS
🌍
ServiceEntry
External APIs
⚖️
Canary
90/10 split
🔄
Retries
3 attempts
⏱️
Timeout
Fast 504
🔌
CircuitBr
Outlier detect

💼 Part 5 — Daily Work Takeaways

  • Write Gateway YAML to accept external traffic into the mesh
  • Write VirtualService YAML for path-based and weight-based routing
  • Do canary deployments: deploy v2 → route 5% → monitor → ramp up
  • Roll back instantly by changing one YAML weight — no downtime
  • Add retries + timeouts to VS (no code changes!)
  • Configure circuit breakers via DR outlierDetection
  • Set up intelligent LB: LEAST_CONN for variable workloads, CONSISTENT_HASH for sticky sessions
  • Register external APIs (Stripe, SendGrid, S3) via ServiceEntry
  • Remember the pattern: VS = WHERE, DR = HOW, GW = WHO enters

📊 Part 6: Observability — See Everything in Your Mesh Chapters 9-11

Chapter 9: Metrics — Measuring Every Request

Istio gives you automatic observability — metrics, traces, and access logs for every request, with ZERO code changes. This chapter covers the three pillars of observability and how Istio makes them available without touching your Django code.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2.5 hours 📊 Observability 🎯 ICA 15%
🎓 ICA Relevance
Observability 15%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapters 9-11
  • "Check RED metrics" = Grafana Istio Service Dashboard
  • "Find why a request is slow" = Jaeger distributed tracing
  • "See live topology" = Kiali service graph
  • "Validate Istio config" = istioctl analyze -n <ns>

9.1 The Observability Pillars: Metrics, Traces, Logs

Observability has three pillars that work together to give you a complete picture of your system. Istio provides Metrics and Traces automatically — zero code changes needed in your Django app.

🔺 THE THREE PILLARS — How They Work Together
📊
METRICS

"WHAT is wrong"

  • 847 req/s
  • 2.1% error rate
  • p99: 234ms

Tool: Prometheus + Grafana

✅ Istio: AUTO (no code)

🔭
TRACES

"WHERE is it slow"

  • Full call chain
  • Each hop timing
  • Bottleneck span

Tool: Jaeger

✅ Istio: AUTO (fwd headers)

📝
LOGS

"WHY is it slow"

  • Error messages
  • Stack traces
  • DB query: "1,089ms"

Tool: EFK / Loki

⚠️ Istio: partial (access logs)

🔍 REAL-WORLD DIAGNOSIS — How the Three Pillars Work Together
1
Metrics

2
Traces

Jaeger: DB query in skills-svc = 1,100ms!

3
Logs

"Missing index on user_id" → ADD INDEX

✅
Fixed!

p99 drops to 87ms. Root cause found!

9.5 Custom Metrics & Telemetry API

Beyond RED metrics, you can add custom dimensions and labels using Istio's Telemetry API — without touching application code.

yaml — Telemetry: Add custom metric dimensions 

apiVersion: telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1
kind: Telemetry
metadata:
  name: anihpj-metrics
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  metrics:
  - providers:
    - name: prometheus
    overrides:
    - match:
        metric: REQUEST_COUNT
      tagOverrides:
        job_category:
          value: request.headers["x-job-category"]
        user_region:
          value: request.headers["x-user-region"]

9.6 Access Logs — Auditing Every Request

From the official docs: Istio can generate access logs for every request flowing through the mesh. Unlike metrics (aggregate data) and traces (sampled requests), access logs provide a full record of every single request — source, destination, latency, status, headers, and more.

📝 ISTIO ACCESS LOG — Anatomy of a Log Entry
[2026-06-02T09:31:27.360Z] "GET /api/jobs/123 HTTP/1.1" 200 - "-" 0 135 5 2 "-" 
"curl/7.60.0" "d209e46f-9ed5-9b61-bbdd-43e22662702a" 
"jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:8000" 
"10.244.1.5:8000" 
inbound|8000|http|jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local - 

BREAKDOWN:
  Timestamp: 2026-06-02T09:31:27.360Z
  Method:    GET /api/jobs/123
  Protocol:  HTTP/1.1
  Status:    200 (success)
  Latency:   5ms (request) + 2ms (response) = 7ms total
  Bytes:     0 sent, 135 received
  Client:    curl/7.60.0
  Trace ID:  d209e46f-9ed5-9b61-bbdd-43e22662702a  ← links to Jaeger!
  Target:    jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:8000
  Direction: inbound (receiving traffic)
  TLS:       mutual_tls (if mTLS enabled)
yaml — Telemetry API: Configure access logs 
apiVersion: telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1
kind: Telemetry
metadata:
  name: anihpj-access-logs
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  accessLogging:
  - providers:
    - name: envoy
    # Optional: filter to only log errors
    # filter:
    #   expression: response.code >= 400
💡 Access Logs vs Metrics vs Traces — When to Use Each

Metrics: "What percentage of requests are failing?" (aggregate). Traces: "Why was THIS specific request slow?" (sampled). Access Logs: "What exactly happened at 9:31:27 AM?" (every request, full detail). Access logs are essential for security audits and debugging specific errors.

9.7 Control Plane Metrics — Monitoring istiod Health

From the official docs: Istio's control plane (istiod) also exports self-monitoring metrics. These are separate from service-level metrics — they tell you about Istio's own health, not your services' health.

🕹️ KEY CONTROL PLANE METRICS — What to Monitor

Metric CategoryWhat It Tells YouExample
xDS Push LatencyHow fast istiod pushes config to Envoypilot_xds_push_time
Config Sync StatusHow many proxies are SYNCED vs STALEpilot_proxy_convergence_time
CSR HandlingCertificate signing request ratespilot_total_xds_rejects
Memory/CPUistiod resource consumptionprocess_resident_memory_bytes
Connected ProxiesNumber of Envoy sidecars connectedpilot_xds gauge
Config RejectsInvalid CRDs rejected by Galleypilot_total_xds_rejects
📖 Proxy-Level vs Service-Level vs Control-Plane Metrics

Proxy-level: Raw Envoy stats (cluster health, connection errors, SSL errors). Service-level: Istio's standard metrics (istio_requests_total, istio_request_duration_ms). Control-plane: istiod's own health metrics. On the ICA exam, service-level metrics are the most commonly tested — they're what you see in Grafana dashboards.
