📝 Chapter 9 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the three RED metrics Istio captures automatically?
Show Answer
Answer

Rate (requests/sec), Errors (4xx/5xx percentage), Duration (latency p50/p95/p99). Measured by Envoy for every request with zero code changes.

Explanation

RED metrics are the foundation of Istio observability. They answer: Is the service getting traffic? (Rate) Is it responding correctly? (Errors) Is it fast enough? (Duration). Every Envoy sidecar emits these metrics on port 15090 for Prometheus to scrape. No Prometheus client library needed in your Django code.

Q2
How does Prometheus collect metrics from Istio, and on what port?
Show Answer
Answer

Prometheus scrapes :15090/metrics from every Envoy sidecar every 15 seconds. Istio configures Envoy to emit metrics in Prometheus format automatically — no application instrumentation needed.

Explanation

Port 15090 is the Prometheus metrics endpoint on every Envoy proxy. It's separate from data plane ports (15001 outbound, 15006 inbound). Prometheus discovers targets via Kubernetes pod annotations. The metrics format is standard Prometheus exposition format, so any Prometheus-compatible scraper works.

Q3
What Grafana dashboard shows per-service RED metrics, and how do you access it?
Show Answer
Answer

The Istio Service Dashboard, accessed via istioctl dashboard grafana. It shows client/server RPS, success rate, latency percentiles, and incoming/outgoing request breakdowns per service.

Explanation

Istio ships with 4 Grafana dashboards: Control Plane (istiod health), Mesh (global overview), Service (per-service RED — your daily dashboard), Workload (per-deployment comparison). The Service Dashboard is the most used — it's where you check if a specific service is healthy.

Q4
What port do Envoy proxies expose metrics on, and how is it different from data plane ports?
Show Answer
Answer

Port 15090. This is the Prometheus metrics endpoint, separate from 15001 (outbound data) and 15006 (inbound data). Prometheus scrapes GET :15090/metrics from every sidecar.

Explanation

Three Envoy ports to know for the ICA exam: 15001 = outbound (app traffic intercepted by iptables), 15006 = inbound (traffic from other services), 15090 = metrics (Prometheus scraping endpoint). Always separate data plane from management plane ports.

Q5
Write the PromQL query that returns the 99th percentile latency for jobpost-api.
Show Answer
Answer
histogram_quantile(0.99, 
  istio_request_duration_ms_bucket{
    destination_service="jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"
  })
Explanation

histogram_quantile calculates percentiles from histogram buckets — it's a Prometheus built-in. The destination_service label uses the FQDN format: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local. On the ICA exam, you may need to construct PromQL queries for specific metrics.

Q6
How do you add a custom metric dimension (e.g., job_category) to Istio metrics without changing code?
Show Answer
Answer

Create a Telemetry resource with tagOverrides that extracts values from request headers.

apiVersion: telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1
kind: Telemetry
metadata:
  name: anihpj-metrics
spec:
  metrics:
  - providers: [{name: prometheus}]
    overrides:
    - match: {metric: REQUEST_COUNT}
      tagOverrides:
        job_category:
          value: request.headers["x-job-category"]
Explanation

The Telemetry API is Istio's way of customizing observability without touching application code. You can add custom labels (tags) to any standard Istio metric by extracting values from HTTP headers, gRPC metadata, or request properties. On the ICA exam, this is the answer when asked about "customizing metrics."

Q7
What's the difference between Mesh Dashboard and Service Dashboard in Grafana?
Show Answer
Answer

Mesh Dashboard = global overview of ALL services (total requests, global error rate). Service Dashboard = drill-down into ONE specific service (per-service RED metrics, incoming/outgoing breakdown). Use Mesh for capacity planning, Service for debugging.

Explanation

Think of Mesh Dashboard as the "helicopter view" and Service Dashboard as the "magnifying glass." Mesh shows you which services are misbehaving; Service shows you WHY a specific service is misbehaving. Both are pre-built by Istio — no dashboard creation needed.

Q8
How do you verify that Prometheus is successfully scraping Istio metrics?
Show Answer
Answer

Open Prometheus dashboard (istioctl dashboard prometheus) and query istio_requests_total. Alternatively, check kubectl port-forward svc/prometheus 9090 -n istio-system → Status → Targets — all Istio targets should show UP.

Explanation

If Prometheus targets show DOWN, check: (1) Envoy sidecars are running (pod shows 2/2), (2) Network policies allow port 15090, (3) Prometheus config has the correct scrape annotations. This is a common ICA troubleshooting scenario.

Q9
Write the PromQL query that shows the error rate percentage for jobpost-api.
Show Answer
Answer
sum(rate(istio_requests_total{
  destination_service="jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local",
  response_code=~"5.*"}[5m]))
/ 
sum(rate(istio_requests_total{
  destination_service="jobpost-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"}[5m]))
* 100
Explanation

This divides 5xx requests by total requests over the last 5 minutes. The rate() function converts counters to per-second rates; dividing gives the error percentage. Use response_code=~"5.*" for server errors or response_code=~"[45].*" for all errors.

Q10
How do you configure Istio to add a custom label from request headers to metrics?
Show Answer
Answer

Create a Telemetry resource with tagOverrides mapping header values to metric labels.

spec:
  metrics:
  - providers: [{name: prometheus}]
    overrides:
    - match: {metric: REQUEST_COUNT}
      tagOverrides:
        user_region:
          value: request.headers["x-user-region"]
Explanation

This is how you customize Istio's built-in metrics — no code changes in your services. The Telemetry API supports: REQUEST_COUNT, REQUEST_DURATION, REQUEST_SIZE, RESPONSE_SIZE, and TCP-level metrics. Custom tags appear as additional labels in Prometheus, which you can then filter and group by in Grafana.

Chapter 10: Distributed Tracing — Follow Any Request Across All Services

A single GET /jobs/ triggers 5+ service calls. Without tracing, you have no idea which service is the bottleneck. Jaeger distributed tracing shows every hop, every duration, in one Gantt chart.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔭 Tracing 🎯 ICA 15%

10.1 What is Distributed Tracing?

🔍
Trace

The entire journey of ONE request across all services. Contains multiple spans linked by a Trace ID.

📏
Span

One unit of work — one service call. Has start time, duration, and parent-child relationships.

🔭 DISTRIBUTED TRACE — anihpj GET /jobs/ (342ms total)
Trace ID: 7f3e9a12b4c06d8e    Duration: 342ms

▶ jobpost-api (GET /jobs/)                                 0ms → 342ms
  ▶ user-service (GET /users/42/)                         12ms → 87ms
    ▶ auth-service (POST /validate-token/)               15ms → 45ms
  ▶ skills-service (GET /skills/?ids=1,2,3)               92ms → 287ms
    ← 195ms ← SLOW! This is the bottleneck ⚠️
  ▶ location-service (GET /locations/7/)                 295ms → 320ms

🔍 skills-service call took 195ms — investigate database query!

10.2 How Istio Enables Tracing (Almost Automatically)

Envoy automatically injects tracing headers into every request. Your Django code just needs to forward them between services.

python — Django header propagation (required!) 

# Add this to your Django views that call other services:
ISTIO_HEADERS = [
    'x-request-id',
    'x-b3-traceid',        # ← links all spans together
    'x-b3-spanid',
    'x-b3-parentspanid',
    'x-b3-sampled',
    'x-b3-flags',
]

def get_jobs(request):
    # Forward tracing headers to propagate trace context:
    headers = {
        h: request.META.get('HTTP_' + h.upper().replace('-', '_'))
        for h in ISTIO_HEADERS
        if request.META.get('HTTP_' + h.upper().replace('-', '_'))
    }
    users = requests.get('http://user-service/users/', headers=headers)
    skills = requests.get('http://skills-service/skills/', headers=headers)
    # ...
⚠️ CRITICAL — No Header Propagation = Broken Traces

If you don't forward the x-b3-* headers, each service call becomes a separate, disconnected trace. You won't see the full call chain — just isolated spans. This is the #1 tracing mistake.

10.3 Jaeger — Visualizing Traces

Jaeger is the default tracing backend. Open with istioctl dashboard jaeger. Key features: trace search, service dependency graph, latency histograms.

💡 How to Find Bottlenecks in Jaeger

1. Open Jaeger → Search → sort by Longest Duration. 2. Click the slowest trace. 3. Look at the Gantt chart — the widest bar is the bottleneck. 4. Click that span to see tags: HTTP method, URL, status code. 5. Cross-reference with logs at that timestamp.

10.4 Tracing Sampling Rate

Tracing every request adds overhead. Istio defaults to 1% sampling — trace 1 out of 100 requests.

🟢 Dev: 100%

Trace everything. Useful for debugging locally.

🟡 Prod: 1-5%

Enough for statistical analysis without overhead. 100% is too expensive in production.

🔭 Tracing — Visual Recap

🔍
Trace
Full request journey
📏
Span
One service call
🆔
Trace ID
Links all spans
📋
Headers
x-b3-traceid etc.
🔭
Jaeger
Gantt chart view
🎯
Sampling
1% prod, 100% dev

📝 Chapter 10 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How does Istio enable distributed tracing without code changes?
Show Answer
Answer

Envoy automatically injects tracing headers (x-b3-traceid, x-b3-spanid) into every request. Your app only needs to propagate these headers when making outbound HTTP calls to other services.

Explanation

Istio provides tracing infrastructure automatically — Envoy creates span contexts and injects Zipkin B3 headers. The ONE thing your code must do: forward these headers. Without forwarding, each service call creates a separate disconnected trace. On the ICA exam, header propagation is the key concept — Envoy does 80% of the work, your code does the remaining 20%.

Q2
What happens if you don't forward Istio tracing headers in your Django code?
Show Answer
Answer

Each service call becomes a separate, disconnected trace. Jaeger shows isolated spans instead of one complete call chain. You cannot see which service caused which downstream call, making bottleneck identification impossible.

Explanation

This is the #1 tracing mistake. Without header propagation, you lose the parent-child span relationship. The trace becomes fragmented — each service shows as an independent trace with its own Trace ID. On the ICA exam, when a question shows isolated spans in Jaeger, the answer is always "application is not forwarding tracing headers."

Q3
What's the default tracing sampling rate, why is it 1%, and how do you change it?
Show Answer
Answer

Default: 1% (1 in 100 requests). Tracing adds CPU/memory overhead — 1% provides statistical significance without excessive cost. Change via Telemetry resource: spec.tracing[0].randomSamplingPercentage: 100.0 for 100% in development.

Explanation

Sampling is a trade-off: higher sampling = better debugging data but more overhead. Production: 1-5% (statistical significance). Development: 100% (debug everything). The ICA exam may ask you to configure different sampling rates for different environments via the Telemetry API.

Q4
What are the five x-b3-* tracing headers and what does each do?
Show Answer
Answer
HeaderPurpose
x-b3-traceidUnique ID for the entire trace — links all spans
x-b3-spanidUnique ID for this specific span
x-b3-parentspanidID of the parent span — creates hierarchy
x-b3-sampledWhether this trace should be recorded (1 or 0)
x-b3-flagsDebug flags (e.g., force sampling)
Explanation

These follow the Zipkin B3 propagation format — an industry standard. All major tracing backends (Jaeger, Zipkin, Lightstep, Datadog) understand B3 headers. The "B3" name comes from "BigBrotherBird" — Zipkin's original codename. On the ICA exam, know that these headers create the trace hierarchy: traceid = whole journey, spanid = this step, parentspanid = who called me.

Q5
What happens if the tracing sampling rate is set to 0%?
Show Answer
Answer

No traces are collected at all. Envoy still injects headers but marks them as x-b3-sampled: 0. Jaeger shows no traces. Use this only to completely eliminate tracing overhead when not needed.

Explanation

Sampling can be changed live without restarting pods — it's pushed via xDS. 0% is useful when you have a known issue and want to verify that tracing overhead is NOT the cause. On the ICA exam, the answer to "no traces appearing" could be sampling=0% or header propagation issues.

Q6
How do you find the slowest request in Jaeger to identify a performance bottleneck?
Show Answer
Answer

Jaeger UI → Search → sort by Longest Duration → click the slowest trace → look at the Gantt chart. The widest horizontal bar is the bottleneck span. Click it to see: HTTP method, URL, status code, and timing breakdown.

Explanation

Jaeger's Gantt chart is the most powerful debugging tool in your Istio arsenal. Each span is a horizontal bar — the wider the bar, the longer that service call took. Nested bars show parent-child call relationships. On the ICA exam, you may be shown a trace diagram and asked to identify the bottleneck.

Q7
What is the relationship between Trace ID, Span ID, and Parent Span ID?
Show Answer
Answer

Trace ID: Same across ALL spans in one request — like a package tracking number. Span ID: Unique per span — like individual scan events. Parent Span ID: Points to the calling span — creates the tree hierarchy showing who-called-whom.

Explanation

The three IDs together form a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of the request journey. Trace ID groups all spans; Span ID identifies each; Parent Span ID creates the edges. This is the Zipkin/Dapper data model. On the ICA exam, understanding this hierarchy is essential for interpreting trace diagrams.

Q8
What propagation format do Istio's tracing headers follow, and why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

The Zipkin B3 propagation format — an industry standard. Headers: x-b3-traceid, x-b3-spanid, x-b3-parentspanid, x-b3-sampled, x-b3-flags. Compatibility with Jaeger, Zipkin, Lightstep, Datadog, and others.

Explanation

B3 is the most widely adopted tracing header format. Istio also supports the W3C Trace Context standard (traceparent header). On the ICA exam, know that Istio uses B3 by default but can be configured for W3C. The key point: these are standard headers — any compliant tracing backend works with Istio.

Q9
How do you configure the tracing sampling percentage using the Telemetry API?
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1
kind: Telemetry
metadata:
  name: mesh-default
  namespace: istio-system
spec:
  tracing:
  - randomSamplingPercentage: 1.0  # 1%
Explanation

The Telemetry API is the modern way to configure tracing in Istio. Apply it to istio-system for mesh-wide defaults, or to a specific namespace for per-namespace overrides. On the ICA exam, this is preferred over the old meshConfig approach. Values: 0.0-100.0.

Q10
In Jaeger, your trace shows only one span instead of the full call chain. What's wrong and how do you fix it?
Show Answer
Answer

Your application code is NOT forwarding the x-b3-* tracing headers between service calls. Fix: extract the headers from the incoming request and add them to every outbound HTTP call.

# Django fix example
ISTIO_HEADERS = ['x-b3-traceid','x-b3-spanid','x-b3-parentspanid','x-b3-sampled']
headers = {h: request.META.get('HTTP_'+h.upper().replace('-','_')) 
           for h in ISTIO_HEADERS}
requests.get('http://user-service/users/', headers=headers)
Explanation

This is THE most common tracing issue. The fix is always the same: forward the B3 headers. You can add a Django middleware to do this automatically for all views. On the ICA exam, when you see "broken traces" or "isolated spans" in Jaeger, the root cause is always missing header propagation.

Kiali is the "control panel" for your Istio mesh. It shows a live service topology graph, validates your Istio configuration, and even generates YAML from a UI. Think of it as Google Maps for your microservices.

Kiali provides four main capabilities: service topology graph, RED metrics visualization, Istio config validation, and YAML wizards. Open with istioctl dashboard kiali.

📝 Chapter 11 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How do you open the Kiali dashboard and what can you see there?
Show Answer
Answer

istioctl dashboard kiali. Kiali shows: service topology graph (live), RED metrics per service, Istio config validation, and YAML wizards for generating VirtualService/DestinationRule.

Explanation

Kiali is your primary visual dashboard for Istio. It's included in the demo profile. On the ICA exam, you'll use Kiali to: verify mTLS (lock icons on edges), check error rates (edge colors), validate config (red errors in Validation tab), and generate YAML via wizards.

Q2
What does a RED edge in the Kiali service graph indicate, and what should you do?
Show Answer
Answer

A RED edge means that specific call path has a high error rate (significant 4xx/5xx). Click the edge to see exact error percentage, then drill down into traces via Jaeger for that specific call path.

Explanation

Kiali's color-coded edges provide instant health visibility: 🟢 Green = healthy, 🟡 Yellow/Orange = degraded (some errors or elevated latency), 🔴 Red = high error rate. Thresholds are configurable in Kiali settings. On the ICA exam, identifying which edge is red is the first step in diagnosing microservices issues.

Q3
Can Kiali generate Istio YAML without you writing code? How?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes! Via Kiali Wizards. Right-click a service → Actions → choose Traffic Shifting, Request Routing, Fault Injection, or Traffic Suspension → Kiali generates correct VirtualService + DestinationRule YAML. You can review and apply directly from the UI.

Explanation

Kiali wizards are the fastest way to create correct Istio YAML. The wizard ensures: subsets match pod labels, weights are properly configured, hosts match the service name, and the VirtualService-DestinationRule connection is correct. On the ICA exam, you can use wizards to quickly generate YAML and then customize it.

Q4
What do the colors 🟢 🟡 🔴 mean for service nodes and edges in the Kiali graph?
Show Answer
Answer

🟢 Green: Healthy — no errors, normal latency. 🟡 Yellow/Orange: Degraded — some errors or elevated latency. 🔴 Red: High error rate — significant 4xx/5xx responses. Thresholds are configurable in Kiali settings.

Explanation

Kiali calculates health based on error rate and latency thresholds. A node (service) changes color based on its overall health; an edge (connection) changes based on that specific call path. On the ICA exam, you'll be asked to identify unhealthy services or connections by color.

Q5
What four common Istio configuration errors does Kiali's Validation tab detect?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) VirtualService referencing a subset not defined in any DestinationRule. (2) Gateway port not matching any open port on the gateway pod. (3) Conflicting VirtualService rules for the same host. (4) DestinationRule with no matching Kubernetes Service. Each error shows the exact YAML field causing the problem.

Explanation

Kiali's validation is equivalent to running istioctl analyze but presented visually. On the ICA exam, always check BOTH Kiali's Validation tab AND run istioctl analyze for comprehensive validation. Kiali catches ~80% of config errors.

Q6
How do you see detailed metrics for a specific call path (edge) between two services in Kiali?
Show Answer
Answer

Click the edge (arrow) between two services. A side panel opens showing: request rate (req/s), error percentage (%), latency distribution (p50/p90/p99), TCP connection stats, and a direct link to view traces in Jaeger for that exact call path.

Explanation

Edge-level metrics are extremely granular — they show metrics for ONE specific caller→callee relationship, not the entire service. This is how you isolate which dependency is failing. On the ICA exam, this is the most efficient way to identify which service-to-service call is causing issues.

Q7
How can you use Kiali to monitor and troubleshoot a canary deployment in progress?
Show Answer
Answer

Kiali shows v1 and v2 as separate nodes with traffic distribution percentages. You can see: what % of traffic each version receives, error rates per version, latency per version (p50/p90/p99). If v2 shows higher errors or latency — click the edge to see details, then rollback by adjusting VirtualService weights.

Explanation

Kiali is your canary dashboard. During a canary deployment (5% v2, 95% v1), you monitor both versions side-by-side. If v2's error rate spikes, you see it instantly as a red node/edge. Rollback is one YAML edit away — change weight to 0. On the ICA exam, this is the recommended monitoring approach for canary deployments.

Explanation

Use Kiali Validation as a pre-apply sanity check. In your CI/CD pipeline: apply YAML to a staging namespace → check Kiali Validation → if no errors, promote to production. On the ICA exam, if a question asks \"how to validate config before production,\" the answer involves both istioctl analyze AND Kiali Validation.

Q9

Explanation

Edge details give you everything you need to diagnose a specific service-to-service communication issue: how much traffic, is it failing, how slow is it, and a link to trace individual requests. This is the fastest path from \"there's a problem\" to \"I know exactly what's wrong.\"

🔒 Part 7: Security — Zero Trust with Istio Chapters 12-13

Chapter 12: Mutual TLS (mTLS) — Encrypting Everything

Inside a Kubernetes cluster, traffic is plain HTTP by default. Any compromised pod can sniff passwords, tokens, and PII from other services. Istio's mTLS encrypts ALL service-to-service traffic automatically — without changing a single line of code.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔒 Security 🎯 ICA 25%
🎓 ICA Relevance
Security 25%
💡 ICA Exam Tips for Chapters 12-13 (Security — 25%)
  • "Encrypt all service traffic" = PeerAuthentication mode: STRICT
  • "Restrict which service can call which" = AuthorizationPolicy
  • "Validate JWT tokens at mesh level" = RequestAuthentication
  • Remember: mTLS = identity (WHO), AuthzPolicy = permissions (WHAT)

12.1 The Security Problem Without mTLS

Without Istio, your Kubernetes cluster has three critical attack vectors open right now:

🔴 WITHOUT ISTIO — 3 Attack Vectors Open
👃
ATTACK 1: Sniffing

Rogue pod on same node runs tcpdump → captures all plain HTTP between services. JWT tokens, passwords, PII — all visible in plaintext.

🎭
ATTACK 2: Impersonation

Attacker deploys a pod named "user-service" → jobpost-api connects to the fake service → returns fake data, steals real requests.

🦶
ATTACK 3: Lateral Movement

Compromised skills-service calls user-service → exfiltrates ALL user data. Any service can call any other service.

🟢 WITH ISTIO — All 3 Attacks Blocked
🔒
DEFENSE 1: mTLS

All traffic encrypted. Rogue pod sees only binary garbage. SPIFFE certificates auto-issued by istiod.

🪪
DEFENSE 2: SPIFFE Identity

Fake service has no valid certificate. Envoy rejects: "Not spiffe://.../ns/anihpj/sa/user-service".

🚫
DEFENSE 3: AuthzPolicy

AuthorizationPolicy: ALLOW from jobpost-api ONLY → skills-service request → 403 Forbidden.

12.2 What is Mutual TLS (mTLS)?

Regular TLS: server proves identity to client (like HTTPS websites). Mutual TLS: BOTH sides prove identity to each other. Istio issues X.509 SVID certificates (SPIFFE standard) to every workload automatically.

🤝 mTLS HANDSHAKE — What Happens in Every Service Call
1
Envoy(jobpost)

"Hello, I am jobpost-api"
Presents SPIFFE cert (SVID)

2
Envoy(user)

"I am user-service"
Presents SPIFFE cert (SVID)

3
Verify

Both verify with istiod
Cert: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api

4
Encrypt

mTLS tunnel established 🔒
GET /users/42 sent encrypted

✅
Done!

Zero code change in Django!
Certs auto-rotate every 24h

📖 SPIFFE Identity Format

Each certificate contains: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api-serviceaccount. This identifies the service, not the node or IP. Even if an attacker gets the same IP, they can't get the same SPIFFE identity without the certificate.

12.3 PeerAuthentication — Configuring mTLS Mode

PeerAuthentication is the Istio CRD that controls mTLS. Three modes: PERMISSIVE (migration), STRICT (enforce), DISABLE (debug only).

yaml — PeerAuthentication for anihpj 

# Enforce STRICT mTLS for entire anihpj namespace
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: default
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT       # ALL traffic must use mTLS
                       # Plain HTTP will be REJECTED

# Per-workload: only user-service requires STRICT
---
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: user-service-mtls
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: user-service
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT
🔄 MIGRATION PATH — PERMISSIVE → STRICT
Week 1
PERMISSIVE
Accept both HTTP + mTLS
No disruption
Week 2
STRICT per namespace
Non-sidecar → 403 errors
Fix: inject sidecars
RESULT
All traffic encrypted ✅
Kiali shows lock icons

12.4 Certificate Lifecycle — How istiod Issues Certs

Every pod gets an X.509 certificate at startup from istiod's built-in CA. Certs auto-rotate every 24 hours — no manual renewal needed.

🔄 CERTIFICATE LIFECYCLE — Full Flow for anihpj
T=0
Pod Deployed

kubectl apply → Pod created

0.1s
Sidecar Injected

Webhook adds istio-proxy

0.3s
CSR Sent

Proxy → istiod:15012
spiffe://.../sa/jobpost-api

0.5s
Cert Issued

istiod validates SA → signs X.509 cert (24h expiry)

0.7s
mTLS Ready

Cert in-memory. All calls now use mTLS!

23h55m
Auto-Rotate

5min before expiry → new CSR → new cert → seamless!

💡 Cert Anatomy

Subject: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api-serviceaccount

Issuer: istiod (cluster.local root CA) · Valid: 24 hours · SANs: SPIFFE URI

Rotation: Every 24h, rotated 5min before expiry. Zero downtime. You never need to manually renew certificates.

12.5 Secure Naming — Preventing Service Impersonation

Secure naming is a critical Istio security concept from the official docs. It answers: "Is this SPIFFE identity allowed to run this service?" Without it, an attacker with a valid certificate for one service could impersonate another.

🎭 IMPERSONATION ATTACK — How Secure Naming Defeats It
ATTACK SCENARIO:
  Attacker compromises test-team's service account → gets valid SPIFFE cert
  Attacker deploys fake user-service pod with test-team's cert
  DNS spoofing redirects jobpost-api traffic to fake user-service

WITHOUT Secure Naming:  ❌ Attack succeeds
  jobpost-api Envoy sees valid SPIFFE cert → trusts it → sends data

WITH Secure Naming:     ✅ Attack BLOCKED
  jobpost-api Envoy checks: "Is spiffe://.../sa/test-team authorized
  to run user-service?"
  → istiod's secure naming map says: NO. Only spiffe://.../sa/user-service
    is authorized to run user-service.
  → Connection REJECTED — authentication fails!

🔑 Secure Naming Map (maintained by istiod):
  spiffe://.../sa/jobpost-api  → jobpost-api  ✅
  spiffe://.../sa/user-service → user-service ✅
  spiffe://.../sa/test-team    → test-service ✅
  spiffe://.../sa/test-team    → user-service ❌ NOT AUTHORIZED
💡 ICA Exam Relevance

While secure naming is automatic in Istio (istiod maintains the mapping, Envoy enforces it), understanding it helps answer questions about "why can't an attacker impersonate a service even with a valid certificate?" The answer: Secure naming — the SPIFFE identity must match the service name in istiod's registry.

12.6 PeerAuthentication Policy Hierarchy — Mesh → Namespace → Workload

From the official docs: PeerAuthentication policies follow a strict narrowest-match-wins hierarchy. Understanding this prevents configuration surprises:

ScopeSelectorApplies ToPriority
Mesh-wideNo selector, root namespaceALL workloads in meshLowest (fallback)
Namespace-wideNo selector, specific namespaceAll workloads in that namespaceMedium
Workload-specificselector.matchLabels: setOnly matching podsHighest (overrides all)
⚠️ Key Rules from Official Docs

Only ONE mesh-wide and ONE namespace-wide PeerAuthentication can exist. If you create multiple, newer ones are IGNORED. Workload-specific policies can coexist — the oldest one wins when multiple match. This is different from RequestAuthentication, where ALL matching policies are combined.

12.X mTLS Troubleshooting — Common Scenarios and Solutions

🔴 Symptom🔍 Most Likely Cause✅ Fix

New pod can't start after STRICTistiod unreachable for CSRCheck istiod pod health, port 15012 reachable
mTLS works but AuthorizationPolicy blocksNo ALLOW policy created after deny-allCreate specific ALLOW AuthorizationPolicy
Cross-namespace call failsPA STRICT in target ns, PERMISSIVE in sourceApply consistent PeerAuthentication across namespaces

📝 Chapter 12 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How do you enforce STRICT mTLS for all services in a namespace?
Show Answer
Answer

Create a PeerAuthentication resource with spec.mtls.mode: STRICT in the target namespace. All plain HTTP traffic will be rejected immediately.

Explanation

PeerAuthentication is the CRD that controls mTLS at the server (inbound) side. STRICT mode = every incoming connection MUST present a valid SPIFFE certificate. On the ICA exam, when asked to "secure service communication," the answer is PeerAuthentication STRICT.

Q2
What's the difference between PERMISSIVE and STRICT mTLS modes?
Show Answer
Answer

PERMISSIVE: Accepts both plain HTTP and mTLS — safe for migration. STRICT: Rejects ALL plain HTTP — only mTLS connections allowed. Migration path: PERMISSIVE first → verify all services have sidecars → switch to STRICT.

Explanation

PERMISSIVE is the safe default during migration — it doesn't break existing connections. STRICT enforces zero-trust. On the ICA exam, the correct migration answer is always PERMISSIVE first, then STRICT after verifying all services are mesh-enabled.

Q3
How often do SPIFFE certificates rotate, and who manages this process?
Show Answer
Answer

Every 24 hours, automatically rotated 5 minutes before expiry by istiod (built-in Citadel CA). Zero downtime, zero manual renewal. External CAs (Vault, Cert-Manager) can be plugged in if needed.

Explanation

Certificate rotation is fully automatic — you never manually renew certs. Each Envoy sends a CSR to istiod before the current cert expires. istiod validates the service account, signs a new cert, and the old cert is replaced without dropping connections. On the ICA exam, know that rotation is automatic and seamless.

Q4
What happens to existing plain HTTP traffic when you switch from PERMISSIVE to STRICT?
Show Answer
Answer

All plain HTTP requests are REJECTED immediately. Only mTLS connections succeed. Always verify ALL services have sidecars (2/2 READY) and check Kiali for lock icons on all edges BEFORE switching.

Explanation

Switching to STRICT without all services being mesh-enabled causes communication failures. The safe sequence: (1) inject sidecars into all services, (2) apply PERMISSIVE, (3) verify Kiali shows lock icons, (4) switch to STRICT. On the ICA exam, this is a common scenario.

Q5
What does a SPIFFE identity look like and what does each component mean?
Show Answer
Answer

spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api — cluster.local = trust domain, ns/anihpj = Kubernetes namespace, sa/jobpost-api = Kubernetes ServiceAccount. Identity is tied to the ServiceAccount, not the IP address.

Explanation

SPIFFE identities are cryptographic, not network-based. An attacker can spoof an IP but cannot spoof a SPIFFE certificate. On the ICA exam, understand that AuthorizationPolicy can match on SPIFFE principals to implement fine-grained access control.

Q6
Can you apply PeerAuthentication to a single workload instead of the whole namespace?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes! Use spec.selector.matchLabels: app: user-service to target only that specific deployment. Useful for gradual rollout: test STRICT on one service first, then apply namespace-wide.

Explanation

PeerAuthentication supports both namespace-wide (no selector) and workload-specific (with selector). Namespace-wide policies apply to all pods in the namespace. Workload-specific policies override namespace-wide for matching pods. On the ICA exam, you may need to apply both types.

Q7
How can you verify that mTLS is actually active between two services?
Show Answer
Answer

(1) Kiali — edges show lock icons when mTLS is active. (2) istioctl proxy-config secret deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj — shows the SPIFFE cert. (3) openssl s_client -connect <pod-ip>:15006 from another pod to see the TLS handshake.

Explanation

Three verification methods from easiest to most detailed: Kiali (visual), istioctl proxy-config (command line), openssl (raw TLS inspection). On the ICA exam, Kiali lock icons are the quickest verification method.

Q8
What happens when PeerAuthentication STRICT is applied to a namespace with non-Istio pods?
Show Answer
Answer

Non-Istio pods (no Envoy sidecar) get 403 Forbidden when communicating with pods in that namespace. They can't present valid SPIFFE certificates, so Envoy rejects their connections.

Explanation

This is the most common mTLS migration issue. Fix: inject sidecars into those pods, use PERMISSIVE temporarily, or apply STRICT only to specific workloads via selector. On the ICA exam, always check if all communicating services have sidecars before applying STRICT.

Q9
What is the certificate chain of trust in Istio's mTLS?
Show Answer
Answer

Root CA (self-signed, in istiod) → Intermediate CA (optional, multi-cluster) → Workload Certificate (SVID, per Envoy proxy). All proxies trust the same root, enabling cross-service mTLS verification.

Explanation

The root CA is auto-created during Istio installation. All Envoy proxies trust this root, so they can verify each other's certificates. In multi-cluster setups, sharing a common root CA (or chaining intermediate CAs) enables cross-cluster mTLS.

Q10
How do you integrate an external CA (like Vault or Cert-Manager) with Istio?
Show Answer
Answer

Configure istiod via IstioOperator: meshConfig.ca.externalCa with the external CA's address and TLS settings. istiod acts as a Registration Authority, forwarding CSR requests to the external CA for signing. Alternatively, mount the external CA cert and key directly.

Explanation

External CA integration is for enterprises that need to use their existing PKI infrastructure. istiod becomes a proxy — it doesn't sign certs itself but forwards CSRs. On the ICA exam, this is an advanced security topic related to compliance requirements.

Chapter 13: Authorization Policies — Who Can Call What

mTLS proves identity, but doesn't restrict access. Without AuthorizationPolicy, any service with a valid cert can call any other service. AuthorizationPolicy is Istio's Layer 7 firewall — you define exactly WHO can call WHAT, using WHICH methods, on WHICH paths.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🛡️ Access Control 🎯 ICA 25%

13.1 The Need for Service-Level Authorization

mTLS ensures the caller IS who they claim to be. But should a compromised skills-service be allowed to call user-service and read all user data? With zero-trust, the answer is NO. Every call must be explicitly authorized.

⚠️ CRITICAL — mTLS ≠ Authorization

Even with STRICT mTLS, without AuthorizationPolicy, ANY service can call ANY other service. mTLS proves identity but doesn't restrict permissions. You need BOTH: mTLS for encryption + identity, AuthorizationPolicy for access control.

13.2 AuthorizationPolicy — The Istio Firewall

AuthorizationPolicy works at Layer 7 (HTTP). You can allow/deny based on: source identity (SPIFFE), HTTP method (GET/POST/DELETE), HTTP path, headers, and source namespace.

🔍 AUTHORIZATION POLICY EVALUATION — How Istio Decides
1
Request

skills-service → GET /users/42 → user-service

2
CUSTOM?

Custom policy exists? Evaluate first

3
DENY?

Matching DENY policy? → DENY ❌

4
ALLOW?

Matching ALLOW policy? → ALLOW ✅

5
Default

No matching ALLOW → DENY ❌

Result
403

skills-service NOT in allow-list for user-service

yaml — Complete AuthorizationPolicy for anihpj 

# Step 1: Deny ALL traffic in anihpj namespace
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: deny-all
  namespace: anihpj
spec: {}                        # ← empty spec = deny everything
---
# Step 2: Allow ingress → jobpost-api (GET/POST only)
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-ingress-to-jobpost
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: jobpost-api
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals:
        - "cluster.local/ns/istio-system/sa/istio-ingressgateway"
    to:
    - operation:
        methods: ["GET", "POST"]
---
# Step 3: Allow jobpost-api → user-service (GET only)
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-jobpost-to-users
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: user-service
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals:
        - "cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"
    to:
    - operation:
        methods: ["GET"]
        paths: ["/api/users/*"]

13.3 Building a Zero-Trust Policy for anihpj

Step-by-step: deny everything → allow specific paths only. Here's the complete policy matrix:

🔐 ZERO-TRUST POLICY MATRIX — anihpj

SourceTargetAllowed Methods
istio-ingressgatewayjobpost-apiGET, POST
jobpost-apiuser-serviceGET only
jobpost-apiskills-serviceGET only
jobpost-apilocation-serviceGET only
user-serviceauth-serviceGET, POST
PrometheusALL servicesGET /metrics
ANY OTHERANY❌ DENIED
💡 ICA Exam Tip

If ANY AuthorizationPolicy targets a workload, all requests NOT explicitly ALLOWed are DENIED. The default "allow all" only applies when ZERO policies target that workload.

13.4 RequestAuthentication — JWT Verification at the Mesh Level

Validate JWT tokens at the Istio layer — no auth code needed in every Django service. Istio verifies the JWT signature and extracts claims for use in AuthorizationPolicy conditions.

yaml — RequestAuthentication + JWT-based AuthorizationPolicy 

# Verify JWT tokens from auth-service
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: RequestAuthentication
metadata:
  name: jwt-auth
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  jwtRules:
  - issuer: "auth-service.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"
    jwksUri: "http://auth-service/.well-known/jwks.json"
---
# Allow only requests with valid JWT containing role=admin
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: require-admin-jwt
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - when:
    - key: request.auth.claims[role]
      values: ["admin"]

13.5 Exclusion Matching — "NOT" Conditions in AuthorizationPolicy

From the official docs: AuthorizationPolicy supports exclusion matching using notPaths, notValues, notIpBlocks, and notPrincipals. This allows you to say "apply this policy to everything EXCEPT..." — a powerful pattern for health checks and public endpoints:

🩺
Pattern 1: Skip JWT for Health Checks
rules:
- to:
  - operation:
      notPaths: ["/healthz", "/ready"]
  from:
  - source:
      requestPrincipals: ["*"]

Requires JWT for ALL paths EXCEPT /healthz and /ready. Health probes bypass auth.

🚫
Pattern 2: Deny External to Admin
action: DENY
rules:
- to:
  - operation:
      paths: ["/admin"]
  from:
  - source:
      notNamespaces: ["anihpj"]

Denies /admin access from ANY namespace EXCEPT anihpj.

📋 EXCLUSION MATCHING FIELDS — What You Can Negate

Exclusion FieldUsageExample
notPathsAll paths EXCEPT thesenotPaths: ["/public"]
notValuesAll values EXCEPT these (when conditions)notValues: ["hack"]
notIpBlocksAll IPs EXCEPT thesenotIpBlocks: ["10.0.0.0/8"]
notNamespacesAll namespaces EXCEPT thesenotNamespaces: ["external"]
notPrincipalsAll identities EXCEPT thesenotPrincipals: ["cluster.local/..."]
notRequestPrincipalsAll JWT principals EXCEPT thesenotRequestPrincipals: ["*"]

13.6 TCP Authorization — Protecting Non-HTTP Workloads

From the official docs: AuthorizationPolicy works for any plain TCP protocol (MongoDB, Redis, MySQL, Kafka), not just HTTP. The difference: HTTP-only fields (hosts, methods, paths, requestPrincipals) are ignored for TCP traffic. Only ports, principals, namespaces, and ipBlocks apply.

yaml — TCP AuthZ: Only jobpost-api can access MongoDB on port 27017 
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: mongodb-policy
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: mongodb
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"]
    to:
    - operation:
        ports: ["27017"]          # ← ONLY port-level control for TCP
⚠️ Important: mTLS Dependency for Principal-Based Rules

From the official docs: if you use principals, namespaces, or source.principal in AuthorizationPolicy, mTLS MUST be enabled. These fields derive from SPIFFE certificates — without mTLS, they're empty or spoofable. Always pair principal-based AuthZ with PeerAuthentication STRICT.

🛡️ Authorization — Visual Recap

🚫

🪪

📝 Chapter 13 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How do you deny ALL traffic in a namespace using AuthorizationPolicy?
Show Answer
Answer

Create an AuthorizationPolicy with an empty spec: spec: {}. With no rules, nothing is allowed → everything is denied. This is the starting point for zero-trust architecture.

Explanation

An empty spec means "no rules match" which means "nothing is allowed." This is the Istio zero-trust pattern: deny everything first, then create explicit ALLOW policies for specific call paths. On the ICA exam, this is the first step when building a zero-trust security model for a namespace.

Q2
Does mTLS alone prevent service A from calling service B? Why or why not?
Show Answer
Answer

NO! mTLS proves the caller's identity (WHO they are) but does NOT restrict permissions (WHAT they can do). Without AuthorizationPolicy, any service with a valid SPIFFE certificate can call any other service. You need BOTH: mTLS for encryption+identity, AuthorizationPolicy for access control.

Explanation

This is a critical distinction tested on the ICA exam. mTLS = identity verification (like checking someone's ID). AuthorizationPolicy = access control (like checking if they're on the guest list). A valid ID doesn't mean you're allowed in — you need both. On the exam, always answer "NO — you need AuthorizationPolicy" when asked if mTLS alone restricts access.

Q3
How do you restrict a service to only GET requests on specific paths using AuthorizationPolicy?
Show Answer
Answer
rules:
- from:
  - source:
      principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"]
  to:
  - operation:
      methods: ["GET"]
      paths: ["/api/users/*"]
Explanation

AuthorizationPolicy operates at L7 (HTTP level). You can restrict by: source identity (principals = SPIFFE), HTTP method (GET/POST/DELETE), URI path (with wildcards), HTTP headers, and JWT claims. On the ICA exam, you'll write policies combining multiple conditions. Remember: combine from (who) with to (what operation) for defense-in-depth.

Q4
What happens if NO AuthorizationPolicy targets a workload?
Show Answer
Answer

ALL traffic is ALLOWED (default allow-all behavior). Istio only starts enforcing when at least one AuthorizationPolicy selects that workload. The moment you create ANY policy targeting a workload, the default switches from ALLOW-ALL to DENY-ALL — only explicitly ALLOWed requests pass.

Explanation

This is the most important AuthorizationPolicy concept. Before any policy exists: everything is allowed. After the first policy: only what's explicitly allowed passes. This means your first policy should be the deny-all (spec: {}), followed by specific ALLOW policies. On the ICA exam, this behavior is frequently tested.

Q5
How do you write a DENY policy, and when does it take priority over ALLOW?
Show Answer
Answer

Set action: DENY instead of action: ALLOW. DENY policies are evaluated BEFORE ALLOW policies. If any DENY matches, the request is immediately rejected with 403 — regardless of matching ALLOW policies. Use DENY for blacklisting specific callers.

Explanation

Evaluation order: CUSTOM → DENY → ALLOW. DENY always wins over ALLOW. This is useful for blocking known bad actors or deprecated services while maintaining a general ALLOW list. On the ICA exam, when a question involves both DENY and ALLOW policies matching the same request, the answer is: DENY takes priority.

Q6
How does RequestAuthentication differ from AuthorizationPolicy? Do you need both?
Show Answer
Answer

RequestAuthentication: Validates WHO the end-user is — verifies JWT signature, extracts claims (role, user ID). Does NOT deny requests — only validates tokens.

AuthorizationPolicy: Decides WHAT they can do — uses JWT claims from RequestAuthentication to make ALLOW/DENY decisions.

Yes, you need BOTH for JWT-based access control.

Explanation

Think of it as: RequestAuthentication = the bouncer checking your ID (is it real? who are you?). AuthorizationPolicy = the guest list (are you on it? which room can you enter?). On the ICA exam, for JWT scenarios, you always create both: a RequestAuthentication to validate tokens, and an AuthorizationPolicy to enforce permissions based on claims.

Q7
Can you combine JWT validation with SPIFFE identity in one AuthorizationPolicy for defense-in-depth?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes! Use from: source: principals: (SPIFFE for service identity) AND when: key: request.auth.claims[role] (JWT for end-user identity). Both conditions must be true. This gives you two layers: the calling service AND the end-user must both be authorized.

Explanation

This is defense-in-depth. Even if an attacker compromises a service and gets its SPIFFE cert, they still need a valid JWT with the right claims. On the ICA exam, combining service-level (SPIFFE) and user-level (JWT) auth in one policy is an advanced security pattern.

Q8
What happens when an ALLOW policy and a DENY policy both match the same request?
Show Answer
Answer

DENY wins! The evaluation order is CUSTOM → DENY → ALLOW. DENY policies are checked BEFORE ALLOW. If any DENY matches → immediate 403, regardless of matching ALLOW policies.

Explanation

This is crucial for security design. You can have a broad ALLOW policy but use DENY to carve out exceptions. Example: ALLOW all services in namespace anihpj to call user-service, but DENY from skills-service specifically. On the ICA exam, remember: DENY always beats ALLOW.

Q9
Write an AuthorizationPolicy that allows ONLY GET requests to /api/users/* from jobpost-api's service account.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-jobpost-get-users
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: user-service
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals:
        - "cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"
    to:
    - operation:
        methods: ["GET"]
        paths: ["/api/users/*"]
Explanation

This is a complete, production-ready AuthorizationPolicy. Key elements: selector targets the destination workload (user-service), principal matches the source's SPIFFE identity, operation restricts to GET /api/users/* only. POST, DELETE, or any other method/path is denied. On the ICA exam, you'll write similar policies from scratch.

Q10
What's the complete relationship between mTLS, RequestAuthentication, and AuthorizationPolicy?
Show Answer
Answer

mTLS (PeerAuthentication): Encrypts traffic + proves SERVICE identity (SPIFFE). "This is user-service calling."

RequestAuthentication: Validates END-USER identity (JWT). "The user is admin@anihpj.com with role=admin."

AuthorizationPolicy: Enforces permissions using BOTH identities. "Only user-service (SPIFFE) with admin users (JWT role=admin) can POST to /api/jobs."

Explanation

Three layers of Istio security: mTLS = encryption + service identity (layer 4), RequestAuthentication = end-user identity validation (layer 7), AuthorizationPolicy = access control using both (layer 7). On the ICA exam, you need to know which CRD handles which security concern and how they work together.

  • mTLS (Ch 12): Encrypts ALL service traffic. SPIFFE certs auto-issued, auto-rotated every 24h.
  • PeerAuthentication: PERMISSIVE (migration) → STRICT (enforce). Zero Django code changes.
  • AuthorizationPolicy (Ch 13): L7 firewall — allow/deny based on identity, method, path, headers.
  • RequestAuthentication: JWT validation at mesh level — no auth code in services.
  • Remember: mTLS = WHO (identity), AuthzPolicy = WHAT (permissions). You need BOTH.

Chapter 14: Canary Deployments — Safe Feature Releases

Deploying a new version is risky. Canary deployments let you send a small percentage of real users to v2, monitor it, and gradually ramp up — with instant rollback at any stage by changing one YAML value. No downtime, no DNS changes, no user impact.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🚀 Deployment 🎯 ICA 10%

14.1 The Full Canary Deployment Workflow

Phase-by-phase rollout for jobpost-api:v2 — from zero traffic to 100% production:

📈 CANARY LIFECYCLE — jobpost-api:v2 Gradual Rollout
Day 1
v1: 100% · v2: 0%
Deploy v2 only
Check v2 starts OK
Day 2
v1: 95% · v2: 5%
Error rate < 1%? ✅
Proceed
Day 3
v1: 75% · v2: 25%
p99 < 300ms? ✅
Proceed
Day 4
v1: 50% · v2: 50%
v2 errors 5%? ⚠️
❌ ROLLBACK!
Day 7
v1: 0% · v2: 100%
Fix bug, restart
✅ Production!
💡 Instant Rollback

At ANY phase, rollback takes ~2 seconds: change weight to 0% for v2 in VirtualService. v1 handles all traffic instantly. User impact: ZERO. Compare this to a traditional deployment rollback which can take 10+ minutes.

📊 Deployment Strategy Comparison

StrategyTraffic SwitchRollback SpeedBest For
Canary (Istio)Gradual %~2sRisk mitigation, monitoring
Blue-GreenInstant 100%~2sFast switch, staging parity
Rolling UpdatePod-by-podMinutesSimple apps, less control
Shadow/MirrorParallel copyN/AZero-risk production testing

14.2 Shadow / Mirror Traffic Testing

Mirror 100% of live traffic to v2 — but discard v2 responses. Users see v1, but v2 processes real production traffic silently. Best way to test under real load before going live.

yaml — Traffic Mirroring: v2 gets silent copy 

spec:
  http:
  - route:
    - destination:
        host: jobpost-api
        subset: v1              # Real users get v1
      weight: 100
    mirror:
      host: jobpost-api
      subset: v2                # v2 gets silent copy
    mirrorPercentage:
      value: 100.0              # Copy 100% to v2

# ✅ Users: 100% served by v1 (zero impact)
# ✅ v2: receives all real production requests
# ✅ Check v2 metrics BEFORE any user sees it

14.3 Blue-Green Deployments with Istio

Maintain two identical environments — blue (current) and green (new). Switch ALL traffic instantly with one YAML change. Rollback is equally instant.

🔵 BLUE (Current)

v1 running, 100% traffic. Green deployed but receives 0%. Both environments fully ready.

🟢 GREEN (New)

Switch: VS weight → v2:100%. Traffic cuts over instantly. If issues: switch back to blue — 2 seconds. No DNS propagation delay.

14.X Deployment Strategy Decision Matrix — Which to Use When

ScenarioBest StrategyWhy
High-risk code change, need monitoringCanarySmall blast radius (5%), gradual ramp, monitor each phase
Database migration, need instant parityBlue-GreenInstant switch, instant rollback, both environments identical
Testing v2 with zero user impactTraffic Mirrorv2 gets real traffic silently, responses discarded, zero risk
New feature for beta testers onlyHeader-based RoutingRoute based on x-user-type: beta → v2, others → v1
Major version (API v1→v2), long migrationCanary (extended)5%→25%→50% over weeks, monitor each phase for days
Infrastructure change (node pool, K8s version)Blue-GreenDeploy to new cluster, switch DNS/VirtualService instantly

📝 Chapter 14 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
How do you mirror 100% of production traffic to a v2 deployment for safe testing?
Show Answer
Answer

In VirtualService, add mirror: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2} with mirrorPercentage: {value: 100.0}. v1 handles all user responses; v2 gets a silent copy and its responses are discarded.

Explanation

Traffic mirroring is the safest way to test v2 under real production load. Users see v1 responses; v2 processes silently. Even if v2 crashes or returns errors, no user is affected. On the ICA exam, mirroring is the answer when the scenario is "test with production traffic without affecting users."

Q2
What's the difference between canary and blue-green deployments?
Show Answer
Answer

Canary: Gradual traffic ramp (5%→25%→50%→100%) over time. Safer for high-risk changes — small blast radius. Blue-Green: Instant 100% switch between two identical environments. Faster but riskier. Both use VirtualService weights and both have ~2s rollback.

Explanation

Canary = dip your toe in (test with 5%, increase gradually). Blue-Green = jump in the pool (instant 100% switch). On the ICA exam, choose canary for high-risk changes and blue-green for infrastructure/database migrations where you need instant environment parity.

Q3
How fast can you roll back a canary deployment if v2 starts failing, and what command does it?
Show Answer
Answer

~2 seconds. Edit the VirtualService: change v2's weight to 0. v1 handles all traffic instantly. kubectl edit virtualservice jobpost-api-vs -n anihpj — change weight to 0, save. Zero user impact. No DNS, no pod restart, no deployment rollback needed.

Explanation

This is the killer feature of Istio canary deployments. Traditional rollback: git revert → CI/CD pipeline → pod restart → health check → 5-10 minutes. Istio rollback: edit one YAML number → 2 seconds. On the ICA exam, this speed is the primary advantage of Istio-managed canary deployments.

Q4
What happens to mirrored traffic responses from v2?
Show Answer
Answer

They are completely discarded. The mirror sends a copy of the request to v2 but ignores its response. Users always see v1's response. v2 can crash, return errors, or be slow — it affects ZERO users. This is what makes mirroring completely safe.

Explanation

Mirroring is fire-and-forget. Envoy sends the mirrored request and immediately discards whatever comes back. This means: no double processing from the user's perspective, no response merging, just pure observation. On the ICA exam, remember: mirror = observe only, canary = actual traffic.

Q5
When would you choose blue-green deployment over canary?
Show Answer
Answer

When you need instant cut-over with full environment parity. Examples: database migration (both blue and green point to new DB), major infrastructure change, compliant environments where you must maintain two identical stacks. Blue-green = instant 100% switch with instant rollback.

Explanation

Blue-green trades gradual safety for instant speed. Choose it when: (1) you have full environment parity, (2) you've tested green thoroughly in staging, (3) you need the ability to switch back to blue instantly if anything goes wrong. On the ICA exam, blue-green is for "instant switch" scenarios.

Q6
How do you monitor v1 vs v2 metrics side-by-side during a canary deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Kiali: v1 and v2 appear as separate nodes with traffic distribution percentages. Click each for RED metrics. Grafana Workload Dashboard: Compare v1 vs v2 error rates, latency (p50/p90/p99), and throughput side-by-side. Watch for v2 error rate spikes.

Explanation

During canary, you need two dashboards open: Kiali for visual health (red node = rollback now) and Grafana for detailed metrics (compare p99 latency). On the ICA exam, Kiali is the recommended monitoring tool during canary deployments because it shows health at a glance.

Q7
What VirtualService field controls canary traffic split percentages? Write the YAML.
Show Answer
Answer

The weight field under each route destination.

http:
- route:
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api
      subset: v1
    weight: 90
  - destination:
      host: jobpost-api
      subset: v2
    weight: 10

Weights are relative — they don't need to sum to 100 (but should for clarity). Subsets must exist in a DestinationRule.

Explanation

The weight field is the core of canary deployments. Envoy computes the percentage: weight / sum(all weights) × 100. So weight: 90 + weight: 10 = 90% and 10% respectively. On the ICA exam, you'll write this YAML multiple times — it's the most frequently tested hands-on task.

Q8
How does traffic mirroring differ from a canary deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Mirroring: Silent copy to v2, v2 responses DISCARDED, users always see v1. Zero risk — v2 can't affect users. Used for testing. Canary: Real traffic to v2, users see v2 responses. Has risk — v2 bugs affect users. Used for gradual rollout.

Explanation

Think of mirroring as "shadow mode" and canary as "live mode." Mirror first (verify v2 handles real traffic), then canary (send real users). On the ICA exam, this is the recommended progression: mirror → canary (5%) → canary (25%) → canary (50%) → full production.

Q9
During canary, v2's error rate spikes to 8% and p99 latency jumps to 2 seconds. What do you do?
Show Answer
Answer

ROLLBACK IMMEDIATELY: Change VirtualService weight for v2 to 0. Takes ~2 seconds. Then investigate: (1) check v2 logs for errors, (2) compare v1 vs v2 Grafana metrics, (3) verify v2 deployment config/env vars, (4) fix the bug, redeploy v2, restart canary from 5%.

Explanation

This is the real-world canary workflow. Don't investigate while v2 is still getting traffic — rollback first, then debug. The key metric thresholds: error rate > 2% above v1 = rollback, p99 latency > 2× v1 = rollback. On the ICA exam, always answer "rollback immediately" when canary metrics show degradation.

Q10
How do blue-green and canary deployments differ in both rollout and rollback speed?
Show Answer
Answer

Both use VirtualService weight changes, so both roll out and roll back in ~2 seconds. The difference is risk: canary limits blast radius (5%→25%→50%→100%), blue-green impacts 100% at once. Canary rollback at any phase = instant. Blue-green rollback = also instant (switch weight back to blue).

Explanation

The speed advantage is the same for both — ~2 seconds via VirtualService weight edit. The strategic choice is about risk tolerance: canary = safety through gradualism, blue-green = speed through environment parity. On the ICA exam, both are valid; choose based on the scenario's risk requirements.

Chapter 15: Rate Limiting and Envoy Filters

Protect your services from overload with rate limiting. Istio supports both local (per-pod) and global (shared across all pods) rate limiting via EnvoyFilter — no code changes needed in Django.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🛡️ Protection 🎯 ICA 10%

15.1 Local Rate Limiting (Per-Pod)

Each pod has its own independent rate limit bucket. Pod 1 at limit → Pod 2 still serves normally.

🪣 LOCAL RATE LIMITING — Token Bucket per Pod (100 req/s each)
Pod 1

Token bucket: 100 tok/s
████████░░░ 82/100
✅ Serving normally

Pod 2

Token bucket: 100 tok/s
███████████ 100/100
❌ 429 — AT LIMIT!

Pod 3

Token bucket: 100 tok/s
███░░░░░░░ 30/100
✅ Serving normally

Total capacity: 3 pods × 100 = 300 req/s. Each pod's limit is independent — Pod 2 hitting its limit doesn't affect Pods 1 and 3.

15.2 Global Rate Limiting (Shared Counter)

Rate limit by source IP, user, or API key across ALL pods combined. Requires a Redis-backed rate limit service.

🌐 GLOBAL RATE LIMITING — Architecture
1
Request

IP 203.0.113.42 → jobpost-api

2
Envoy Checks

"Check rate limit for this IP" → gRPC to rate limit service

3
Redis Lookup

IP: 203.0.113.42 count: 987/1000
Under limit ✅

4
Allowed

Request forwarded to Django ✅

1001
Denied

Request #1001: count: 1001/1000
→ 429 Too Many Requests ❌

15.3 Traffic Shaping — Controlling Latency

Apply different rate limits based on request type — protect real users from batch jobs:

👤 Regular User

Header: x-client: browser
No rate limit, full speed
0ms overhead

🤖 Batch Job

Header: x-client: batch-job
EnvoyFilter: 100ms delay
Max 10 req/s

🛡️ Rate Limiting — Recap

🪣
Local
Per-pod bucket
🌐
Global
Shared Redis counter
🚦
Shaping
Priority lanes

📝 Chapter 15 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
What Istio resource implements local rate limiting per pod, and what fields configure the token bucket?
Show Answer
Answer

EnvoyFilter with applyTo: HTTP_FILTER targeting SIDECAR_INBOUND. Configures a token bucket with: maxTokens (burst size), tokensPerFill (refill), fillInterval (period). Each pod has an independent bucket.

Explanation

Local rate limiting is per-Envoy — each pod's sidecar has its own counter. 3 pods × 100 req/s = 300 req/s total. Pod 1 at limit doesn't affect Pods 2 and 3. EnvoyFilter directly patches Envoy configuration — it's the most powerful (and potentially dangerous) Istio CRD.

Q2
What's the difference between local and global rate limiting, and what backend does global require?
Show Answer
Answer

Local: Per-pod counter — 3 pods × 100 = 300 req/s independent. No external deps. Global: ALL pods share ONE counter via Redis-backed rate limit service. Total limit applies across entire deployment regardless of pod count.

Explanation

Local = simple, scales with pod count. Global = accurate, requires Redis + rate limit service. On the ICA exam, choose local for per-pod protection; global when limit must apply across all pods (per-user limits).

Q3
What HTTP status code does Envoy return when rate limit is exceeded?
Show Answer
Answer

429 Too Many Requests. For global rate limiting, Retry-After: 60 header is also added. The request never reaches Django — Envoy blocks it at the proxy level.

Explanation

429 is the standard HTTP status for rate limiting. Retry-After tells clients when to retry. On the ICA exam, if a question asks what happens at rate limit: Envoy returns 429, Django never sees the request.

Q4
How can you distinguish between regular users and batch jobs for different rate limits?
Show Answer
Answer

Use HTTP headers (e.g., x-client: browser vs x-client: batch-job). Apply different EnvoyFilter rules per header — regular users get full speed, batch jobs get throttled. This is traffic shaping.

Explanation

Traffic shaping is like express lanes vs regular lanes. Match on request headers to apply different rate limits per traffic class. On the ICA exam, this is the answer for \"protect user-facing services from batch processing overload.\"

Q5
What backend does global rate limiting require and how does Envoy communicate with it?
Show Answer
Answer

A Redis-backed rate limit service — a separate deployment (not built into Istio). Architecture: Envoy → gRPC → Rate Limit Service → Redis (shared counter). All proxies query the same Redis, making the counter truly global.

Explanation

Global rate limiting requires running an external rate limit service. Envoy sends gRPC checks for every request (adds latency). On the ICA exam, global rate limiting = Redis requirement. The rate limit service is NOT part of Istio.

Q6
What is the difference between token bucket and leaky bucket rate limiting algorithms?
Show Answer
Answer

Token bucket (Envoy): Tokens refill at fixed rate, burst allowed until bucket empties. Leaky bucket: Requests queue and process at constant rate, excess overflows. Envoy uses token bucket: maxTokens = burst, tokensPerFill = refill rate.

Explanation

Token bucket is burst-friendly (good for web traffic spikes). Leaky bucket smooths traffic (good for consistent-rate processing). On the ICA exam, know that Envoy's local rate limiter uses token bucket algorithm.

Q7
How do you configure rate limiting for batch jobs while regular users get full speed?
Show Answer
Answer

Use EnvoyFilter with header match: x-client: batch-job → apply rate limit (10 req/s + 100ms delay). Requests without this header pass through at full speed. Different limits for different traffic classes.

Explanation

This is traffic shaping by header value. On the ICA exam, when asked to \"prioritize user traffic over batch,\" use EnvoyFilter with header-based routing to apply different rate limits.

Q8
What Istio resource type configures EnvoyFilter and what are its key fields?
Show Answer
Answer

apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3, kind: EnvoyFilter. Key fields: workloadSelector (target pods), configPatches (what to modify), applyTo (HTTP_FILTER, NETWORK_FILTER, LISTENER, CLUSTER, ROUTE). Directly modifies Envoy proxy config.

Explanation

EnvoyFilter is the most powerful Istio CRD — it patches Envoy's raw configuration. Use for: local rate limiting, custom Lua filters, header manipulation, WASM plugins. On the ICA exam, it's the answer for any rate limiting or custom Envoy behavior.

Q9
What happens when a local rate limit is exceeded on one pod but not others?
Show Answer
Answer

Envoy returns 429 for that pod only — Django never sees the request. Other pods still serve normally since each has its own bucket. This means total capacity = pods × limit, but distribution isn't guaranteed — hot pods may hit limits while others idle.

Explanation

Local rate limiting's independence is both a strength (no single point of failure) and weakness (uneven distribution). On the ICA exam, this is the key distinction: local = per-pod (no shared state), global = shared (Redis).

Q10
How does global rate limiting provide a Retry-After header to clients?
Show Answer
Answer

The rate limit service returns a gRPC response with retry_delay field. Envoy translates this into HTTP Retry-After: 60 header in the 429 response. Clients can read this to schedule retries. This is a global rate limiting feature — local rate limiting doesn't provide Retry-After.

Explanation

The Retry-After header makes rate limiting user-friendly — it tells clients exactly when to retry instead of forcing them to guess. On the ICA exam, this is a differentiator: global rate limiting provides Retry-After; local does not.

Chapter 16: Multi-Cluster and Advanced Scenarios

Span Istio across multiple Kubernetes clusters, add legacy VMs to the mesh, and explore the new ambient mesh — sidecar-free Istio with 6× less memory overhead.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🌍 Global 🎯 ICA 10%

16.1 Multi-Cluster Istio — Spanning Kubernetes Clusters

Single mesh across EU + US clusters with locality-based routing — EU users go to EU cluster, auto-failover to US if EU is down. mTLS works seamlessly across clusters.

🌍 MULTI-CLUSTER — anihpj EU + US
🌍 EU Users                                    🌎 US Users
     │                                               │
[EU LoadBalancer]                            [US LoadBalancer]
     │                                               │
┌────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┐
│                        SINGLE ISTIO MESH                              │
│  CLUSTER 1 (EU — Frankfurt)    │    CLUSTER 2 (US — Virginia)         │
│  istiod (EU primary)           │    istiod (US replica)               │
│  jobpost-api:v1 · user-service │    jobpost-api:v1 · user-service     │
│       │    mTLS cross-cluster ─┼─────────┘                            │
│                                                                       │
│  VirtualService: locality-based failover                              │
│  • EU users → EU cluster (low latency)                               │
│  • EU cluster down → auto failover to US cluster                     │
Primary-Remote

One istiod manages both clusters. Simpler but single point of failure.

Multi-Primary

Each cluster has own istiod. Recommended for production.

External CP

istiod outside any cluster. Advanced setup for managed services.

16.2 VM Workloads in the Mesh

Add legacy VMs (databases, monoliths) to the Istio mesh. The VM gets a SPIFFE certificate and is treated like a Pod — full mTLS, metrics, and authorization policies.

yaml — WorkloadEntry: Register a VM 

# Register legacy PostgreSQL VM in the mesh
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3
kind: WorkloadEntry
metadata:
  name: postgres-vm
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  address: 10.0.5.20           # VM IP address
  labels:
    app: postgres              # Labels for policy matching
  serviceAccount: postgres-sa  # For SPIFFE identity

# Result: skills-service → PostgreSQL VM over mTLS!
# VM gets cert from istiod, metrics in Grafana,
# AuthorizationPolicy controls who can query it.

16.3 Istio Ambient Mesh — Sidecar-Free Mode

New architecture: no sidecar injection required! ztunnel (per-node) handles L4 mTLS, waypoint proxy handles L7 features per namespace. 6× less memory overhead.

🏗️ SIDECAR vs AMBIENT

Sidecar ModeAmbient Mode
ArchitectureEnvoy proxy per podztunnel per NODE (shared)
Memory per pod~128Mi~0Mi (shared)
7 anihpj services7 × 128Mi = ~900Mi1 ztunnel = ~50Mi
L7 featuresBuilt-in per sidecarOptional waypoint proxy
Pod startup+50ms (sidecar)No overhead
Pod READY2/2 (app + sidecar)1/1 (app only!)
bash — Enable ambient mode for anihpj 

# Install Istio with ambient profile
istioctl install --set profile=ambient -y

# Enable ambient for namespace (no istio-injection label!)
kubectl label namespace anihpj istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambient

# Result: pods in anihpj get L4 mTLS via ztunnel automatically
# No 2/2 — pods stay at 1/1!

16.4 Ambient Mesh Architecture — How ztunnel and Waypoint Work Together

From the official docs: Ambient mesh splits Istio's functionality into two layers — a zero-trust secure overlay (ztunnel for L4) and an optional L7 processing layer (waypoint proxy). This layered approach is what gives ambient its 6× memory savings.

🏗️ AMBIENT MESH ARCHITECTURE — Two-Layer Design
🔒
LAYER 1: ztunnel (L4 — Always On)

One ztunnel pod per Kubernetes Node. Handles: mTLS encryption, simple L4 authorization, TCP metrics, and traffic routing at TCP level. Written in Rust for performance. No per-pod overhead. Every pod on the node shares one ztunnel.

🧠
LAYER 2: Waypoint Proxy (L7 — Optional)

One Envoy-based waypoint deployment per namespace (or per service account). Handles: VirtualService routing, retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, L7 AuthorizationPolicy, telemetry. Only deploy if you need L7 features.

🔄 AMBIENT TRAFFIC FLOW — How a Request Travels
  L4 ONLY (no waypoint):                     L7 (with waypoint):
                                        
  Pod A ──▶ ztunnel(Node1) ──▶ Pod B        Pod A ──▶ ztunnel ──▶ waypoint ──▶ ztunnel ──▶ Pod B
  (app)    (mTLS, L4 authz)  (app)          (app)    (mTLS)    (VS routing,  (mTLS)    (app)
                                                    │          retries, CB)           │
                                                    │                                  │
  ✅ mTLS encryption                          ✅ mTLS + L4 authz                      │
  ✅ L4 authz (basic allow/deny)             ✅ VS weight-based routing               │
  ✅ TCP metrics                             ✅ Retries, timeouts, CB                 │
  ❌ HTTP routing (URI/headers)              ✅ L7 AuthorizationPolicy                │
  ❌ Retries, timeouts, circuit breaker      ✅ Full telemetry (RED metrics)          │

🚀 AMBIENT MIGRATION — Sidecar → Ambient Coexistence

StepActionResult
1Install Istio with ambient profileztunnel DaemonSet runs on all nodes
2Label test namespace: istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambientPods get L4 mTLS via ztunnel, no sidecars
3Verify: kubectl get pods shows 1/1No istio-proxy container needed!
4Deploy waypoint if L7 needed: istioctl waypoint apply -n anihpjPer-namespace Envoy for L7 features
5Gradually migrate namespaces from sidecar to ambientBoth modes coexist seamlessly
6Remove istio-injection=enabled from migrated namespacesClean cutover from sidecar to ambient

📝 Chapter 16 — ICA Practice Questions

Q1
What Istio resource registers a legacy VM in the service mesh, and what does it enable?
Show Answer
Answer

WorkloadEntry — specifies the VM's IP address, labels, and service account. Combined with WorkloadGroup, istiod issues it a SPIFFE certificate. Full mesh policies (mTLS, AuthorizationPolicy, metrics) apply to the VM like any pod.

Explanation

WorkloadEntry brings non-Kubernetes workloads into the mesh. The VM gets a SPIFFE identity, participates in mTLS, and appears in Kiali/Grafana. On the ICA exam, WorkloadEntry is the answer for "add a VM or bare-metal server to the mesh."

Q2
What's the main advantage of ambient mesh over sidecar mode, and why?
Show Answer
Answer

~6× less memory overhead. Sidecar mode: one Envoy per pod (~128Mi each). Ambient mode: one ztunnel per Node (~50Mi total, shared). Pods show 1/1 instead of 2/2. L7 features are optional via per-namespace waypoint proxy.

Explanation

Ambient mesh is Istio's newer, lighter architecture. ztunnel handles L4 (mTLS, TCP metrics) at the node level. Waypoint proxy (per-namespace Envoy deployment) handles L7 (routing, retries, circuit breaking) only when needed. On the ICA exam, ambient is about resource efficiency and operational simplicity.

Q3
What are the three multi-cluster deployment models, and which is recommended for production?
Show Answer
Answer

Primary-Remote: One istiod manages both clusters (simpler, single point of failure). Multi-Primary: Each cluster has own istiod (recommended for production — no SPOF). External Control Plane: istiod outside any cluster (advanced, managed services).

Explanation

Multi-Primary is the production recommendation because each cluster is independent — if one cluster's istiod fails, the other cluster continues operating normally. On the ICA exam, Multi-Primary = production, Primary-Remote = simpler/dev.

Q4
Does mTLS work between pods in different clusters? How?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes, seamlessly! All clusters share a common root CA. Services in different clusters receive SPIFFE certificates from the same trust domain. They authenticate each other via mTLS just like within one cluster — no additional config needed.

Explanation

Cross-cluster mTLS works because: (1) all clusters use the same root CA, (2) SPIFFE identities are globally unique (include cluster info), (3) east-west gateways handle cross-cluster traffic routing. On the ICA exam, mTLS is mesh-wide and cluster-transparent.

Q5
How do you enable ambient mode for a namespace instead of traditional sidecar injection?
Show Answer
Answer

Label: kubectl label namespace anihpj istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambient (NOT istio-injection=enabled). Install Istio: istioctl install --set profile=ambient -y. Pods get L4 mTLS via ztunnel automatically — no sidecar containers.

Explanation

Key differences from sidecar mode: different label key, different install profile, pods stay at 1/1, no istio-init container, no iptables interception. On the ICA exam, know both injection methods and when to use each.

Q6
How does cross-cluster service discovery work in multi-primary Istio?
Show Answer
Answer

Each cluster's istiod discovers local services via Kubernetes API. Clusters exchange endpoint information through a shared root CA and east-west gateway. A Service in Cluster A resolves Cluster B's Service via DNS — Envoy routes through the east-west gateway with full mTLS.

Explanation

The east-west gateway is a dedicated Istio Gateway for cross-cluster traffic. It's separate from the north-south (ingress) gateway. On the ICA exam, east-west gateway = cross-cluster communication; ingress gateway = external-to-mesh communication.

Q7
What is locality-based routing in multi-cluster Istio and how do you configure it?
Show Answer
Answer

Routes traffic to the nearest cluster based on geographic labels. Configure in DestinationRule: trafficPolicy.loadBalancer.localityLbSetting.enabled: true. EU users → EU cluster (low latency). If EU fails → auto-failover to US cluster. Uses topology.istio.io/subzone node labels.

Explanation

Locality-based routing optimizes latency by keeping traffic within the same geographic region. It's a DestinationRule feature (load balancing policy). On the ICA exam, this is the answer for "route users to the nearest data center."

Q8
Can a VM running outside Kubernetes get a SPIFFE certificate from istiod? How?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes! Register via WorkloadEntry + WorkloadGroup. Install istio-proxy on the VM. The VM's Envoy sends CSR to istiod:15012. istiod validates the VM via its WorkloadEntry registration, signs an SVID certificate. VM now participates in mTLS like a K8s pod.

Explanation

This is how you bridge legacy infrastructure into the mesh. The VM needs: (1) istio-proxy installed, (2) network access to istiod:15012, (3) WorkloadEntry registration. On the ICA exam, this is the pattern for "include a database VM in the mesh."

Q9
What L7 features does ambient mesh NOT provide without a waypoint proxy?
Show Answer
Answer

Without waypoint proxy, ambient provides only L4: mTLS encryption, simple authz, TCP metrics. Missing L7: VirtualService routing (URI/header-based), retries, timeouts, fault injection, circuit breaking, L7 AuthorizationPolicy. Waypoint = per-namespace Envoy deployment for L7.

Explanation

Ambient is layered: ztunnel (L4, always on) + optional waypoint (L7, per-namespace). This gives you granular control — add L7 only to namespaces that need it. On the ICA exam, ambient L4 = ztunnel (always), ambient L7 = waypoint (optional).

Q10
What happens to existing sidecar-based workloads when you migrate to ambient mesh?
Show Answer
Answer

Sidecar and ambient coexist in the same cluster. Label some namespaces istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambient, keep others with istio-injection=enabled. Traffic between ambient and sidecar uses mTLS seamlessly — istiod manages both data plane modes. Migrate gradually.

Explanation

Coexistence is a key ambient design feature — you don't need a big-bang migration. Run sidecar-injected and ambient-injected workloads side by side, migrate namespace by namespace. On the ICA exam, this is the recommended migration strategy.

📝 Part 8 Summary — Advanced Patterns (10% of ICA)

  • Canary (Ch 14): Gradual % rollout with ~2s rollback. Mirror traffic to test v2 safely.
  • Rate Limiting (Ch 15): Local (per-pod bucket) and Global (Redis-backed). Protect services from overload.
  • Multi-Cluster/Ambient (Ch 16): Span clusters, add VMs via WorkloadEntry, sidecar-free ambient mode.
